# Part I · Chapter 5: HBM Economics — Manufacturing Intensity, Residual Capacity, and Memory Scarcity

## 1. The memory question is manufacturing intensity, not bit demand alone

HBM should not be analyzed as a GPU BOM line. In frontier systems it affects model capacity, batching, context length, concurrency, communication, and accelerator utilization; at rack scale, its manufacturing intensity becomes a system-level capacity constraint.

The industrial question is therefore not just "how many HBM bits are demanded?" It is:

> **How much wafer input, cleanroom space, packaging capacity, qualification effort, and capital does each unit of high-value memory absorb, and what residual supply remains for conventional DRAM?**

That is why HBM can reprice the broader memory system even when aggregate DRAM bit capacity continues to grow.

## 2. Micron's ~3× disclosure is the first-party manufacturing anchor

Micron's FY24 Q2 prepared remarks provide a clear boundary: at the **same technology node and for the same number of bits**, HBM3E requires approximately **3× the wafer supply of DDR5**.[^2] Micron's FY25 10-K further confirms that producing the same number of HBM bits requires more wafers and cleanroom space.[^3]

This evidence supports the following conclusions:

- HBM3E consumes more manufacturing input per bit than DDR5;
- HBM expansion places greater pressure on DRAM wafer / cleanroom input;
- effective residual capacity for conventional DRAM can face stronger pressure.

It does **not** mean that HBM is priced at 3× DDR5, nor can it be treated as an immutable "3:1 law" for every fab and every HBM generation. Micron's public statement on HBM4 is only that the trade ratio is expected to rise further; a specific HBM4/HBM4E ratio still requires future vendor disclosure.

## 3. The 51% forecast uses a different denominator

TrendForce **forecasts** that **HBM + RDIMM together will account for roughly 51% of 2026 DRAM bit supply**.[^4] This B-grade institutional forecast must retain three boundaries:

1. the 51% includes **HBM and RDIMM** and cannot be rewritten as "51% of DRAM is HBM";
2. it is a **bit-supply** share, not a wafer share;
3. it is a **2026E forecast**, not completed full-year actuals.

Combined with Micron's same-bit wafer trade ratio, this creates a more important mechanism: **bit share does not fully capture manufacturing-input pressure.** When a high-value bit requires more wafer and cleanroom input, its impact on manufacturing resources can exceed its share of bits.

## 4. Residual capacity is the downstream denominator

Memory reallocation can be expressed as a clear transmission chain:

**AI/server memory demand rises → HBM/RDIMM mix rises → wafer / cleanroom / packaging input shifts toward high-value products → conventional DDR/LPDDR residual capacity tightens → non-AI end markets face greater price and configuration pressure.**

The key variable is not total global DRAM capacity. It is the effective supply actually left for consumer electronics and other traditional workloads after AI/server demand has absorbed the priority resources.

This is why the report uses **Non-AI Residual Capacity** as the central memory metric rather than treating gross DRAM-capacity growth as proof that traditional markets have returned to abundance.

## 5. HBM has changed memory-vendor economics

SK hynix reported FY25 revenue of **KRW97.1467T**, operating profit of **KRW47.2063T**, operating margin of **49%**, and net income of **KRW42.9479T**, while stating that HBM revenue more than doubled year over year.[^5]

These figures do not prove that HBM alone generated all profit, but they are sufficient to show that high-value memory product mix has materially improved memory-vendor economics. Conventional server memory remains important as well, reinforcing the point that HBM and DDR5/RDIMM are not fully isolated capital and manufacturing systems.

## 6. Supply is concentrated; the public denominator is incomplete

High-end HBM supply is concentrated primarily among SK hynix, Samsung, and Micron. The barriers are not just DRAM-fab CapEx. They also include:

- TSV / stacking;
- yield learning;
- advanced-packaging integration;
- customer qualification;
- generation timing;
- high-volume manufacturing stability.

Public first-party material is insufficient to construct a fully comparable and auditable three-vendor HBM market-share table. This report therefore does not upgrade media or sell-side estimates into company-official share data.

### 6.1 Price feedback limits one-way scarcity narratives

Resource reallocation contains a price-feedback mechanism. TrendForce's June 2, 2026 model estimate found that after conventional DRAM prices rose sharply from the second half of 2025, **DDR5 64GB RDIMM per-wafer revenue exceeded HBM in 1Q26**; the same research estimated HBM profitability below that of DDR5 64GB RDIMM.[^6]

This is B-grade institutional estimation, not product-level realized gross-margin disclosure from Samsung, SK hynix, or Micron. Its analytical value is the self-correcting mechanism it reveals:

> HBM/AI demand rises → conventional-DRAM residual supply tightens → DDR5/RDIMM prices rise → conventional-DRAM per-wafer economics improve → the relative incentive for further HBM conversion declines → memory vendors rebalance product mix.

AI Resource Gravity is therefore not an infinitely one-way pump. Long-run allocation depends on risk-adjusted return per scarce manufacturing input, qualification, yield, generation transitions, and customer-contract structure.

## 7. Rack specs show intensity, not industry demand

GB200 NVL72's **13.4 TB of HBM3E per rack** is a useful system-level material identity.[^1]

It shows that once procurement moves from an accelerator card to a rack-scale system, memory intensity must also be evaluated at the system level.

But without a public rack shipment or deployment count, the specification cannot be scaled into an industry PB-demand estimate.

Therefore:

> **Per-system HBM specifications can describe memory intensity; industry demand still requires observed shipments, customer demand, or memory-vendor supply data.**

## 8. HBM4 raises cross-layer capital density without a fixed public multiplier

The complexity of next-generation HBM comes not only from higher stack counts. It can also involve logic base dies, advanced foundry processes, packaging, testing, and qualification.

If logic base dies use more advanced processes, the memory bottleneck extends further into foundry and packaging:

**HBM vendor → logic base die → advanced foundry → advanced packaging → accelerator qualification**

HBM can therefore evolve from a "memory bottleneck" into a system bottleneck spanning memory, foundry, and packaging. Before vendors formally disclose the data, however, HBM3E's ~3× ratio cannot be mechanically rewritten as 4× or 5× for HBM4, and one 12-Hi or 16-Hi configuration cannot be treated as the universal HBM4 production standard.

## 9. HBM does not clear like a standard commodity-memory cycle

One important difference between HBM and standard commodity memory is generation-specific qualification. Product generations move quickly, they are closely tied to accelerator generations, qualification cycles are long, and obsolescence risk is higher.

The industry can therefore experience:

> **looser supply in an older HBM generation while the next frontier generation remains constrained.**

Oversupply in one generation does not automatically solve qualification, yield, and packaging constraints in the next.

## 10. Inference can shift the bottleneck without eliminating memory economics

Some inference workloads can trade bandwidth for lower-cost DDR/LPDDR, but frontier inference still faces KV-cache, long-context, large-batch, MoE-routing, low-latency serving, and multi-accelerator communication constraints.

More importantly, RDIMM is already included in TrendForce's forward AI/server memory structure. Shifting some workloads from HBM toward DDR5 therefore changes the cost/bandwidth tradeoff inside a broader shared memory-resource pool rather than creating a completely independent supply river.

## 11. Current read and falsification

HBM scarcity is not inevitable. The current mechanism would weaken materially if:

- memory architecture sharply reduces dependence on high-bandwidth stacked memory;
- algorithmic efficiency persistently lowers memory bandwidth / capacity demand;
- the HBM wafer/cleanroom trade ratio improves materially;
- stacking, yield, logic-base, and advanced-packaging supply elasticity rises sharply;
- conventional DRAM and HBM manufacturing resources become much more decoupled.

The long-run claim is therefore not "HBM will always be scarce." It is:

> **As long as frontier-system bandwidth demand grows faster than HBM supply elasticity and manufacturing input per bit remains materially above conventional DRAM, HBM will remain a core scarce layer of AI infrastructure.**

## References

[^1]: NVIDIA, “GB200 NVL72,” official product page, https://www.nvidia.com/en-us/data-center/gb200-nvl72/
[^2]: Micron Technology, FY24 Q2 Prepared Remarks, 2024-03-20, HBM3E ≈3× DDR5 wafer supply at same node / same bits, https://s25.q4cdn.com/621799436/files/doc_financials/2024/q2/FY24-Q2-Prepared-Remarks.pdf
[^3]: Micron Technology, FY25 Form 10-K, HBM wafer and cleanroom requirements, U.S. SEC, https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/723125/000072312525000028/mu-20250828.htm
[^4]: TrendForce, 2026-08-25, 2026E HBM + RDIMM share of DRAM bit supply, https://www.trendforce.com/presscenter/news/20260825-13198.html
[^5]: SK hynix, FY25 Financial Results, https://news.skhynix.com/en/sk-hynix-announces-fy25-financial-results/
[^6]: TrendForce, 2026-06-02, per-wafer revenue comparison for HBM and DDR5 64GB RDIMM, https://www.trendforce.com/presscenter/news/20260602-13074.html

# Part I · Chapter 6: Advanced Packaging — The Quantity Gate Between Wafers and Deliverable Systems

## 1. Packaging is the quantity gate between wafers and systems

A frontier accelerator is not commercially deliverable when the logic die leaves the fab. Multi-die integration, HBM, interposers, substrates, testing, yield, and qualification still have to close within the same delivery window.

Advanced packaging therefore acts as a **quantity gate**:

> **front-end logic available ≠ complete AI system deliverable**

The relevant industrial variable is not only how many leading-edge wafers can be fabricated, but how many qualified systems can pass through the packaging/integration layer in the same delivery window.

## 2. Tightness is first-party observable; revenue labels still need denominator discipline

TSMC's public statements are sufficient to establish advanced-packaging tightness:[^1][^2]

- 4Q24: management described relevant capacity as **very tight** and unable to satisfy all customer demand;
- 2Q26: management stated more directly that packaging capacity was **so tight that now it limits my customers' growth**.

Revenue disclosures must retain their original year-specific labels:

- 4Q24 transcript: **advanced packaging and testing >8% of revenue**;
- 4Q25 transcript, using the company's corrected wording: **advanced packaging slightly over 10% of 2025 revenue**.[^5]

These labels are not perfectly identical. This report therefore does **not** treat >8% → >10% as a strict same-denominator time series, nor does it rewrite the 2025 "advanced packaging" figure back into "advanced packaging + testing."

## 3. Tightness is observable; customer allocation is not

TSMC public material supports the following: advanced packaging is tight, CoWoS is an important part of that system, and packaging tightness is limiting customer growth.

TSMC does not disclose an auditable customer-level CoWoS allocation table for this report, nor does it provide an official CoWoS share by customer.

Two questions therefore have to remain separate:

- **A-grade fact:** packaging / CoWoS tightness;
- **not disclosed:** exactly how much CoWoS capacity each customer receives.

Annual customer revenue share, wafer revenue share, and CoWoS customer share are not interchangeable.

## 4. Demand concentration is observable only through institutional estimates

TrendForce's research on 2025 CoWoS demand identifies NVIDIA as the largest demand customer and estimates its demand share at **more than 60%**.[^3]

The evidence grade must remain **B — institutional estimate**. The figure is useful for analyzing demand concentration and designing stress tests, but it cannot be rewritten as:

- TSMC officially confirming that NVIDIA received >60% of allocation;
- >60% being a 2026 actual;
- NVIDIA possessing exclusive CoWoS rights.

The correct use is: **highly concentrated demand has a real institutional anchor, while actual allocation still awaits first-party disclosure.**

## 5. Packaging is a separate physical denominator

The formal report does not use a report-side "CoWoS wafers per rack" geometry factor to infer industry rack capacity.

Advanced-packaging capacity depends on packaging flow, interposer/substrate constraints, HBM integration, tool capacity, testing, yield, and qualification. It is not interchangeable with TSMC's front-end 12-inch-equivalent wafer shipments.

Therefore:

- front-end logic wafers cannot be divided by a packaging-geometry factor to infer rack output;
- packaging demand share cannot be converted into customer allocation;
- rack specifications cannot be converted into actual shipments without observed deployment data.

## 6. Packaging can bind before front-end logic

Leading-edge logic supply can grow through new fabs, process migration, and capacity expansion. Advanced packaging simultaneously depends on tools, interposers, substrates, testing, process flows, HBM integration, and qualification.

The result is:

> **logic wafer available ≠ system deliverable**

Advanced nodes are closer to an entry ticket into a frontier system; advanced packaging can become the quantity gate that determines how many complete systems are delivered. Both matter, but their physical units and expansion clocks are different.

## 7. Incremental access must be measured at the packaging layer

The forward question is not a synthetic cross-layer capture ratio.

It is whether competing buyers can obtain qualified advanced-packaging supply with acceptable:

- lead time;
- allocation fulfillment;
- delivery stability;
- supplier diversification;
- generation transition;
- integration yield.

That evidence should be evaluated directly at the packaging layer.

## 8. Second stacks do not automatically escape packaging / HBM

Google, AWS, Microsoft, and Meta custom accelerator systems can divert part of NVIDIA's platform profit, but their next-generation systems still rely heavily on advanced logic, HBM, and high-density integration.

Two effects can therefore coexist:

1. **platform substitution:** in-house cloud systems reduce dependence on the full NVIDIA platform;
2. **upstream competition:** those same systems increase demand for the same advanced manufacturing, HBM, and packaging resources.

More custom silicon does not automatically mean lower CoWoS/HBM pressure.

## 9. Bottleneck location and profit location are different questions

Advanced packaging can be the system-delivery bottleneck without having the highest margin in the value chain. TSMC management stated in 2Q24 that advanced-packaging gross margin had historically been significantly below corporate average and was approaching, but had not yet reached, the company average.[^4]

Physical bottlenecks and profit pools therefore have to remain separate:

- packaging may determine **whether the system can be delivered**;
- the system platform can still capture stronger economics through software, interconnect, integration, and customer switching costs.

> **bottleneck location ≠ profit location**

This distinction is essential for AI-infrastructure value-capture analysis.

## 10. Packaging is an independent China-closure gate

A frontier AI parallel stack cannot be judged from logic nodes alone. Even if accelerator dies can be produced, insufficient advanced packaging, substrates, HBM integration, testing, or high-volume yield can still prevent stable delivery of large systems.

"Domestic chip production" therefore cannot hide packaging as an independent physical layer. China system closure must validate logic, HBM, packaging, interconnect, software, and power separately.

## 11. Current read and falsification

As of August 2026, the most defensible conclusions are:

- TSMC has confirmed advanced-packaging tightness with first-party evidence;
- TrendForce provides a B-grade institutional anchor for highly concentrated NVIDIA CoWoS demand;
- customer-level allocation remains undisclosed by TSMC;
- 5.37 wafers/rack is only a D-grade geometry stress test;
- custom silicon can still compete for the same upstream scarce resources.

The judgment that advanced packaging is a core scarce layer should be weakened if TSMC stops describing packaging as a growth constraint, lead times fall materially, customer-level allocation becomes more distributed, and HBM/packaging supply elasticity continues to improve.

## References

[^1]: TSMC, 4Q24 Earnings Conference Transcript, advanced packaging and testing >8% of revenue; capacity “very tight,” https://investor.tsmc.com/english/encrypt/files/encrypt_file/reports/2025-01/84aeb15bbe33894365d33f52e027c5268ba95dcf/TSMC%204Q24%20Transcript.pdf
[^2]: TSMC, 2Q26 Earnings Conference Transcript, packaging capacity limiting customer growth, https://investor.tsmc.com/english/quarterly-results/2026/q2
[^3]: TrendForce, 2025 CoWoS demand structure, NVIDIA estimated >60%, B-grade institutional estimate, https://www.trendforce.com/research/download/RP250314ZJ
[^4]: TSMC, 2Q24 Earnings Conference Transcript, advanced-packaging margin relative to corporate average, https://investor.tsmc.com/english/encrypt/files/encrypt_file/reports/2024-08/5122725a56670882d777a8e8bfe0ed247cc55330/TSMC%202Q24%20Transcript.pdf
[^5]: TSMC, 4Q25 Earnings Conference Transcript, corrected 2025 advanced-packaging revenue identity, https://investor.tsmc.com/english/encrypt/files/encrypt_file/reports/2026-01/51d09df96cd89ac19d65af39032b038dc2896a24/TSMC%204Q25%20Transcript.pdf

# Part I · Chapter 7: Time-to-Power — From Rack Density to Deployable AI Capacity

## 1. The power question is deployable capacity, not electricity price

At roughly **120 kW/rack**, power is no longer just an operating-cost input. It becomes a deployment gate.[^1]

A D-grade stress conversion makes the scale visible:

> 500 racks × 120 kW/rack = **60 MW of rack-level full-load power**

This does not describe any specific campus and excludes facility overhead. It shows why hundreds of dense racks quickly pull **powered land, interconnection, substations, transformers, cooling, firm generation, and permitting** into the same delivery chain as chips and HBM.

The relevant industrial variable is therefore:

> **Time-to-Power: how long it takes to move from project intent to energized, usable capacity for a real AI workload.**

## 2. Power requires a delivery-state model

Power / LPS projects should be separated into six states:

> **Announced → Contracted / Secured → Permitted → Construction / Transition → Energized → Utilized**

A 1 GW PPA cannot be rewritten as "1 GW of AI compute online." A secured LPS position or future IT-GW design capacity cannot be treated as energized load, and a 500 MW development target cannot be converted directly into an active rack count. The real economic value of a project depends on how far it has advanced from announcement toward high-utilization delivery—not on the maximum MW/GW in a headline.

Generation MW/GW, PPA capacity, IT-GW, energized facility load, and utilized AI load must retain their distinct physical identities.

## 3. Representative projects sit on different delivery clocks

### 3.1 Microsoft / Constellation: restarting an existing ~835 MW nuclear asset

Microsoft and Constellation entered a **20-year PPA** supporting the restart of the former Three Mile Island Unit 1 as the Crane Clean Energy Center, with an approximately **835 MW** public capacity anchor.[^2]

The case shows how hyperscaler demand can support a large existing power asset returning to service.

But 835 MW is a project / unit-capacity anchor, not evidence that 835 MW is already being delivered in real time to AI data centers. The project still sits on regulatory, repair, restart, interconnection, and commercial-operation clocks.

Constellation's official release states that the restart requires significant investment but does **not** disclose a US$1.6B restart CapEx figure. That number therefore does not enter this chapter's fact layer.

### 3.2 Talen / AWS: a revised PPA of up to 1,920 MW

The Talen/AWS revised PPA has a full contract quantity of **up to 1,920 MW** and runs through 2042.[^3]

Talen's 2026 Q2 10-Q states that the transition to the revised AWS PPA **occurred in April 2026**. An earlier 2025 10-K had stated that **full transition was expected in spring 2027**.[^3][^4]

April 2026 can therefore be used as the current disclosed status of the revised-PPA transition. It **cannot** be interpreted as proof that every physical conversion, transmission modification, and full-delivery step had already been completed.

The relevant forward indicators are the capacity ramp, energized MW, AWS load, and utilization.

This case shows that regulation and commercial structure can change the delivery path for power without making large-scale AI electricity demand disappear.

### 3.3 Google / Kairos: 50 MWe in 2030 and up to 500 MWe by 2035

The Google/Kairos public timeline is much longer: **Hermes 2 up to 50 MWe in 2030**, followed by a fleet of **up to 500 MWe by 2035**.[^5]

This is better understood as a long-duration supply option, industrial-learning path, and capacity hedge than as spot power that can be called on in 2026–2028.

The near-term value of SMRs therefore lies mainly in future optionality; they should not be used to erase today's interconnection, transformer, and powered-site constraints.

### 3.4 PORTS-Pike / NVIDIA / SB Energy / OpenAI: secured LPS with initial 4.25 IT-GW design capacity

On **August 17, 2026**, NVIDIA disclosed a different infrastructure structure at the PORTS-Pike Technology Campus: NVIDIA is providing credit support for land, power, and shell buildout with SB Energy; the initial deployment is designed to support **4.25 IT-GW**; OpenAI will use the site; and SB Energy will build, own, and operate the data center.[^7]

Planned capacity is expected to come online in phases beginning in **2028**.

This makes secured LPS a more direct precursor to deployable compute than a generic headline PPA. But **4.25 IT-GW is still design / planned capacity, not 2026 energized or utilized AI load**.

## 4. Project capacities cannot be summed into active AI load

The project numbers above do not share one physical identity. Generation MW/MWe, PPA capacity, and IT-GW design capacity sit at different layers and cannot be added into one active AI-load figure.

**Table 7.1 — Representative AI power / LPS pathways**

| Project | Nominal scale | Primary status |
|---|---:|---|
| Crane / Microsoft | ~835 MW | restart + regulatory process |
| Talen / AWS | up to 1,920 MW | revised-PPA transition / ramp |
| Kairos / Google | 50 MWe in 2030; up to 500 MWe by 2035 | long-duration development |
| PORTS-Pike / NVIDIA / SB Energy / OpenAI | initial 4.25 IT-GW design capacity | LPS secured / future phased buildout from 2028 |

**Source:** Constellation, Talen Energy, Kairos Power / Google, and NVIDIA.[^2][^3][^4][^5][^7]  
**Note:** Generation MW/MWe, PPA capacity, and IT-GW design capacity are different physical quantities. Their contract, permitting, construction, energization, and utilization states must remain separate.

These pathways represent different infrastructure mechanisms: restarting an existing large generator, restructuring a large-load PPA, developing next-generation nuclear supply, and securing land + power + shell for future AI-factory buildout. Their maturity, units, clocks, and utilization dates are not directly comparable.

## 5. Grid queues measure friction, not AI demand

Berkeley Lab's Queued Up data show that active U.S. generation/storage interconnection requests peaked near **2,600 GW at the end of 2023** and stood at approximately **2,061 GW at the end of 2025**.[^6]

These numbers cannot be converted into "the United States lacks 2,061 GW of data-center power." The queue is primarily generation/storage projects and includes projects that may withdraw, duplicate one another, or experience long delays.

The narrower supported conclusion is that **interconnection and transmission buildout can take years, making Time-to-Power a real constraint.**

What matters for AI system delivery is how many MW a specific region can actually deliver inside a specific hardware-generation window—not the total GW sitting in a national queue.

## 6. Time-to-Power is a system-competition variable

Advanced chips can advance on monthly and quarterly product clocks. Power infrastructure often advances on multi-year clocks.

That creates a variable comparable in importance to the chip roadmap:

> **Time-to-Power**

Projects with existing grid interconnection, powered land, substation / transformer capacity, firm generation, and executable permitting paths gain a timing advantage.

Hyperscaler competition is therefore not only about who acquires more accelerators. It is also about an **illustrative deployment-scale question**:

> **who can energize the next 50 MW / 100 MW / 500 MW of systems sooner.**

The 50 / 100 / 500 MW values are scenario-scale markers used to illustrate Time-to-Power; they are not disclosed project capacities.

## 7. Power can become binding after semiconductor supply eases

AI infrastructure does not have one permanent bottleneck.

One possible **Rolling Bottleneck Cascade** is:

> HBM / advanced packaging tight → hardware delivery constrained → manufacturing supply expands → more racks reach sites → power / interconnection becomes binding → deployment and utilization slow → next-cycle hardware orders / commitments decelerate

The reverse is also possible. If power infrastructure is built far ahead of workload demand, the problem can shift from scarcity to low utilization and weak capital returns.

"Power is now a core constraint" and "power is not yet the sole dominant bottleneck" can therefore both be true.

## 8. Power contracts and compute assets run on different clocks

AI servers have shorter technological and economic lives than major power assets and long-duration PPAs.

Crane involves a **20-year PPA**, while compute hardware will turn over much faster.[^2] This creates a classic duration mismatch:

> **shorter-cycle compute asset vs. longer-cycle power / infrastructure asset**

In high-demand scenarios, long-duration power access can be a moat. In S4 Capital Hard Landing, the same long-duration obligation can become a burden.

Power contracts therefore need to be evaluated through duration, ramp, take-or-pay / cancellation terms, project execution, utilization, and residual value.

## 9. When does power become the binding constraint?

Power should be upgraded from "one of the core constraints" to "the dominant constraint" only if several signals appear together:

- major buyers repeatedly disclose power/interconnection as the binding growth ceiling;
- delivered hardware sits idle waiting to be energized;
- energized capacity persistently lags deployment plans;
- next-generation orders are delayed by power constraints;
- Time-to-Power remains a decisive differentiator for high-value powered sites;
- utilization and capital returns deteriorate systematically because of power-delivery timing.

As of August 2026, public evidence supports a narrower conclusion:

> **Power has joined HBM and advanced packaging as a core physical constraint, but the evidence is not yet sufficient to show that power alone dominates the entire AI-infrastructure expansion rate.**

## 10. What would change the view

The judgment that power / LPS is a core AI-infrastructure constraint should be downgraded if the following conditions persist:

- Time-to-Power shortens materially;
- secured / contracted projects convert into energized capacity at high and predictable rates;
- transformers, substations, grid interconnection, and cooling stop causing major deployment delays;
- energized capacity grows persistently faster than deployable compute demand;
- regional power price / availability stops materially affecting site selection.

Headline GW growth alone is not sufficient evidence that the power bottleneck has disappeared. The relevant test is the speed of state transition into energized capacity and actual utilization.

## References

[^1]: NVIDIA, DGX GB200 Hardware Guide, rack power approximately 120 kW, https://docs.nvidia.com/dgx/dgxgb200-user-guide/hardware.html
[^2]: Constellation, Crane Clean Energy Center / Microsoft 20-year PPA and approximately 835 MW unit, https://investors.constellationenergy.com/news-releases/news-release-details/constellation-launch-crane-clean-energy-center-restoring-jobs
[^3]: Talen Energy, 2026 Q2 Form 10-Q, revised AWS PPA transition and up to 1,920 MW, U.S. SEC, https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1622536/000162253626000066/tln-20260630.htm
[^4]: Talen Energy, 2025 Form 10-K, expected full transition timing, U.S. SEC, https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1622536/000162253626000017/tln-20251231.htm
[^5]: Kairos Power / Google partnership, Hermes 2 up to 50 MWe in 2030 and fleet up to 500 MWe by 2035, https://www.kairospower.com/google
[^6]: Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory, “Queued Up” 2026 update, active interconnection queue, https://emp.lbl.gov/news/backlog-power-plants-seeking-transmission-grid-connection-eased-somewhat-2025-amidst
[^7]: NVIDIA, “NVIDIA Guarantees SB Energy's PORTS-Pike Technology Campus in Ohio to Exclusively Host NVIDIA AI Compute,” 2026-08-17, https://investor.nvidia.com/news/press-release-details/2026/NVIDIA-Guarantees-SB-Energys-PORTS-Pike-Technology-Campus-in-Ohio-to-Exclusively-Host-NVIDIA-AI-Compute/default.aspx

# Part I · Chapter 8: Quantitative Framework — What Can Be Measured, What Cannot, and Why

## 1. Quantification starts with denominator closure

The first quantitative question is not “what is the number?” It is **whether a defensible number exists at all**.

A metric is admissible only when numerator, denominator, physical unit, delivery window, qualification boundary, and evidence identity can be made consistent. Front-end wafers, HBM bits, package starts, energized MW, accounting CapEx, and seller commitments are not interchangeable components of one synthetic total.

The report therefore permits four quantitative classes in the main argument:

1. **Compute / memory intensity**;
2. **Rack-scale material or power intensity**;
3. **Layer-specific stress tests**;
4. **Capacity / commitment / utilization indicators**.

If a public denominator cannot be closed, the variable remains a parameter or proxy. The report does not fill the gap with a point estimate simply to make the model look complete.

## 2. Peak FLOPS is not a workload denominator

The core Roofline relationship is:

> **Performance = min(Peak Compute, Memory Bandwidth × Arithmetic Intensity)**

The critical arithmetic intensity is:

> **I_critical = Peak Compute / Memory Bandwidth**

When workload arithmetic intensity falls below the critical threshold, the system becomes memory-bound.

Autoregressive inference repeatedly reads model state, KV cache, routing, and activation data. Actual token throughput can therefore remain constrained by HBM bandwidth and memory movement even when accelerator peak FLOPS are very high.

This is why HBM, interconnect, and compute have to be analyzed as one system rather than interpreting frontier inference from peak compute alone.

## 3. Efficiency changes the demand function, not the measurement standard

Algorithm and architecture choices can change:

- activated compute;
- bytes/token;
- KV-cache requirements;
- communication patterns;
- batch / concurrency;
- serving efficiency.

Public cases such as DeepSeek-V3 / V2 show that architecture improvements can significantly change the unit resources required for a given capability.[^1][^2]

But a changed demand function does not eliminate physical constraints. Whether higher efficiency reduces aggregate infrastructure demand still depends on workload volume and demand elasticity.

## 4. Training-run arithmetic is a bounded cost conversion

DeepSeek-V3 reports **2.788M H800 GPU-hours** for full formal training, and the paper uses a **US$2/GPU-hour** rental-cost assumption.[^1]

A clearly labeled **D-grade arithmetic conversion** is therefore:

> **2.788M × US$2 ≈ US$5.576M**

This is only a formal-training compute conversion. It excludes research iteration, failed runs, data engineering, engineering payroll, cluster depreciation, networking, storage, and the post-training ecosystem.

The US$5.576M figure can therefore be used as a public training-run scale anchor, but not as "the full company cost of one frontier model."

## 5. System specs support intensity analysis, not deployment inference

The official GB200 NVL72 configuration is:[^3]

- 72 Blackwell GPUs;
- 36 Grace CPUs;
- 13.4 TB HBM3E;
- 17 TB LPDDR5X.

These specifications are useful for describing the **material and memory intensity of one rack-scale system**.

They cannot be multiplied by an invented industry rack count and presented as actual HBM, LPDDR, packaging, or power demand.

Therefore:

> **per-system specification × observed deployment can support demand analysis; per-system specification × hypothetical deployment remains a scenario and should not be used as a factual industry total.**

Where shipment or deployment cannot be closed from public evidence, the formal report prefers seller commitments, vendor supply guidance, customer contracts, lead times, utilization, and other observable indicators over synthetic fleet-size arithmetic.

## 6. HBM: bits and wafer input are different denominators

Micron FY24 Q2 prepared remarks provide an important first-party boundary:

> **industrywide / same technology node / same number of bits**, HBM3E requires approximately **3× the wafer supply of DDR5**.[^4]

Bit demand and manufacturing input are therefore not one-to-one. As HBM bit share rises, pressure on DRAM wafer / cleanroom input can rise faster than bit share alone would suggest.

The 3× figure is not a fixed Micron-fab MES constant and cannot be mechanically extrapolated into a permanent HBM4/HBM5 ratio.

## 7. Advanced packaging requires a separate physical denominator

Advanced-packaging analysis should use observable or source-bounded variables such as qualified capacity, lead time, allocation fulfillment, package starts, or supplier guidance.

The report does not use a synthetic "CoWoS wafers per rack" factor to infer shipments.

Front-end wafers, back-end packaging capacity, HBM bits, and energized MW are different physical pools and must remain dimensionally separate.

## 8. Absolute Capacity Gap: current balance

For each physical layer (i), define:

```text
AI_Capacity_Gap_i(t)
= AI_Effective_Demand_i(t)
- AI_Effective_Capacity_i(t)
```

The layer (i) can separately represent:

- advanced logic;
- HBM / DRAM;
- advanced packaging;
- networking;
- power.

Demand and capacity must use the same:

- physical layer;
- delivery window;
- qualification / quality boundary;
- physical unit.

Examples include wafers/month, qualified package starts/month, bits/year, and MW.

Interpretation:

- **Gap > 0:** current effective supply is insufficient;
- **Gap ≈ 0:** the market is near balance;
- **Gap < 0:** effective slack exists.

---

## 9. Capacity Growth Gap: direction of travel

Define:

```text
AI_Capacity_Growth_Gap_i(t)
= Growth(AI_Effective_Demand_i)
- Growth(AI_Effective_Capacity_i)
```

It answers:

> **Is the current supply-demand gap more likely to widen or narrow?**

- **Growth Gap > 0:** shortage pressure may intensify, or existing slack may shrink;
- **Growth Gap < 0:** supply is catching up and shortage pressure may ease;
- **Growth Gap = 0:** demand and capacity are growing at the same rate; it does not mean the current gap is zero.

Therefore:

> **Absolute Gap and Growth Gap must be read together.**

A market can still be short while supply is catching up rapidly, or still have slack while faster demand growth is eroding that slack.

---

## 10. Non-AI Residual Capacity answers a different question

AI Capacity Gap measures the AI side.

Traditional markets require a separate residual-access measure:

```text
NonAI_Residual(t)
= Effective_Supply(t)
- AI_Server_Absorption(t)
- Other_Priority_Demand(t)
```

It asks:

> **After AI and other priority demand absorb supply, how much effective capacity remains available to non-AI markets?**

AI Capacity Gap, AI Capacity Growth Gap, and Non-AI Residual Capacity are therefore not interchangeable. The first two describe AI-side balance and direction; the third describes residual access for traditional markets.

---

## 11. Power needs both physical quantity and delivery state

Approximately **120 kW/rack** can be used as a rack-level power-density input.[^5]

Power must also retain its state identity:

> Announced → Contracted / Secured → Permitted → Construction / Transition → Energized → Utilized

Contracted or secured capacity is not energized capacity; energized capacity is not highly utilized AI load. Generation MW/GW, PPA capacity, and IT-GW must also retain their distinct physical identities.

### Table 8.1 — NVL72 rack-to-MW D-grade intensity conversion

| Item | Official / known input | Mechanical conversion at 1 MW of rack IT load |
|---|---:|---:|
| Rack power | ≈120 kW / rack | ≈8.33 racks |
| GPU | 72 / rack | ≈600 GPUs |
| HBM3E | 13.4 TB / rack | ≈111.7 TB |
| LPDDR5X | 17 TB / rack | ≈141.7 TB |

**Source:** NVIDIA GB200 NVL72 official specification and DGX GB200 Hardware Guide.[^3][^5]  
**Note:** This is a **D-grade mechanical intensity conversion**. It answers only: “if 1 MW of rack IT load were composed entirely of this rack configuration, what would the hardware intensity be?” It is **not** a shipment, deployment, energized-facility, or industry-demand estimate, and it excludes PUE, network/storage, power-conversion, redundancy, and facility overhead.

This kind of conversion turns system specifications into auditable engineering intensity while preserving the boundary that specification is not deployment.

## 12. When shipment is unobservable, use bounded forward indicators

NVIDIA's disclosed manufacturing, supply, and capacity commitments progressed as follows:[^6]

- 2026-01-25: **US$95.2B**;
- 2026-04-26: **US$119B**;
- 2026-07-26: **US$279B**.

The FY27 Q2 10-Q states that the latest commitments are primarily associated with memory and manufacturing facilities required for data-center infrastructure systems.

The figure cannot identify how much goes to any individual supplier, but it is an observable proxy for future resource-reservation intensity.

When customer-level HBM bits, CoWoS allocation, and rack shipment are unavailable, forward commitments are more informative than "revenue divided by an invented rack price."

## 13. Scarce-capacity access remains layer-specific

The report does not collapse advanced logic, HBM, advanced packaging, networking, and power into one composite "capture ratio."

For each layer, track variables such as:

- qualified supplier count;
- lead time;
- allocation fulfillment;
- long-term agreements / prepayments;
- delivery timing;
- buyer credit quality;
- supply elasticity.

A reliable percentage may be used only when customer-level allocation is observable for that specific physical pool.

### Table 8.2 — 2026 public-data quantification readiness by layer

| Physical layer | Publicly observable inputs today | Can Absolute Gap be closed? | More defensible 2026 output |
|---|---|---|---|
| Advanced logic | node / platform revenue mix, CapEx, supplier guidance, selected roadmaps | **No**: customer-level qualified wafer demand / allocation is missing | direction, access proxies, node mix; no synthetic wafer-share gap |
| HBM / DRAM | vendor supply guidance, same-bits wafer trade ratio, institutional bit forecasts | **Partly quantifiable, but no global gap closure**: customer-level bits / wafer allocation is missing | manufacturing intensity, tightness, product-mix shift |
| Advanced packaging | supplier guidance, tightness statements, selected institutional demand estimates | **No**: no unified qualified package-start denominator or customer allocation | lead time, qualification, allocation proxies |
| Networking | system specifications, public topology / deployment, vendor product data | **Partial**: no unified workload-level topology / utilization denominator | workload-specific bandwidth / latency / TCO |
| Power / LPS | project-level contracted / secured / permitted / construction / energized states | **Partly at project level; not globally** | Time-to-Power, state conversion, project delivery gap |
| Capital / Utilization | CapEx, supply commitments, financing structure, selected revenue disclosure | **Capital base is visible; real utilization is not broadly public** | capital-base growth versus utilization / monetization proxies |

**Source:** Report synthesis based on the degree to which public evidence can close each denominator.  
**Note:** “Not closed” is not an excuse for missing data. It identifies **which denominator is absent**. A layer should be upgraded to an auditable Absolute Gap only when demand and capacity are observable in the same physical unit, delivery window, and qualification boundary.

The quantitative rule is therefore:

> **Calculate what can be closed; use explicit proxies where closure is partial; do not fill a missing denominator with a point estimate.**

## 14. Separate risk classes before testing for cascade

System risk in AI infrastructure can be divided into:

1. **Physical bottleneck:** logic / HBM / packaging / interconnect;
2. **Power bottleneck:** grid / substation / permit / cooling;
3. **Capital / utilization risk:** asset utilization fails to cover depreciation, power, operations, and financing;
4. **System-substitution risk:** cloud custom stacks / open software change platform economics;
5. **Regulatory / geopolitical risk:** export controls, supply interruption, interface remedies, regional fragmentation.

One risk does not equal an industry-wide crisis. The more dangerous state is when several clocks begin to reinforce one another.

## 15. Rolling Bottleneck Cascade is a state-transition model

AI-infrastructure bottlenecks rotate rather than remaining permanently fixed at one layer.

A positive expansion cascade can look like:

> HBM / packaging tight → hardware delivery constrained → supply expands → more racks reach sites → power / interconnection becomes binding → utilization becomes the next constraint

A reverse capital cascade can look like:

> power delay → utilization decline → buyer ROI decline → next CapEx slows → hardware orders slow → HBM / packaging tightness eases → upstream CapEx slows

This is the **Rolling Bottleneck Cascade**.

It explains why easing HBM does not prove that AI infrastructure has no constraints, and why power becoming a bottleneck does not mean semiconductor constraints have disappeared permanently.

## 16. Capital returns have to reconcile different asset clocks

A minimal economic relationship is:

> **Economic Return = Revenue from deployed compute − Depreciation − Power − Operations − Financing**

The core risk is duration mismatch: hardware can turn over rapidly while data-center shells, grid assets, PPAs, and debt remain in place for much longer.

A technically functional system can therefore produce weak capital returns if utilization is low or long-duration obligations are excessive.

## 17. False-precision guardrails

The full report maintains the following hard boundaries:

- revenue share ≠ wafer share;
- platform revenue share ≠ physical shipment share;
- contracted MW ≠ energized MW;
- energized MW ≠ utilized AI load;
- rack spec × hypothetical racks ≠ shipment;
- CoWoS geometry ≠ fab yield;
- CapEx guidance ≠ actual CapEx;
- AI exposure ≠ job loss;
- model rental conversion ≠ full company cash cost;
- institutional forecast ≠ company actual;
- one physical pool ≠ another physical pool.

## 18. Preferred quantitative workflow

The report follows this sequence:

> **official system spec → material identity → scenario demand → supply constraint → forward commitment → utilization → falsification**

The alternative—starting with a grand end-state and searching backward for numbers to fill a model—is not accepted.

This discipline does not require conservative scenarios. It allows scenarios to remain ambitious while preserving an auditable fact layer and consistent physical dimensions.

## References

[^1]: DeepSeek-AI, “DeepSeek-V3 Technical Report,” https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.19437
[^2]: DeepSeek-AI, “DeepSeek-V2,” repository and technical report, https://github.com/deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V2
[^3]: NVIDIA, “GB200 NVL72,” official product page, https://www.nvidia.com/en-us/data-center/gb200-nvl72/
[^4]: Micron Technology, FY24 Q2 Prepared Remarks, HBM3E ≈3× DDR5 wafer supply at same node / same bits, https://s25.q4cdn.com/621799436/files/doc_financials/2024/q2/FY24-Q2-Prepared-Remarks.pdf
[^5]: NVIDIA, DGX GB200 Hardware Guide, rack power approximately 120 kW, https://docs.nvidia.com/dgx/dgxgb200-user-guide/hardware.html
[^6]: NVIDIA Corporation, FY27 Q2 Form 10-Q, aggregate manufacturing, supply and capacity commitments, U.S. SEC, https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1045810/000104581026000075/nvda-20260726.htm

# Part I · Chapter 9: Semiconductor Supply-Chain Resilience — Equipment, Materials, and Geographic Redundancy

## 1. Resilience is a system-continuity problem

Frontier-AI supply security is not a map of fabs or corporate nationalities. The relevant test is whether the industrial chain can **keep producing, qualifying, servicing, repairing, and scaling through disruption**.

The physical supply chain of a frontier AI system includes at least:

- lithography / deposition / etch / metrology equipment;
- silicon wafers and high-purity materials;
- leading-edge foundry;
- HBM;
- advanced packaging / substrates;
- testing;
- data-center power / cooling.

Supply security for an accelerator designer is therefore not determined by chip design alone. Multiple industrial layers must be simultaneously available.

## 2. Equipment is itself a system-level capability

ASML's 2025 Annual Report reports **48 EUV lithography systems sold** during the year. By the end of 2025, customers had run more than **400,000 wafers** on High-NA systems while qualification continued.[^1]

On **July 15, 2026**, ASML announced the first High-NA EUV use in high-volume logic production on select Intel 18A layers for Panther Lake.[^8] That moves High-NA from a qualification-only phase into an observed HVM production milestone, while still not implying that High-NA has become universal across leading-edge logic.

The strategic constraint in advanced lithography cannot therefore be captured by a single-machine price.

The relevant variables are:

- system availability;
- throughput;
- imaging / overlay performance;
- customer qualification;
- field service;
- long-term coordination across optics / source / stage / metrology.

High-NA matters primarily as a process-roadmap and industrial-maturity path, not as a media-reported price tag.

## 3. A fab is not independent of its supplier ecosystem

Leading-edge manufacturing depends on equipment installation, spare parts, software, process recipes, materials, metrology, and long-duration field service.

Even if two regions both contain fab buildings, their effective capacity will not be equivalent if supplier density, engineering talent, qualification cycles, tool service, and material stability differ.

This report therefore defines supply-chain independence as **system-level continuity**, not simply "a fab exists on the map."

### 3.1 Upstream continuity has multiple independent constraints

The existence of a domestic fab does not automatically mean that capacity expansion is independent of external upstream supply.

ASML's public export-control statements provide an auditable boundary:[^2][^3]

- sales of EUV systems to China are already restricted;
- the most advanced immersion DUV systems require Dutch export licences;
- the 2024 U.S. update expanded restrictions to additional advanced semiconductor-manufacturing technologies, including metrology and software.

This does **not** support the claim that every Chinese fab would stop operating under a supply interruption. ASML itself notes that the controls focus on advanced technologies / specific fabs rather than every system.

The variables that matter are:

> **critical equipment availability + software/licensing + spare parts/field service + materials qualification**

These variables determine whether new capacity can ramp on schedule, whether existing lines can sustain yield, and whether process generations can continue advancing.

"Memory capacity is expanding" and "the memory supply chain is fully autonomous" are therefore different propositions.

## 4. Material qualification is a hidden time cost

Advanced nodes are highly sensitive to contamination, particles, uniformity, defect density, and long-term stability.

Changing a materials supplier is not a normal procurement switch. Even chemically similar materials can require requalification and renewed learning across yield, tool recipes, and reliability.

The relevant materials constraint is therefore not an unsupported market-share statistic. It is:

> **Does a qualified alternative supplier exist, and how long would switching take?**

## 5. Geographic redundancy is real, but cluster replication is slow

TSMC's 2025 Annual Report and 2026 Arizona updates show:[^4][^9]

- the first Arizona fab entered N4 high-volume production in **4Q24**;
- the second Arizona fab structure is complete and targets **3 nm volume production in 2H27**;
- construction of the third Arizona fab began in 2025, with N2 / A16 planned and volume production targeted around the end of this decade;
- a fourth fab and the first advanced-packaging facility entered early construction activity in 2026;
- the current Arizona plan has expanded to **six logic fabs + two advanced-packaging facilities + an R&D center**;
- the first Kumamoto fab in Japan entered volume production by the end of 2024, while the second fab plan includes 3 nm;
- the Dresden project in Germany targets automotive / industrial specialty technologies;
- Taiwan continues building 2 nm and more advanced capacity together with advanced packaging.

Geographic diversification is therefore a real trend, and it is extending from logic fabs into packaging and R&D.

It is still not the same as reproducing the Taiwan cluster overnight.

Different regions continue to differ in node mix, product mix, packaging, workforce, supplier density, and power/water conditions.

## 5A. Geographic, supplier, and technology redundancy are different

A TSMC fab in another country mainly increases **geographic redundancy**.

It does not automatically create:

- foundry supplier redundancy;
- EDA / equipment redundancy;
- HBM supplier redundancy;
- packaging supplier redundancy;
- design portability.

At least three forms of resilience therefore need to be separated.

### Geographic Redundancy

The same technology / supplier operates manufacturing capacity across multiple regions.

This reduces single-region disruption risk while potentially retaining dependence on the same company, process ecosystem, or key equipment base.

### Supplier Redundancy

The same workload / design can move between two or more qualified suppliers.

That requires:

- process portability;
- design requalification;
- packaging compatibility;
- yield / reliability validation;
- software / firmware adaptation.

This is materially harder than geographic expansion by the same supplier.

### Technology Redundancy

If one equipment class, process, or architecture is constrained, another technical path can still deliver acceptable performance / cost.

Therefore:

> **geographic diversification ≠ true second source ≠ technology independence**

For frontier-AI supply security, the highest-value resilience comes from improving all three forms of redundancy rather than merely making the fab map more geographically distributed.
## 6. Subsidies reduce redundancy cost; they do not create instant independence

The U.S. Department of Commerce final CHIPS award for TSMC Arizona includes up to **US$6.6B of direct funding** supporting three leading-edge fabs. Final NAPMP advanced-packaging awards total **US$1.4B**, while Amkor's Arizona advanced-packaging/test facility received up to **US$407M of direct funding**.[^5][^6][^7]

These programs directly support a broader conclusion:

> **Strategic redundancy requires additional public capital to offset geographic duplication, cluster formation, and early-utilization risk.**

Subsidies cannot instantly reproduce decades of yield learning, supplier qualification, and engineering density. They can accelerate capacity formation, but they are not equivalent to immediate supply-chain independence.

## 7. Redundancy carries an efficiency cost

Greater resilience often requires:

- duplicated fabs / packaging lines;
- duplicated inventories;
- spare / reserve capacity;
- regional supplier qualification;
- lower initial utilization;
- government incentives;
- additional engineering / compliance overhead.

The supply chain can therefore move from:

> **lowest-cost global optimization**

toward:

> **higher-cost strategic redundancy**

This is not simply an efficiency failure. It is a changed cost function under a changed security objective.

## 8. AI demand accelerates geographic restructuring

Demand for leading-edge logic, HBM, packaging, and power has pushed advanced manufacturing beyond the normal commercial cycle toward strategic infrastructure.

TSMC's 2025 Annual Report links Arizona expansion to strong multi-year AI-related demand, while U.S. CHIPS programs treat AI/HPC as important use cases for leading-edge and advanced-packaging investment.[^4][^5][^6]

The AI-infrastructure cycle and industrial-policy cycle are therefore increasingly overlapping.

## 9. Geographic redundancy is not full localization

A regional supply stack has to be tested across:

- equipment / service;
- materials;
- foundry;
- HBM;
- packaging / substrate;
- testing;
- power / water;
- workforce;
- customer qualification.

Localizing one layer reduces dependency in that layer only. It does not automatically close the other risks.

This is why the frontier-system resilience of China, the United States, Europe, or Japan cannot be summarized with one localization percentage.

## 10. China localization and Western reshoring face the same systems problem

China's localization of equipment, materials, foundry, memory, and packaging and the U.S./European/Japanese reshoring or friend-shoring programs pursue different strategic objectives.

They nevertheless face the same engineering requirement:

> **performance + yield + scale + qualification + cost must all work at the same time.**

The existence of one product is not system closure. Real substitution must be sustainable at production scale, reliability, and long-duration service.

## 11. Fragmentation can raise investment while lowering system efficiency

If the world develops two or more relatively separate semiconductor / AI system ecosystems, the result can include:

- repeated fab investment;
- repeated packaging investment;
- duplicated software/runtime;
- higher strategic inventory;
- lower cross-region fungibility;
- more policy-driven capacity.

Fragmentation does not necessarily reduce global semiconductor CapEx. It can instead produce **more duplicated construction, lower utilization efficiency inside each individual system, and greater resilience**.

## 12. Evidence standard for supply-chain resilience

This report does not define complete self-sufficiency as the only valid objective.

More useful tests are:

1. have single points of failure been reduced?
2. does each critical layer have a qualified second source?
3. how long does system-level recovery take after disruption?
4. does regional capacity have sufficient power, workforce, and supplier support?
5. can the cost of redundancy still be covered by the economics of the final workload?

The real competition is therefore not which country produces every input domestically, but:

> **who can maintain the highest level of system continuity at an acceptable cost when a critical disruption occurs.**

## 13. Current read

As of 2026, public evidence supports a relatively clear direction:

- core leading-edge technologies remain highly concentrated;
- High-NA EUV has moved from qualification into real HVM logic production;
- geographic redundancy is accelerating and increasingly covers logic + packaging + R&D;
- supplier redundancy is improving more slowly than geographic redundancy;
- government participation has increased materially;
- advanced packaging is now an explicit policy and investment object;
- redundancy raises resilience while also raising capital and operating cost;
- overseas fab expansion still needs time to reproduce supplier, workforce, packaging, and infrastructure ecosystems.

The semiconductor supply chain of the next decade is therefore more likely to consist of **highly concentrated core technologies + greater regional redundancy + higher strategic inventories + greater public-capital participation** than to return quickly to a fully dispersed and fully substitutable global market.
## References

[^4]: TSMC, 2025 Annual Report, Arizona/Japan/Germany/Taiwan manufacturing footprint and AI-related demand context, https://investor.tsmc.com/static/annualReports/2025/english/index.html

# Part II · Chapter 10: Model Unit Economics, Algorithmic Efficiency, and Open-Source Competition

## 1. Model economics cannot be separated from infrastructure economics

APIs, subscriptions, and agent services sit on top of a real variable-cost stack: accelerator time, HBM bandwidth, KV cache, storage / retrieval, networking, third-party tools, human review, cloud margin, and serving utilization.

AI software therefore cannot be analyzed with a near-zero-marginal-cost SaaS assumption by default.

The relevant unit is:

> **Revenue per completed workflow − total variable AI cost per completed workflow**

## 2. Algorithmic efficiency changes the demand function

DeepSeek-V3 first-party disclosures include:[^1]

- 671B total parameters;
- 37B activated parameters;
- 14.8T pre-training tokens;
- 2.664M H800 GPU-hours for pre-training;
- 2.788M H800 GPU-hours for full formal training.

Using the paper's US$2/GPU-hour assumption, 2.788M GPU-hours corresponds to approximately **US$5.576M of formal-training compute conversion**.

That is not full enterprise R&D cost.

The important implication is that frontier capability is not linearly fixed to hardware input. Architecture, sparsity, routing, and memory design can materially change the resources required per unit of capability.

## 3. Algorithms can attack memory cost directly

DeepSeek-V2 reports, relative to DeepSeek 67B:[^2]

- approximately **93.3% KV-cache reduction**;
- approximately **5.76× maximum generation throughput**.

These figures belong to V2, not V3.

Their economic significance is that the same HBM capacity can support more concurrency, memory traffic per token can fall, and serving utilization can improve.

Lower unit cost, however, does not guarantee lower aggregate infrastructure demand. If lower cost causes token, agent, and workflow volume to rise faster, total demand can still increase.

### 3A. Price compression does not imply falling aggregate compute demand

Stanford AI Index 2025 provides a historical anchor: at the report's GPT-3.5-equivalent MMLU performance level, inference cost fell from about **US$20 per million tokens** in November 2022 to about **US$0.07 per million tokens** in October 2024—roughly a **280× decline**.[^3]

That comparison depends on a specific benchmark, model set, and time window. It should not be rewritten as "all model prices fell 280×." What it does establish is narrower:

> **The unit price of a benchmark-equivalent capability can compress rapidly; model-layer rent is not fixed.**

Stanford AI Index 2026 also reports further convergence among leading models on Arena-style evaluations, shifting more competitive pressure toward cost, reliability, and domain-specific performance.[^4]

This strengthens the chapter's core mechanism: **falling model prices and rising total infrastructure demand can coexist**. The decisive variables remain workload volume, resource intensity, and utilization.

## 4. Open source compresses model-layer rent before infrastructure rent

Open-source models increase capability diffusion, private deployment, and model-switching options while pressuring API premiums.

Model-layer switching generally happens faster than replacement of accelerators, HBM, networking, packaging, or data-center infrastructure.

The market can therefore experience:

> **model price compression + infrastructure utilization growth**

This is why falling API prices are not equivalent to falling AI-infrastructure demand.

## 5. GPU vs. ASIC should be compared at System TCO

Google TPU, AWS Trainium, Microsoft Maia, and Meta MTIA create alternative compute paths for internal workloads and model companies.

The comparison should not focus on accelerator-die price alone. It must include:

- workload compatibility;
- compiler/runtime maturity;
- HBM / interconnect configuration;
- cluster scaling;
- debugging / porting cost;
- effective utilization.

A theoretically cheaper chip can still produce a higher system TCO if migration, debugging, or utilization are materially worse.

## 6. AI software unit economics

A production workflow should at minimum include:

> **Contribution Margin = Revenue per workflow − inference − retrieval/storage − tools/APIs − retries − human review − compliance/operations**

The metrics that matter include:

- cost per successfully completed task;
- gross margin after inference;
- revenue / productivity value per workflow;
- retention;
- retry / failure rate;
- human-review burden;
- utilization of reserved compute.

## 7. Anchor Buyers let model companies shape infrastructure formation

The largest model companies increasingly influence cloud, chip, and power investment through multi-year compute commitments, dedicated data-center projects, and long-duration capacity reservations.

A model company is therefore not permanently limited to passively accepting cloud pricing. At sufficient scale, it can become a demand anchor for infrastructure capital formation.

The same mechanism amplifies risk. If architecture efficiency rises, revenue underperforms, or workloads migrate elsewhere, long-duration capacity commitments can turn into utilization and duration risk.

## 8. Price compression and rebound

Algorithmic efficiency and open-source competition reduce the unit cost of intelligence.

Aggregate infrastructure demand depends on:

> **Net Infrastructure Demand = Unit Resource Intensity × Total Workload Volume**

If unit resource intensity falls 50% while workload volume grows 4×, total demand still increases.

Long-run analysis therefore has to observe real workload, utilization, and business outcomes rather than training cost, benchmark scores, or token price in isolation.

Chapter 14 adds a third mechanism: the **AI Inheritance Effect**. A large share of potential AI workload does not start from zero; it can be layered onto existing ERP, CRM, databases, cloud systems, APIs, digital documents, source code, and established workflows. Workload growth can therefore come not only from more calls per task but from **the penetration of AI into an already large digital installed base**. Long-run demand must consequently be read through unit efficiency, workflow penetration, automation depth, and execution frequency together.

## 9. Long-run competitive variables at the model layer

The most important economic variables for model companies include:

### Capability per Unit Resource

How much useful capability can be produced from a given amount of compute and memory.

### Serving Utilization

Whether reserved infrastructure can sustain high utilization.

### Model Portability

Whether customers can switch between models at low cost.

### Distribution

Who can acquire and retain workloads at low marginal distribution cost.

### Monetization

Whether model capability converts into durable revenue or measurable productivity value.

### Infrastructure Commitments

Whether long-duration compute commitments remain aligned with real workload growth.

The final variable links directly to the Anchor Compute Buyer mechanism developed in Chapter 13.

## 10. Current read

As of 2026, a more defensible description is:

- model-layer price competition and capability diffusion are real;
- unit cost continues to fall;
- open source raises buyer bargaining power;
- infrastructure demand has not therefore entered a confirmed decline cycle;
- large model companies are using long-duration commitments to shape infrastructure buildout.

Algorithmic efficiency can become either a **demand interrupter** or a **usage multiplier** for infrastructure. Which effect dominates can only be determined through workload volume, utilization, and unit economics.

## References

[^1]: DeepSeek-AI, “DeepSeek-V3 Technical Report,” model architecture and formal-training compute, https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.19437
[^2]: DeepSeek-AI, “DeepSeek-V2,” repository and technical report, MLA / KV-cache and throughput results, https://github.com/deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V2
[^3]: Stanford HAI, “2025 AI Index Report — Research and Development,” inference-price comparison at a GPT-3.5-equivalent MMLU level, https://hai.stanford.edu/ai-index/2025-ai-index-report/research-and-development
[^4]: Stanford HAI, “2026 AI Index Report — Technical Performance,” frontier-model performance convergence and the shift toward cost, reliability, and domain-specific competition, https://hai.stanford.edu/ai-index/2026-ai-index-report/technical-performance

# Part II · Chapter 11: NVIDIA's System-Orchestration Strategy Beyond the Accelerator

## 1. NVIDIA extends control through orchestration, not ownership

NVIDIA's control does not come from owning leading-edge fabs, HBM fabs, or the full data-center real-estate stack.

Its operating model is better described as:

> **interface control + reference architecture + software/runtime + supply orchestration + financing coordination**

The system boundary now spans GPU / CPU, NVLink / NVSwitch, networking, rack-scale design, liquid-cooling requirements, CUDA/runtime, AI Enterprise, NIM / NeMo, DGX, and cloud-service extensions.[^1][^2][^3]

## 2. Rack-scale reference architecture changes OEM / ODM economics

In the accelerator-card era, OEMs had wide latitude to define server architecture around the GPU.

At rack scale, power delivery, cooling, fabric, cable topology, firmware, rack layout, and software validation are more tightly coupled to the reference architecture.

This does not mean Dell, HPE, Lenovo, ODMs, or other integrators lose product capability. It means the system-platform owner exerts stronger constraints over whole-system design and qualification.

## 3. Networking is part of the compute platform

Large-model training and inference increasingly depend on multi-GPU scaling and collective communication.

When accelerators, NVLink/NVSwitch, InfiniBand/Ethernet networking, and the software communication stack are co-designed, the network stops being merely an external component and becomes part of system performance and switching cost.

Platform competition therefore moves from "GPU + arbitrary network" toward a more integrated AI Factory architecture.

## 4. DGX Cloud is a platform-extension channel

Without a separately disclosed large-scale revenue line, DGX Cloud should not be presented as a second business that has already replaced hyperscalers.

Its more defensible strategic functions are:

1. demonstrating the complete NVIDIA architecture;
2. collecting real workload / deployment feedback;
3. lowering the initial adoption barrier for the full stack;
4. increasing NVIDIA's channel and bargaining options relative to hyperscalers.

DGX Cloud is therefore better understood as a platform extension and reference-deployment channel.

## 5. Software deepens integration and migration cost

Software products extend the system platform from hardware purchase into model serving, deployment, optimization, enterprise support, and lifecycle management.

Until independent software revenue is disclosed, this report does not estimate a fixed "software tax" or separate software profit pool.

The more accurate strategic effect is:

> **lower deployment friction inside the platform and higher migration friction outside it.**

## 6. Asset-light orchestration raises capital efficiency

NVIDIA leaves much of the manufacturing CapEx with TSMC, memory vendors, the packaging ecosystem, and infrastructure providers while influencing their expansion through long-duration commitments, prepayments, roadmap coordination, and volume certainty. NVIDIA's FY26 Form 10-K provides a company-level factual anchor for this supply-orchestration model through its manufacturing, supply, and capacity commitments.[^4]

The structure is:

> system definition / cash generation stays with the platform → heavy manufacturing CapEx stays with suppliers → the platform uses orders and commitments to reserve future resources

This raises platform capital efficiency and turns external supply elasticity into part of platform strategy.

## 6A. Asset-light does not mean zero balance-sheet exposure

Transaction structures disclosed in August 2026 show NVIDIA's supply orchestration extending partly into infrastructure capital.

- The company announced compute-financing platforms with large long-duration capital providers, targeting **more than US$500B of third-party capital mobilization over time**.[^5]
- In PORTS-Pike, NVIDIA is providing credit support for land + power + shell buildout and investing **US$1.5B** in SB Energy.[^6]

NVIDIA has not become a traditional heavy-asset operator that owns every fab, data center, and power asset. But "asset-light platform" can no longer be interpreted as zero infrastructure-financing exposure.

A more precise description is:

> **The platform seeks to keep fabs, data centers, and power assets on external balance sheets while selectively using cash, equity, credit support, and long-duration coordination to accelerate formation of critical infrastructure.**

That can improve Time-to-Capacity, but it can also pull customer-concentration, credit, and duration risk partly back onto the platform.

## 7. Physical AI is an extension path, not current Data Center economics

Automotive, robotics, simulation, and edge deployment follow the same system logic: hardware architecture, runtime, simulation, developer tooling, and model deployment form a combined platform.

But "strategically important" does not mean "already contributing Data Center-scale revenue."

Physical AI has to be evaluated independently through design wins, deployment scale, revenue, reliability, and ecosystem adoption.

## 8. Boundaries to vertical expansion

NVIDIA's system expansion faces real counterforces:

- hyperscaler custom system stacks;
- open compiler/runtime and portability;
- alternative networking;
- customer multi-sourcing;
- regulation / interface remedies;
- physical power and supply constraints;
- China's parallel stack.

If these variables strengthen together, NVIDIA can remain the strongest system platform while losing some bargaining power over system definition.

## 9. "AI Factory" is a system-design concept, not an ownership claim

AI Factory should not be interpreted as an ownership claim that "every data center is an NVIDIA factory."

It is better understood as a design philosophy in which compute, memory, networking, software, racks, cooling, and deployment are optimized as one production system.

Its industrial importance is that the sales unit, qualification process, supply commitments, and data-center engineering are pulled into one coordinated roadmap.

## 10. Current read

As of 2026, the clearest change is that NVIDIA has moved materially from accelerator vendor toward system-platform / AI-Factory architecture.

"Vertical expansion" and "the industry is fully controlled by one platform" remain different propositions.

The next question is whether system definition can continue to translate into long-duration workloads, supply commitments, and cross-generation customer retention with low in-platform migration cost.

## References

[^1]: NVIDIA, NIM official product page, optimized inference microservices across cloud, data center, workstation, and edge, https://www.nvidia.com/en-us/ai-data-science/products/nim-microservices/
[^2]: NVIDIA, AI Foundation Models / NeMo, NVIDIA AI Foundry architecture, https://www.nvidia.com/en-us/ai-data-science/foundation-models/
[^3]: NVIDIA, “Introducing NVIDIA DGX Cloud Lepton,” unified AI platform and compute marketplace, https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/introducing-nvidia-dgx-cloud-lepton-a-unified-ai-platform-built-for-developers/
[^4]: NVIDIA Corporation, FY26 Form 10-K, U.S. SEC, Data Center, networking, platform, and supply-chain disclosures, https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1045810/000104581026000021/nvda-20260125.htm
[^5]: NVIDIA, 2026-08-10, AI compute infrastructure financing platforms and >US$500B third-party-capital mobilization target, https://investor.nvidia.com/news/press-release-details/2026/NVIDIA-Partners-With-Apollo-BlackRock-Blackstone-Brookfield-Goldman-Sachs-and-KKR-to-Establish-AI-Compute-Infrastructure-Financing-Platforms-to-Mobilize-Over-500-Billion-of-Third-Party-Capital/default.aspx
[^6]: NVIDIA, 2026-08-17, PORTS-Pike / SB Energy / OpenAI, LPS credit support and US$1.5B SB Energy investment, https://investor.nvidia.com/news/press-release-details/2026/NVIDIA-Guarantees-SB-Energys-PORTS-Pike-Technology-Campus-in-Ohio-to-Exclusively-Host-NVIDIA-AI-Compute/default.aspx
