# Part II · Chapter 12: The Second System Stack — How Hyperscaler Substitution Actually Works

## 1. The test is workload substitution, not chip availability

The strategic question is not whether a hyperscaler can design an ASIC. It is whether an alternative stack can **absorb production workload at an acceptable TCO, migration cost, reliability, and scale — and keep doing so across generations**.

Google TPU, AWS Trainium, Microsoft Maia, and Meta MTIA therefore have to be evaluated as system stacks:

- accelerator;
- HBM / memory subsystem;
- compiler/runtime;
- scale-up fabric;
- scale-out network;
- rack/pod;
- orchestration;
- production workload.

The report uses **Cloud System-Stack Substitution** as shorthand for this economic substitution test. A benchmark win is not enough. The stack has to survive production utilization, software friction, and workload-specific system economics.

## 2. Captive workload is the structural advantage

The structural difference between the major clouds and an ordinary chip startup is that hyperscalers simultaneously possess:

- massive internal workloads;
- data-center scale;
- compiler / software engineering;
- cloud distribution;
- capital;
- power procurement;
- custom-silicon teams.

They do not need to reproduce CUDA's entire external developer ecosystem on day one.

If an in-house system continuously lowers cost on a large captive workload, it already creates real economic value and generates production feedback for improving the compiler, interconnect, rack, and deployment tooling.

That produces a powerful learning loop:

> **internal workload → production feedback → system optimization → lower TCO → more internal workload**

## 3. Google: TPU is already a system stack

Google's TPU path spans multiple generations.

Ironwood further moves the product definition toward large pod/system scale, combining custom accelerators with XLA/software, high-bandwidth interconnect, HBM, and Google Cloud workloads.[^1]

Google's competitive unit is therefore:

> **TPU + compiler/runtime + network + pod + captive workload + cloud distribution**

If this system continuously lowers total cost of ownership for selected training or inference workloads, it can divert part of NVIDIA's platform profit even without becoming the universal development platform for the entire industry.

## 4. AWS: Trainium is a system, not a component

AWS Trainium has likewise moved from accelerator toward system.

Trainium3 UltraServers, the Neuron software stack, AWS networking/data-center deployment, and large cloud workloads together form an alternative system.[^2]

AWS also benefits from long-duration anchor demand. Anthropic has publicly deepened its compute relationship with AWS, giving Trainium a large workload base on which to iterate system economics.[^3]

But "having an anchor workload" is not the same as replacing NVIDIA's external ecosystem.

The variables that still need to be tested include large-cluster scaling, utilization, software maturity, migration cost, and external-customer adoption.

## 5. Microsoft: Maia matters only if Azure-scale economics close

The correct standard for Microsoft Maia 200 is not whether it supports every model on day one.[^4]

It is whether Maia can create a stable cost advantage on a sufficiently large Azure / Microsoft internal workload.

Long-run competitiveness depends on:

- compiler/runtime maturity;
- scale-up / scale-out capability;
- HBM configuration and supply;
- rack/data-center integration;
- high utilization;
- broader Azure deployment.

If those conditions hold across a large enough captive workload, Maia can divert part of external platform economics.

Becoming a general external substitute would require lower migration cost and a wider ecosystem.

## 6. Meta: specialization can substitute without generality

Meta operates very large recommendation, ranking, and generative-AI workloads.

MTIA 300's logic is not to become a general-purpose accelerator first.[^5] It targets the company's most important internal workloads and gradually organizes silicon, software, networking, and rack deployment into a custom full-stack solution.

The economic objective is:

> **give the company's largest workloads a lower long-run TCO.**

MTIA does not need to beat general-purpose GPUs across every workload to create value.

## 7. Substitution changes platform economics before it changes upstream scarcity

### 7.1 Platform-rent diversion

In-house systems can reduce external accelerator purchases, lower dependence on an external software/platform stack, improve procurement bargaining power, and keep more economics inside the cloud platform.

### 7.2 Upstream scarcity can persist

Next-generation custom systems still need advanced logic, HBM, advanced packaging, networking, and power.

Their expansion can therefore increase competition for the same upstream scarce resources used by NVIDIA.

The combined result is:

> **Cloud custom silicon can weaken NVIDIA platform profit while intensifying competition for TSMC / HBM / packaging / power.**

The two effects are not contradictory.

## 8. Cloud CapEx is not an NVIDIA-revenue proxy

Hyperscaler CapEx includes custom silicon, third-party accelerators, networking, memory, data centers, power, offices, fulfillment assets, and other categories. Fiscal periods, PPE definitions, and lease accounting also differ across companies.

Therefore:

> **Cloud CapEx ≠ AI hardware subtotal ≠ NVIDIA purchases**

Higher CapEx can support NVIDIA shipments while also accelerating systems designed to substitute for NVIDIA.

The four-cloud CapEx numbers cannot be compared directly with NVIDIA revenue to construct an "AI compute tax rate."

## 9. Algorithmic efficiency is a separate axis

Algorithmic and architectural efficiency directly affects system demand, but it is a different competitive axis from hyperscaler custom silicon.

Efficiency can:

- reduce compute / memory required for the same workload;
- lower serving cost;
- increase hardware utilization;
- stimulate additional token / workflow demand.

Efficiency gains can therefore reduce unit hardware demand while increasing aggregate infrastructure demand through demand elasticity.

DeepSeek training-cost, architecture-efficiency, and unit-intelligence economics are analyzed in [Chapter 10](part-II-ch10-model-economics.md). This chapter does not repeat GPU-hours or KV-cache figures, so the same evidence has one canonical home.

---

## 10. Evidence standard for real substitution

Single-chip benchmarks are not enough.

At least the following variables need to be observed together:

1. mature compiler/runtime;
2. large-cluster scaling;
3. high utilization;
4. acceptable workload-migration cost;
5. sustainable HBM / packaging / power supply;
6. stable rack/pod deployment;
7. significant production workload share.

Success on internal workloads can already create real economic substitution.

But broader erosion of an external general-purpose platform additionally requires:

- cross-customer portability;
- cross-workload reliability;
- external developer adoption;
- persistently lower migration cost.

---

## 10A. Two thresholds: internal economics and external ecosystem

A cloud custom system can succeed at two distinct levels.

### Internal Economic Substitution

A custom stack already creates real economic substitution if, inside the hyperscaler's own or anchor workloads, it can:

- run reliably;
- sustain high utilization;
- deliver lower TCO than the external alternative;
- continue iterating across generations.

This level does not require reproducing the entire CUDA external ecosystem.

### External Ecosystem Substitution

To weaken a general-purpose external platform more broadly, the system additionally needs:

- external customer adoption;
- developer portability;
- broader workload coverage;
- low migration cost;
- stable commercial support;
- multi-generation compatibility.

Therefore:

> **internal success ≠ external ecosystem replacement**

Internal success can still materially change hyperscaler procurement, bargaining power, and long-run platform-profit distribution.

## 11. The likely equilibrium is workload segmentation

The most realistic long-run structure may not be one side eliminating the other.

A more plausible equilibrium is workload segmentation.

That segmentation also has to distinguish **training topology** from **inference topology**. Frontier training is typically more sensitive to large-scale collective communication, scale-up / scale-out fabrics, and synchronization efficiency. Inference is more workload-dependent, with memory capacity / bandwidth, latency, batching, power efficiency, and cost per token often carrying greater weight. At the same time, inference does not mean networking becomes irrelevant: Google Ironwood still integrates a 9,216-chip pod with dedicated ICI, while Microsoft states that Maia 200 scales over standard Ethernet to clusters of up to 6,144 accelerators.[^1][^4]

The report therefore does not generalize frontier-training interconnect barriers to all inference workloads, nor does it assume that custom silicon / Ethernet makes large-scale inference network-insensitive. The correct comparison is **memory, network, latency, utilization, and TCO for the specific workload topology**.

### Areas where NVIDIA can remain stronger

- general-purpose frontier training;
- broad external developer ecosystems;
- heterogeneous enterprise workloads;
- workload portability across customers.

### Areas where hyperscaler custom stacks can be stronger

- massive internal workloads;
- repeatable architecture;
- vertically optimized training / inference;
- cloud-specific TCO optimization;
- captive distribution.

The boundary will move with software maturity, memory supply, interconnect, workload structure, and migration cost.

---

## 12. Current read

As of August 2026, the most defensible description is:

- cloud custom silicon is already a real competitive force;
- the competitive unit has moved from custom chip to complete system stack;
- hyperscalers are attacking accelerator, software, interconnect, rack/pod, and workload economics together;
- frontier training and the broad external ecosystem have not been shown to have systematically moved away from NVIDIA;
- custom systems still compete heavily for advanced logic, HBM, advanced packaging, and power.

Therefore:

> **Cloud System-Stack Substitution is one of the structural variables most capable of moving S2 — Baseline Concentration toward S1 — Competitive Expansion.**

Its strength must be measured through production workload share, migration cost, utilization, external adoption, and upstream-resource dependence—not product-launch count.

## 13. What would change the view

Cloud System-Stack Substitution should be downgraded if custom systems remain confined to small internal workloads and the following conditions persist:

- production utilization remains low;
- migration / debugging cost stays high;
- external customer adoption remains weak;
- multi-generation software compatibility remains insufficient;
- HBM / packaging / power access does not improve;
- hyperscalers increase dependence on one external platform again.

The evidence that would genuinely support S1 is not another chip launch. It is simultaneous improvement in production workload share, TCO, portability, and external adoption.

## References

[^1]: Google Cloud, “Ironwood TPU,” https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/infrastructure-and-cloud/google-cloud/ironwood-tpu-age-of-inference/
[^2]: Amazon Web Services, “Amazon EC2 Trn3 UltraServers,” https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2025/12/amazon-ec2-trn3-ultraservers/
[^3]: Anthropic, “Anthropic and Amazon expand collaboration,” long-duration AWS compute commitment, https://www.anthropic.com/news/anthropic-amazon-compute?lang=us
[^4]: Microsoft, “Maia 200: the AI accelerator built for inference,” 2026-01-26, https://blogs.microsoft.com/blog/2026/01/26/maia-200-the-ai-accelerator-built-for-inference/
[^5]: Meta Engineering, “MTIA 300,” 2026-08-24, https://engineering.fb.com/2026/08/24/networking-traffic/mtia-300-meta-training-chip-built-in-nics/

# Part II · Chapter 13: Anchor Compute Buyers — How Long-Duration Demand Pulls Infrastructure Forward

## 1. Frontier model demand can pull infrastructure forward

At small scale, a model company is a compute tenant. At frontier scale, durable demand can become a financing input for chips, data centers, land, and power.

The relationship therefore changes from:

> model company → rent existing compute

toward:

> **model-company commitment → revenue visibility → financing certainty → earlier cloud / data-center / power / chip buildout**

The analytical question is not whether a model company "owns the stack." It is whether its demand is large and durable enough to change when upstream capacity gets financed, reserved, and built.

## 2. Demand certainty becomes a capital-formation input

The mechanism can be written as:

> **long-duration model demand → infrastructure-financing certainty → earlier data-center/power buildout → earlier chip/HBM/packaging reservation → priority capacity for anchor workloads**

Model-company demand has therefore become an upstream signal for capital formation, not merely a passive consequence of hyperscaler investment.

## 3. OpenAI / Stargate: delivery state matters more than headline scale

OpenAI announced Stargate in January 2025, with a plan to invest **US$500B over four years** in U.S. AI infrastructure.[^1]

The long-run objective was later described as securing **10 GW** of U.S. AI infrastructure by 2029.[^2]

The status moved further forward in 2026. On **April 29, 2026**, OpenAI said Stargate had **surpassed the 10 GW secured-capacity milestone** and had added more than 3 GW in the prior 90 days.[^2] On **June 1, 2026**, the **1 GW The Barn** data-center campus in Michigan broke ground.[^3]

These states must remain separate:

> commitment / secured capacity / construction / energized capacity / utilization

More than 10 GW secured does not mean more than 10 GW is already energized and operating.

### 3.1 PORTS-Pike: anchor demand now reaches LPS and credit support

On August 17, 2026, NVIDIA disclosed its role in the PORTS-Pike Technology Campus with SB Energy in Ohio. NVIDIA is providing credit support to secure land, power, and shell (LPS) capacity; the initial deployment is designed for **4.25 IT-GW** of AI-factory capacity. OpenAI is the customer, while SB Energy will build, own, and operate the data center under a **20-year lease** to OpenAI. Phased capacity is planned to begin coming online in **2028**.[^5]

The significance is not only the scale. The transaction structure has changed:

> **Anchor model demand + system-platform credit support + infrastructure developer + long-duration lease**

This shows a system seller participating directly in the bridge between a model company's demand/credit profile and long-duration physical-infrastructure finance.

The state boundaries must remain explicit:

- 4.25 IT-GW is initial deployment **design capacity**, not 2026 energized load;
- future PORTS-Pike capacity is buildout, not current active compute;
- a 20-year lease improves revenue visibility but also raises customer-concentration and duration risk;
- secured land / power / shell does not prove final compute utilization.

On August 26, 2026, NVIDIA management further said on the FY27 Q2 earnings call that OpenAI's existing and planned NVIDIA Compute commitments total approximately **12 GW**.[^6] This is platform-side management commentary about demand visibility. It is not evidence that OpenAI already has 12 GW of energized capacity.

The relationship between PORTS-Pike's **4.25 IT-GW** and OpenAI's previously disclosed **>10 GW secured capacity** is not stated in public materials; this report neither adds the two figures nor assumes a subordination relationship.

---

## 4. Anthropic / AWS: anchor workload can accelerate a second stack

Anthropic disclosed in April 2026 that:[^4]

- its future ten-year commitment to AWS technologies is **more than US$100B**;
- it can access **up to 5 GW** of capacity;
- it is already using **more than 1M Trainium2 chips**;
- by the end of 2026 it expects close to **1 GW of Trainium2/3 capacity**.

Model-company demand can therefore become an important anchor workload for cloud custom silicon.

The public disclosure establishes scale. It does not establish a "largest customer" ranking without a disclosed workload-share denominator.

### 4.1 xAI / Colossus: the self-built variant

xAI is the case of a model company entering infrastructure construction directly. In its December 2024 first-party update, xAI said **Colossus** had become fully operational in **122 days** using **100,000 NVIDIA Hopper GPUs**, and that it planned to double the cluster to a combined **200,000 Hopper GPUs**.[^7] The case therefore shows a model company directly organizing large-scale compute infrastructure rather than relying only on pre-existing cloud capacity.

This path is more direct than Stargate or Anthropic/AWS: rather than renting capacity through a cloud or a joint venture, the model company itself drove front-loaded capital formation in land, power, and chips. Anchor Buyers can therefore guide upstream capital formation through more than one mode — long-duration leased commitments and self-built buildouts can coexist.

The xAI case is subject to the same state boundaries: Colossus going online is not full utilization, and an expansion plan is not installed capacity.

## 5. Anchor Buyers gain coordination power, not ownership

A model company can influence:

- demand certainty;
- chip / system selection;
- cloud roadmaps;
- rack deployment;
- power planning;
- infrastructure financing.

But it does not normally own the leading-edge foundry, HBM fab, advanced-packaging fab, or transmission grid.

Anchor Buyer power is therefore an increase in demand-side bargaining and coordination—not full supply-chain ownership.

## 6. Long-duration commitments create switching costs

A model company chooses a full system composed of accelerator, HBM, interconnect, compiler/runtime, scheduler, rack/pod, and cloud operations.

Migration can require kernel adaptation, distributed-training changes, performance tuning, observability rework, and operational revalidation.

That creates infrastructure switching costs and helps explain why exit costs from long-duration capacity commitments can be much higher than those of ordinary SaaS contracts.

## 7. Anchor demand still depends on workload economics

If architecture sharply lowers the compute required for the same capability, a model company can reduce long-duration capacity commitments, improve buyer bargaining power, and increase utilization risk for infrastructure providers.

If lower unit cost stimulates workload volume even faster, long-duration commitments can continue to expand.

Anchor Buyer Demand is therefore a dynamic demand function, not a fixed exponential curve.

## 8. Anchor demand propagates through the infrastructure chain

Large-scale model demand can propagate across the full chain:

> **model demand → cloud CapEx / custom silicon → accelerator / HBM / packaging → data center → power → grid / generation**

AI-infrastructure analysis therefore has to look beyond:

- NVIDIA revenue;
- hyperscaler CapEx;
- foundry / memory CapEx.

It must also track independently:

- model-company long-duration contracts;
- secured capacity;
- project construction;
- utilization;
- commitment rescheduling.

If Anchor Buyer commitments keep expanding and are absorbed by real workloads, the capital-formation force behind S2/S3 strengthens.

If commitments are delayed, reduced, renegotiated, or underutilized, S4 risk rises.

---

## 9. Risk: concentration and duration mismatch

Anchor Buyers improve financing certainty, but they also concentrate risk.

If a large infrastructure project depends on a small number of model-company workloads, capital recovery becomes more exposed to those customers' future:

- revenue;
- financing;
- technical roadmap;
- workload growth;
- contract performance.

At the same time, AI hardware has a relatively short technology cycle while:

- data-center shells;
- power contracts;
- grid interconnections;
- generation assets

have much longer economic lives.

That creates a structural mismatch:

> **shorter-cycle AI workload / compute technology vs longer-cycle infrastructure obligation**

If the model-company growth thesis fails, a long-duration commitment can move from a financing anchor to a stranded-capacity risk.

---

## 10. Monitoring Anchor-Buyer strength

### Strengthening signals

- long-duration compute commitments continue to expand;
- secured capacity grows;
- dedicated campus / power projects move into construction;
- custom silicon receives more anchor workloads;
- utilization and workload revenue grow together;
- commitments are renewed or extended.

### Weakening signals

- project rescheduling;
- commitment renegotiation;
- cancellation;
- utilization below plan;
- model-company financing or revenue pressure;
- capacity delivery materially outrunning workload growth.

Headline commitment is therefore not the endpoint.

The decisive question is:

> **Does long-duration demand ultimately convert into high-utilization, monetizable production workload?**

---

## 11. Current read

As of August 2026, large model companies constitute a distinct Anchor Buyer category inside AI infrastructure.

OpenAI Stargate's secured-capacity and construction states, Anthropic/AWS's ten-year compute commitment and Trainium deployment, xAI's self-built Colossus buildout, and PORTS-Pike's LPS/credit-support structure show that model workloads are now shaping:

> **cloud architecture, custom silicon, data-center buildout, power procurement, and infrastructure finance**

Anchor Buyer commitments are therefore a key leading indicator connecting **S2/S3 expansion** with **S4 capital clearing**. The time-leadership of this signal is supported so far by three documented cases (Stargate, Anthropic/AWS, and xAI Colossus) and must be re-verified across further full cycles.

They can accelerate AI-infrastructure capital formation, but under weak utilization they can also amplify stranded-capacity and duration-mismatch risk.

## 12. What would change the view

The Anchor Compute Buyer Effect should be downgraded if:

- long-duration commitments are broadly delayed, reduced, or renegotiated;
- secured capacity fails for extended periods to become energized / utilized capacity;
- anchor-workload utilization remains persistently weak;
- model-company monetization cannot cover long-duration infrastructure obligations;
- project finance becomes increasingly dependent on stronger seller support rather than customer / third-party cash flow.

Conversely, larger headline contract values or additional secured GW should not, by themselves, strengthen this chapter's thesis without delivery and utilization evidence.

## References

[^1]: OpenAI, “Announcing The Stargate Project,” 2025-01, https://openai.com/index/announcing-the-stargate-project/
[^2]: OpenAI, “Building the compute infrastructure for the Intelligence Age,” 2026-04-29, surpassed 10 GW secured-capacity milestone, https://openai.com/index/building-the-compute-infrastructure-for-the-intelligence-age/
[^3]: OpenAI, “Stargate Michigan Data Center,” 2026-06-01, 1 GW The Barn campus groundbreaking, https://openai.com/index/stargate-michigan-data-center/
[^4]: Anthropic, “Anthropic and Amazon expand collaboration,” 2026-04, >US$100B over ten years, up to 5 GW, and >1M Trainium2 chips, https://www.anthropic.com/news/anthropic-amazon-compute?lang=us
[^5]: NVIDIA, “NVIDIA Guarantees SB Energy's PORTS-Pike Technology Campus in Ohio to Exclusively Host NVIDIA AI Compute,” 2026-08-17, initial 4.25 IT-GW design capacity, LPS credit support, 20-year OpenAI lease, and phased online plan beginning in 2028, https://investor.nvidia.com/news/press-release-details/2026/NVIDIA-Guarantees-SB-Energys-PORTS-Pike-Technology-Campus-in-Ohio-to-Exclusively-Host-NVIDIA-AI-Compute/default.aspx

# Part II · Chapter 14: Agent-Native Software and AI Software Unit Economics

## 1. The unit of software economics moves from seat to workflow

Traditional SaaS can often add seats without proportional variable compute. Agent-native software cannot assume that cost structure.

A single workflow may trigger retrieval, reasoning, multiple model calls, tool invocation, browser/action execution, retries, and human review.

The cost unit therefore shifts from:

> **account / seat**

toward:

> **workflow execution**

## 1A. 2026 baseline: AI is broad; agent deployment is still early

Stanford AI Index 2026 reports that **88%** of surveyed organizations say they use AI and **70%** use Generative AI in at least one business function. Yet AI-agent deployment remains in the **single digits across nearly all business functions**.[^3]

The correct reading is not a fixed penetration forecast. It is a stage-of-market distinction:

> **Broad AI adoption has already occurred, while production-grade autonomous or semi-autonomous workflow deployment is still early.**

That creates a large potential workflow pool, but production reliability, review burden, integration cost, and unit economics have not yet been proven at the same scale.

Anthropic's June 2026 Economic Index also reports that its product usage has shifted toward more long-running agentic tasks and that higher compute use is associated with higher estimated artifact value.[^4] Because this is first-party product telemetry, it should not be treated as representative of the entire enterprise-software market.

## 1B. AI Inheritance Effect: IT is the installed base

Moving a physical industrial process into the digital domain usually requires sensors, networks, databases, software, identity systems, process digitization, and organizational change. A highly digitized organization starts from a different position: ERP, CRM, MES, CAD/PLM, databases, cloud systems, APIs, digital documents, source code, identity/access systems, and workflow logs already form a reusable digital substrate.

This report defines the structural advantage created when **AI can add an intelligence layer on top of existing digital assets and workflows without rebuilding the full digital foundation** as the **AI Inheritance Effect**.

The demand transmission is:

> **Digital Installed Base → AI Overlay → Workflow Penetration → Automation Depth → Workload Volume → Infrastructure Demand**

Long-run AI-infrastructure demand therefore does not depend only on whether individual models keep scaling upward. It also depends on **how many existing digital workflows begin invoking AI and how deeply each workflow moves from assistant features toward semi-autonomous or autonomous execution**.

This mechanism is compatible with Algorithmic Efficiency:

- compute / memory required per task can keep falling;
- the number of workflows for which AI becomes economical can expand;
- execution frequency and agent depth inside each workflow can rise;
- aggregate infrastructure demand therefore depends on whether **unit resource intensity falls faster or slower than workflow penetration and execution volume rise**.

### Boundary: lower Digital → AI friction does not mean zero-friction deployment

Existing digital infrastructure can reduce marginal deployment friction, but production-grade AI can still be constrained by:

- data quality / access;
- legacy integration;
- identity / permission;
- security / privacy;
- compliance / responsibility;
- workflow redesign;
- reliability / observability;
- human review;
- unit economics.

Physical AI and industrial processes that are not yet digitized still require sensorization, control integration, qualification, safety engineering, and field modification.

The more defensible statement is therefore:

> **Relative to Industrial → Digital transformation, highly digitized organizations generally face lower marginal transformation cost when moving from Digital → AI. AI can inherit the IT installed base, but it cannot skip production integration or responsibility constraints.**

## 2. There is no universal AI-software margin structure

Variable cost differs by product and depends on model choice, context length, caching, retrieval, tools/APIs, failure/retry rates, human review, pricing model, and task depth.

This report therefore does not treat a fixed path such as "traditional SaaS 85% → AI SaaS 55%" as fact.

It uses **AI Software Unit Economics** instead.

## 3. Core unit-economics identity

A production workflow can be approximated as:

> **Contribution Margin = Revenue / Productivity Value per workflow − Variable AI cost − Tools/APIs − Human review**

Variable AI cost includes at least:

- inference;
- retrieval / storage;
- model routing;
- retries;
- observability;
- failure handling.

The optimization target is **cost per successfully completed workflow**, not merely cost per token.

## 4. Agent depth changes the cost curve

A shallow assistant may look like:

> single prompt → single response

A deeper agent looks more like:

> plan → retrieve → reason → tool call → inspect → retry → finalize

As loop depth grows, compute, tool usage, and the failure surface all expand.

Agent products therefore have to optimize:

- task success;
- loop length;
- model routing;
- caching;
- tool reliability;
- failure / retry rate.

## 5. Seat pricing comes under pressure

Seat pricing can remain effective for summarization, autocomplete, and shallow-assistant products.

If one seat can launch research, coding, support, sales, and operations workflows with very different depths, compute variance becomes much larger.

Long-run pricing can therefore shift toward combinations of:

- usage;
- credits;
- workflow;
- outcome;
- hybrid models.

This does not imply that seat pricing disappears. It means agent depth weakens the correlation between a seat and real variable cost.

## 6. Multi-model routing becomes infrastructure

Sending every task to the most expensive model directly damages unit economics.

A more efficient system design can use:

- cheap / local models for classification, extraction, and high-volume routine work;
- medium models for routine reasoning;
- frontier models for complex tasks and high-uncertainty decisions;
- specialist/custom models for stable professional workloads.

Routing is a dynamic allocation problem across quality, latency, cost, and failure probability.

## 7. Open source can move profit toward workflow and distribution

Open-source models increase portability, pressure API premiums, expand private deployment, and improve buyer bargaining power.

Model capability itself therefore becomes harder to preserve as permanent software margin.

More durable value can move toward:

- proprietary workflow;
- domain data / context;
- distribution;
- integration;
- trust / compliance;
- measurable outcomes.

## 8. System-stack competition can transmit into application cost

Software companies usually do not purchase HBM, CoWoS, or grid capacity directly.

Cloud pricing, however, embeds accelerator, memory, networking, data-center, and power costs.

If hyperscaler custom stacks lower real system TCO, part of that benefit can transmit over time through instance or API pricing into the application layer.

Infrastructure competition around Google Ironwood TPU, AWS Trainium3, and other systems therefore affects software contribution margin as well as the chip market.[^1][^2]

## 8A. Workflow elasticity: lower unit cost does not imply lower total demand

Once agent software turns model calls into workflow execution, aggregate infrastructure demand can be approximated as:

```text
Total AI Infrastructure Demand
≈ Workflow Count
× Attempts per Workflow
× Resource Intensity per Attempt
```

Lower model prices or more efficient routing reduce only part of this expression.

If lower cost also produces:

- more users;
- longer agent loops;
- more tool calls;
- deeper retry / exploration;
- automation of workflows that were previously uneconomic;

then total compute / memory / network demand can still rise.

The software layer's feedback into infrastructure is therefore better summarized as:

> **cost per verified workflow × demand elasticity**

This is why agent adoption can compress model-layer margin while still expanding aggregate use of cloud, accelerators, memory, and networking.

## 9. Human review is a variable-cost line

In high-risk workflows such as legal, finance, and enterprise operations, human review may remain necessary for a long time.

If agent-generation speed rises 10× but review burden does not fall, labor has simply moved from production to verification.

True automation ROI should include:

> **Human time before − Human time after − new review / exception-handling time**

rather than measuring AI usage or generated tokens alone.

## 10. Sources of durable software profit

Products with stronger long-run economics are more likely to combine:

- high workflow completion;
- low variable AI cost;
- low retry / review burden;
- strong proprietary context;
- distribution;
- measurable customer ROI;
- the ability to route across models and providers.

Software competition is therefore moving from feature competition toward **workflow economics competition**.

## 11. Current read

AI is moving part of software from low-marginal-cost replication toward real-time compute-consuming workflow systems.

This can compress some traditional software margins, but it does not imply that all software becomes a low-margin business.

The dividing line is:

> **who can convert model calls into repeatable, verifiable, low-failure, monetizable business outcomes while keeping workflow-demand growth inside sustainable unit economics.**

## References

[^1]: Google Cloud, “Ironwood TPU,” https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/infrastructure-and-cloud/google-cloud/ironwood-tpu-age-of-inference/
[^2]: Amazon Web Services, “Amazon EC2 Trn3 UltraServers,” https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2025/12/amazon-ec2-trn3-ultraservers/
[^3]: Stanford HAI, “2026 AI Index Report — Economy,” organizational AI adoption, Generative AI use, and early-stage agent deployment, https://hai.stanford.edu/ai-index/2026-ai-index-report/economy
[^4]: Anthropic, “Anthropic Economic Index report: Cadences,” 2026-06-26, long-running agentic tasks and the relationship between compute use and estimated artifact value; first-party product sample, https://www.anthropic.com/research/economic-index-june-2026-report

# Part II · Chapter 15: Physical AI and the Real Bottlenecks in Robotics

## 1. Robotics is a system-closure problem

Physical AI has to close perception, planning, control, actuation, power, safety, and mechanical reliability at the same time.

A robot's productive value therefore cannot be inferred from model capability or edge TOPS alone.

A complete system has to close:

> **model capability × sensor quality × control latency × actuator reliability × energy efficiency × manufacturing economics**

## 2. Cloud training and edge control live in different physical regimes

Large-scale simulation, policy training, and foundation-model training can run in data centers.

Real-time control is constrained by latency, thermals, battery, sensor loops, and safety deadlines.

Many Physical AI systems therefore naturally follow:

> **cloud-side heavy training + edge-side compressed inference/control**

A roughly 120 kW-class data-center rack and a robot's W/Wh-level power budget belong to completely different physical pools.[^4]

## 3. Robot BOM is form-factor specific

Humanoids, AMRs, industrial arms, autonomous vehicles, and drones have very different actuator, reducer, sensor, compute, and battery cost structures.

This report therefore does not treat fixed templates such as "actuators 35% / sensors 20% / compute 25%" as industry fact.

The real task is to identify the **scarce component** for each robot form factor.

## 4. Mechanical precision can remain scarce after compute commoditizes

The software narrative can easily understate mechanical bottlenecks such as:

- frameless torque motors;
- harmonic reducers;
- planetary roller screws;
- bearings;
- precision gearboxes.

The key variables include durability, backlash, repeatability, noise, yield, maintenance, and cost.

The industry can therefore reach a state where:

> **compute gets cheaper while high-precision mechanical components remain system bottlenecks.**

## 5. Sensor quality determines whether model capability becomes control

Robotic systems may require cameras, depth sensing, force/torque sensors, tactile sensors, IMUs, encoders, and other feedback channels.

Model capability becomes stable control only when calibration, latency, robustness, and failure detection are strong enough.

The strategic value of the sensor stack is therefore not just BOM share. It is closed-loop reliability.

## 6. Edge-platform advantage is workload-specific

NVIDIA Jetson Thor, Qualcomm, and other edge accelerators compete across:[^1][^2]

- compute;
- software;
- power efficiency;
- developer ecosystem.

An edge system does not need to replicate data-center rack architecture.

But a platform that spans cloud training, simulation, and edge deployment can create cross-layer cloud-to-robot ecosystem lock-in.

## 7. Physical deployment can create more cloud workload

Even if one robot uses modest edge compute, a large fleet continuously generates:

- video / sensor data;
- simulation workloads;
- retraining;
- synthetic data;
- fleet analytics;
- model updates.

The feedback loop can be:

> **physical deployment → data generation → retraining → more cloud compute**

Robot adoption therefore does not necessarily reduce data-center demand. It can create new training and inference workloads.

### 7.1 The robot becomes a new memory / storage demand pool

Micron's June 24, 2026 investor presentation provides a useful quantitative anchor that must retain its identity as a **company forward-looking view**:[^3]

- L2+ and above vehicles carry more than **5×** the memory and storage content of an average vehicle;
- Micron states that humanoid robots carry roughly **10×** the memory content of an average L2+ vehicle;
- the company expects a sustained, multi-decade robotics memory-demand cycle to begin in the latter part of this decade.

This is not industry-wide actual shipment data and it does not establish a universal "hundreds of GB of DRAM + several TB of SSD per robot" configuration.

What it supports is the mechanism:

> **Physical AI commercial scale ↑ → edge memory/storage content ↑ + fleet-generated cloud workload ↑**

Robotics can therefore become both a new edge-memory demand pool and a new source of cloud retraining / storage demand.

### 7.2 Robotics is both an AI demand source and an industrial-electronics demand pool

Physical AI is not only a new source of AI-compute demand.

A robot also depends on:

- MCU / control electronics;
- sensors;
- motor-drive / power electronics;
- industrial memory / storage;
- edge accelerators;
- networking.

It can therefore face two upstream constraint sets at once:

> **industrial-electronics supply pressure + edge-AI compute / memory pressure**

This is why robotics deployment cannot be inferred from model capability alone. Even if cloud training and edge models improve rapidly, lead times and supply continuity in MCU, sensors, actuator electronics, qualified memory, or other industrial components can still constrain mass deployment.

The reverse attribution error must also be avoided: an industrial-component shortage is not automatically caused by AI. Attribution still requires the shared-resource, product-mix, or allocation evidence defined in Chapters 3 and 21.

---

## 8. The robot-side power wall is battery / thermal

The data-center power bottleneck is grid / interconnection.

The robot-side bottleneck is battery energy density, runtime, charging, thermal constraints, and peak actuator demand.

Physical AI should therefore be evaluated through:

> **Useful task output / Wh**

alongside continuous runtime, recharge time, thermal headroom, maintenance intervals, and field reliability—not TOPS alone.

## 9. The mass-production threshold is manufacturing

A demo can tolerate expensive parts, manual calibration, low yield, and frequent maintenance.

Mass production cannot.

Scale thresholds include component yield, assembly time, field failure rate, maintenance interval, supply-chain stability, and unit economics.

The industrial inflection point for Physical AI therefore has to be demonstrated through manufacturing and deployment data, not demonstration videos.

## 9A. The commercial threshold is capital payback

Once a robot moves from demo to production, the relevant economic test is not only whether it can complete a task.

A minimal relationship is:

```text
Economic Output
≈ Useful Tasks per Hour
× Uptime
× Deployment Scale
- Energy
- Maintenance
- Human Supervision
- Financing / Depreciation
```

A robot can therefore be technically capable and still fail to produce sustainable ROI if:

- utilization is low;
- downtime is high;
- maintenance intervals are short;
- human supervision remains intensive;
- replacement / component failure is frequent;
- upfront CapEx is too high.

Physical AI and data-center AI therefore share one capital rule:

> **capability must ultimately be validated through utilization and cash flow.**

The difference is that data-center bottlenecks are concentrated more heavily in compute / HBM / power, while robotics adds actuators, batteries, mechanical reliability, field service, and safety.

## 10. China's Physical-AI stack differs from its frontier-AI stack

Physical AI depends heavily on motors, batteries, sensors, manufacturing, consumer-electronics supply chains, and system integration.

Those layers do not perfectly overlap with the HBM / advanced-packaging / leading-edge compute closure required for frontier AI infrastructure.

China can therefore have stronger competitiveness in robot systems and mechanical supply chains than in some frontier-AI infrastructure layers.

This is a separate industrial path and should not be reduced to one "AI chip capability" metric.

## 11. Current read

Long-run value capture can appear simultaneously in:

- edge compute / software platforms;
- actuators / reducers;
- sensors;
- batteries / power electronics;
- manufacturing / integration;
- cloud simulation / training infrastructure.

The robotics revolution is not fundamentally a contest over which model tops a benchmark.

It is a contest over:

> **who can convert AI capability into reliable, low-cost, mass-producible, maintainable physical work with sufficiently high utilization to recover capital.**

## References

[^1]: NVIDIA Developer, Isaac platform, simulation/training and robotics workflow, https://developer.nvidia.com/isaac
[^2]: NVIDIA, 2026-06, Isaac GR00T Reference Humanoid Robot, Jetson Thor onboard compute and Isaac GR00T platform, https://nvidianews.nvidia.com/news/nvidia-open-humanoid-robot-reference-design
[^3]: Micron Technology, investor presentation, 2026-06-24, humanoid-robot memory-content outlook, https://s25.q4cdn.com/621799436/files/doc_financials/2026/q3/Micron_Q3_26_Earnings_Deck.pdf
[^4]: NVIDIA, DGX GB200 Hardware Guide, rack power approximately 120 kW, https://docs.nvidia.com/dgx/dgxgb200-user-guide/hardware.html

# Part II · Chapter 16: AMD, Intel, Qualcomm, and Apple — Differentiated Competitive Paths

## 1. Competitors attack different layers of the stack

AMD, Intel, Qualcomm, and Apple attack different layers of the system stack and operate with different manufacturing access, workload bases, distribution channels, and capital structures.

The useful question is not whether they form one anti-NVIDIA bloc. It is:

> **Which source of system-stack power can each company realistically erode?**

## 2. AMD: the most direct data-center system challenger

AMD Instinct is one of the most direct alternatives in data-center acceleration. AMD's current official roadmap also organizes Instinct GPUs, EPYC CPUs, Pensando networking, and ROCm into rack/system-level solutions.[^1][^2]

The real threshold is not GPU design alone. It also includes:

- ROCm / software maturity;
- scale-up / scale-out;
- HBM;
- advanced packaging;
- system reference architecture;
- customer migration / production support.

AMD's competitive unit has therefore also moved from chip to system.

## 3. AMD is exposed to the same scarce-capacity access problem

AMD and NVIDIA compete for many of the same advanced-logic, HBM, and packaging resources.

When NVIDIA demand is exceptionally strong, AMD's challenge is not only market share. It is also access to incremental scarce capacity.

If HBM / packaging supply elasticity rises, the constraints on AMD system expansion fall.

AMD is therefore a useful observable for **non-anchor-buyer access / residual capacity outside the leading buyer**, without requiring a new bespoke core concept.

## 4. Intel: product clock and foundry clock run on different timelines

Intel simultaneously carries CPU/product competition, accelerator efforts, foundry strategy, and domestic-manufacturing policy. Intel's 2025 Form 10-K and its 2026 VLSI Symposium update show that Intel Foundry continues to pursue an external-foundry strategy, with 18A in production and 18A-P in risk production.[^3][^4]

In July 2026, ASML and Intel also confirmed that High-NA EUV had entered real high-volume logic production on selected Intel 18A layers.[^9]

The Product Clock requires short-cycle delivery of performance, software, and customer adoption.

The Foundry Clock requires long-duration progress in process technology, yield, customer trust, packaging, and high-volume execution.

Intel's recovery cannot therefore be proved by one product, one process milestone, or one fab. High-NA HVM is a meaningful manufacturing-execution signal, while external-foundry adoption, yield economics, and customer trust still require separate validation.

## 4A. Historical memory: concentration changes cadence control, not engineering causality

Intel's client-CPU history is useful only as a bounded comparison. It should not be rewritten as “dominance automatically causes toothpaste-style stagnation.”

Intel's own 2016–2017 annual reports disclosed a lengthening process-technology cadence across 14nm and 10nm, with multiple product waves planned on the same node. Its 2018 Form 10-K then stated that 10nm volume production had been delayed from the second half of 2018 into 2019.[^10][^11]

The defensible lesson is:

> **When one technical/manufacturing path carries most of the upgrade cadence, process execution, architecture cadence, and customer replacement timing become more tightly coupled to that roadmap.**

It does **not** prove that market concentration caused Intel's 10nm delay. The filing itself identifies manufacturing, yield, and implementation challenges; engineering causality cannot be replaced with a market-structure story.

Competition also returned through new paths rather than simply making the incumbent's roadmap accelerate internally. AMD launched the first Zen-based Ryzen desktop processors in **2017**; Apple launched M1 in **2020** as the first chip designed specifically for Mac, creating a different vertically integrated client-compute path.[^12][^13]

The useful institutional memory is therefore:

> **When an executable second system is weak, the incumbent has greater power to define upgrade cadence. When real competition returns, it often arrives through a different architecture / manufacturing / system path.**

The analogy to 2026 AI infrastructure is about **migration cost and system definition**, not today's product cadence:

- late Intel client computing combined a mature market with process delay;
- the 2026 NVIDIA data-center stack combines explosive demand, rapid roadmaps, and scarce-capacity allocation;
- the former is closer to “thin upgrade cadence”;
- the latter is currently closer to “buyers want the next generation but face migration and quantity-assurance constraints.”

This report therefore does **not** call current AI scarcity “another Intel toothpaste era.”

The longer-run risk is narrower:

> **If production-grade second system stacks fail to emerge for many years, today's control over scarce-layer access and migration could eventually become greater control over the cadence of progress itself.**

If AMD, hyperscaler custom systems, open/runtime portability, China's parallel alternatives, or other production-grade second paths scale materially, the historical analogy weakens.

---

## 5. Qualcomm: edge substitution changes cloud demand

Qualcomm's relative strengths are closer to smartphones, PCs, edge AI, low-power compute, and connectivity. Its 2026 materials continue to emphasize local agentic AI on Snapdragon X Series PCs and on-device generative AI on mobile platforms.[^5][^6]

It does not need to reproduce NVL72 to capture AI value.

If on-device model capability, memory efficiency, and local inference continue improving, more workloads can move from cloud to device, reducing part of cloud-inference demand.

Qualcomm's primary effect on AI infrastructure is therefore **demand-function restructuring**, not direct substitution for frontier training systems.

## 6. Apple: vertical integration changes workload placement

Apple vertically integrates silicon, operating systems, devices, application ecosystems, distribution, and a massive installed base. Apple Intelligence publicly combines on-device processing with Private Cloud Compute, making workload placement a core part of its AI system design.[^7][^8]

Apple is not a frontier-training system vendor, but it can influence where large volumes of consumer workloads execute: on device, in private cloud, or in public cloud.

If on-device capability improves rapidly, Apple becomes an important platform variable affecting cloud-inference intensity.

## 7. Leading-edge access does not justify invented customer rankings

Apple has long used leading-edge process technology, but without first-party customer-share or ranking disclosures, this report does not treat a specific-year "largest/second-largest customer" identity as fact.

The important forward variables are whether leading-node launch scale remains strong, whether AI/HPC absorbs more incremental capacity, and whether device-AI strategy changes wafer / memory mix.

Historical revenue share is not a substitute for layer-specific analysis of incremental scarce-capacity access.

## 8. Competitive pressure is multi-directional

**Table 16.1 — Four differentiated competitive paths**

| Company | Primary competitive direction |
|---|---|
| AMD | Data-center accelerator / system |
| Intel | CPU + accelerator + foundry |
| Qualcomm | edge / PC / mobile AI |
| Apple | vertically integrated on-device AI / workload placement |

**Source:** AMD, Intel, Qualcomm, and Apple official company/product materials.[^1][^3][^5][^7]  
**Note:** The table classifies competitive paths; it does not imply that the four companies operate in the same market or have directly comparable market shares.

NVIDIA therefore faces multi-layer erosion rather than one unified opponent.

## 9. What would raise S1 weight

- AMD scales a production system stack;
- Intel improves foundry and product execution together, increasing manufacturing/system diversity;
- Qualcomm / Apple move more high-volume inference toward the edge;
- open software / interfaces lower migration cost;
- HBM / packaging supply becomes looser.

These changes can raise S1 relative to S2, but S1 and S2 are parallel conditional states rather than sequential stages.

## 10. Current read

The incumbent semiconductor companies have neither disappeared nor formed a unified alliance.

A more realistic structure is: AMD competes directly at the data-center system level; Intel is attempting to repair both product and manufacturing clocks; Qualcomm and Apple reshape part of the demand function from the edge and device layers.

Whether they materially weaken dominant system-stack economics depends on production workload share, software portability, scarce-resource access, and customer migration—not on product-launch count.

## References

[^1]: AMD, Instinct / Helios official page, rack-scale solution integrating Instinct GPUs, EPYC CPUs, Pensando networking, and ROCm, https://www.amd.com/en/products/accelerators/instinct.html
[^2]: AMD, “ROCm 10: A Simpler Path to Production AI on AMD,” 2026-08-27, https://www.amd.com/en/blogs/2026/amd-rocm-10-a-simpler-path-to-production-ai-on-amd.html
[^3]: Intel Corporation, 2025 Form 10-K, Intel Foundry and manufacturing strategy, https://www.intc.com/filings-reports/all-sec-filings/content/0000050863-26-000011/intc-20251227.htm
[^4]: Intel Foundry, 2026-06 VLSI Symposium process milestones, 18A production and 18A-P risk production, https://newsroom.intel.com/intel-foundry/intel-foundry-details-process-milestones-future-innovation-at-vlsi-symposium
[^5]: Qualcomm, 2026-08, agentic AI applications on Snapdragon X Series PCs, https://www.qualcomm.com/news/onq/2026/08/agentic-ai-apps-snapdragon-x-series
[^6]: Qualcomm, Mobile AI / on-device generative AI, https://www.qualcomm.com/smartphones/features/mobile-ai

[^11]: Intel Corporation, 2018 Form 10-K, 10nm volume-production delay from 2H18 into 2019 and process-execution risks, https://www.intc.com/filings-reports/all-sec-filings/content/0000050863-19-000007/a12292018q4-10kdocument.htm
[^12]: AMD, 2017-03-02, first Ryzen desktop processors based on the new Zen core architecture, https://www.amd.com/en/newsroom/press-releases/2017-3-2-amd-ryzen-tm-7-desktop-processors-featuring-recor.html
[^13]: Apple, 2020-11-10, M1 as the first chip designed specifically for Mac, https://www.apple.com/newsroom/2020/11/apple-unleashes-m1/

# Part II · Chapter 17: China's Parallel Stack — Production-Grade Substitution vs. Autonomous Frontier Closure

## 1. The analytical error is collapsing three different thresholds into one

China's technology position cannot be represented by a single localization rate.

Three distinct questions have to remain separate:

1. **Can domestic consumer-computing systems ship at meaningful scale?**
2. **Can PC / workstation systems sustain software compatibility, performance, and commercial scale?**
3. **Can a frontier AI stack operate production workloads and continue advancing when critical external inputs are constrained?**

Evidence from one threshold does not close another. A strong smartphone supply chain is not proof of frontier-AI closure; frontier-AI constraints are not proof that China lacks semiconductor substitution capacity.

For frontier AI, this report uses two tests:

> **Production-Grade Parallel Stack:** can the stack run real workloads with acceptable performance, scale, portability, supply continuity, reliability, utilization, and economics?

> **Autonomous Frontier Closure:** can that stack keep scaling across generations when critical external manufacturing, equipment, materials, software, or service inputs are constrained?

The distinction is strategic. Production-grade substitution can become economically important before full autonomous closure exists.

## 2. Consumer computing: commercial substitution can precede manufacturing autonomy

Smartphones and parts of consumer computing can form a more complete domestic chain because they can draw on local SoCs, foundries, LPDDR/NAND, packaging, device manufacturing, operating-system/application ecosystems, and large domestic demand.

TSMC's 2025 platform mix still shows smartphone at **29%** of annual platform revenue and HPC at **58%**.[^1]

### 2.1 Shipment scale is broader than one vendor

Counterpoint Research's 2026 Q1 global smartphone AP-SoC shipment shares show:[^2]

- UNISOC: **14%**;
- HiSilicon: **4%**.

If commercial scale is measured by actual smartphone-SoC shipments, domestic supply is therefore not limited to Huawei.

Huawei / HiSilicon's more defensible distinction is that its **premium in-house device + SoC + software/ecosystem vertical loop is more mature**, not that it is the only domestic vendor with shipment scale.

Xiaomi has also moved beyond a pure demonstration stage. Xinhua reported in May 2025 that XRing O1 had entered mass production in the Xiaomi 15S Pro and Pad 7 Ultra.[^3] Reuters reported in August 2026 that O1 had powered **more than 1M devices**, while the next-generation O3 continues on TSMC 3 nm with projected initial device shipments of 200k–300k units.[^4]

The claim that Xiaomi has only "200k–300k scraps of capacity" or that the program is merely a branding exercise therefore does not enter the fact layer.

A better judgment is:

> **Xiaomi has achieved real production deployment, while multi-generation scale, modem/system integration, foundry dependency, and sustained shipment share still require validation.**

UNISOC represents a different commercial loop: much larger shipment scale in mass-market / entry-5G devices without proving premium-flagship system equivalence.

### 2.2 Design origin is not manufacturing closure

One additional supply-chain discipline is required here:

> **"Domestic design" does not mean "manufacturing autonomy," and shipment share cannot substitute for foundry continuity.**

UNISOC is a fabless IC-design company. Its public product materials confirm that multiple current 5G SoCs use **6nm EUV**; the official M6870 page goes further and explicitly identifies **TSMC 6nm EUV**.[^19] UNISOC / Spreadtrum also has a long first-party manufacturing history with TSMC: in 2011, TSMC disclosed that a Spreadtrum 40nm baseband processor had entered initial production at TSMC Fab 12.[^20]

This report does **not** extend those observations into the stronger claim that "all UNISOC products are manufactured by TSMC." The T9100 official page, for example, confirms a **6nm process** but does not itself identify the foundry.[^21] The safe conclusion is therefore:

> **UNISOC's 14% smartphone AP-SoC shipment share proves real commercial design and shipment scale, but it does not prove domestic foundry closure. Public product and historical supply-chain evidence show TSMC is an important manufacturing dependency, so foundry continuity must be evaluated as a separate gate.**

The Xiaomi XRing case is more direct: the Reuters source already cited in this chapter explicitly reports TSMC manufacturing cooperation.[^4] Xiaomi's in-house SoC program is therefore also a case where design capability is real but manufacturing autonomy cannot be inferred from the design origin.

This changes the consumer-computing test. Any future "domestic SoC share" should be decomposed into at least:

1. **design origin**;
2. **foundry identity / process continuity**;
3. **packaging / memory dependency**;
4. **exposure to external licensing, service, or equipment rules**;
5. **multi-generation manufacturing continuity**.

The January 2025 U.S. BIS foundry due-diligence rule did broaden licensing and due-diligence requirements for front-end foundries / packaging companies handling **certain advanced-computing ICs**, and introduced the Approved / Authorized IC Designer framework; the relevant advanced-node definition includes 16/14nm-and-below or non-planar logic.[^22] But this cannot be simplified into "all PRC-designed smartphone SoCs below 16/14nm automatically require a license." Applicability depends on EAR classification, performance/transistor-count conditions, designer status, destination, and transaction structure.

The defensible risk statement for UNISOC / Xiaomi is therefore:

> **External-foundry dependence increases supply-continuity and policy exposure, but without product-specific EAR classification and licensing evidence it cannot be rewritten as an already-occurring or inevitable cutoff.**

Consumer parallel-supply capability should therefore be evaluated as its own layer—not used as proof for or against frontier-AI closure.

## 3. PCs / workstations: compatibility and scale are the binding tests

PCs, workstations, and general computing require CPU/GPU hardware, DDR5, professional software, drivers/tooling, desktop ecosystems, and developer compatibility.

The claim that domestic PC CPUs/GPUs are "blank" is also too strong.

Loongson's current official portfolio includes desktop/general-purpose CPUs such as the 3A6000 and 3B6000.[^5] Moore Threads publicly sells the MTT S80 gaming GPU.[^6]

These facts establish **real products and deployable domestic PC/graphics systems**.

They do not establish:

- mainstream consumer-PC shipment share approaching Intel/AMD/NVIDIA;
- equivalent coverage of professional software / games;
- equivalent performance-per-watt, driver maturity, developer ecosystem, or supply scale.

The more accurate description is therefore:

> **the segment is not empty; products and domestic ecosystems exist, while mainstream scale, compatibility, and system economics remain the closure barriers.**

## 4. Frontier AI is a system-closure problem

Frontier AI requires multiple advanced layers to work simultaneously:

- advanced accelerators;
- HBM-class memory;
- advanced packaging;
- high-speed scale-up / scale-out interconnect;
- compiler/runtime;
- distributed training;
- rack-scale deployment;
- data-center power.

A breakthrough in one layer does not automatically create an equivalent system.

An accelerator without HBM, hardware with very high migration cost, or a cluster with weak utilization/interconnect scaling can all produce materially weaker system economics.

This report therefore does not treat "one domestic chip can run a model" as sufficient evidence of frontier substitution.

## 5. Ascend / CANN should be judged by system completeness, not peak-card metrics

The key value of a domestic accelerator stack is not the peak FLOPS of one generation.

It is whether compiler/runtime, framework adaptation, distributed communication, deployment tooling, and production-workload feedback form a closed loop.

The preferred observables include:

- large-cluster utilization;
- compiler stability;
- operator completeness;
- framework compatibility;
- failure recovery;
- multi-generation compatibility;
- production deployment scale.

Because some specific Ascend/CANN performance and migration-efficiency figures currently come primarily from secondary sources, this chapter does not upgrade unverified parameters into confirmed facts.

## 6. Memory: conventional scale and frontier HBM are different thresholds

CXMT's **May 27, 2026 STAR Market registration prospectus** discloses DDR and LPDDR products. The company subsequently began trading on Shanghai's STAR Market on **July 27, 2026**.[^7][^18]

Full-text searches of the relevant public offering / registration materials for `HBM` and the Chinese term for high-bandwidth memory do not provide evidence sufficient to confirm a volume HBM product line. This report therefore keeps one narrow conclusion:

> **Current public offering and product materials are insufficient to confirm a volume-deliverable HBM product line.**

This does **not** prove that the company has no HBM R&D. Listing changes the company's capital-market and financing state; it does not by itself cross the HBM, advanced-packaging, or frontier-system technical gates.

To move HBM from possible R&D into frontier-system supply, the evidence would need to include official products, customer qualification, volume production, bandwidth/capacity, and packaging integration.

The same prospectus also requires strict separation between project investment and planned offering proceeds. For the DRAM technology-upgrade project, **RMB18.0B total investment** and **RMB13.0B planned offering proceeds** are different denominators.[^7]

DDR/LPDDR supply expansion and HBM-class memory closure therefore have to remain separate.

### 6.1 Conventional DRAM / NAND can improve before HBM closure

CXMT's current official product page discloses DDR5 chips up to **8000 Mbps**, with 16Gb/24Gb densities and server/workstation/PC module coverage including RDIMM/MRDIMM.[^8]

YMTC officially describes itself as a 3D NAND IDM and discloses its Gen5/Xtacking 4.0 product path together with raw NAND, embedded memory, client SSD, and enterprise SSD portfolios.[^9]

These facts support a narrower conclusion:

> **consumer/general-purpose memory resilience can improve before frontier HBM closure is achieved.**

Front-end manufacturing for conventional DRAM and NAND is not the same physical pool as TSMC leading-edge logic foundry capacity.

But HBM-class AI systems can reintroduce advanced-logic/base-die, advanced-packaging, and accelerator-integration dependencies. Micron, for example, has publicly disclosed a TSMC partnership for manufacturing HBM4E base logic dies.[^10]

"Conventional memory can bypass TSMC logic foundry" and "the frontier AI memory stack is independent of TSMC" are therefore different propositions.

### 6.2 Revenue share and shipment share are different denominators

CXMT's prospectus, citing Omdia, provides an auditable metric: based on **2025 Q2 DRAM revenue**, CXMT held approximately **3.97% global share**, ranking fourth globally and first in China.[^11]

The claim that "CXMT already has 8% of global DRAM" therefore does not enter this report's fact layer.

YMTC's latest institutional metric uses a different denominator. Counterpoint Research's Q2 2026 Memory & Storage Tracker places YMTC at roughly **14% of NAND shipments**, ranking third globally.[^12] The same research firm's Q1 2026 **13%** figure is a NAND **revenue share**.[^13]

Those two percentages cannot be turned into one continuous time series because their denominators differ.

What they support is a narrower conclusion:

> **China's conventional DRAM / NAND presence in global supply has risen materially, but share growth does not by itself prove HBM, leading-edge logic, or frontier-system closure.**

Claims such as "capacity will double by 2027" or specific Phase-III monthly wafer output remain B/C-grade background unless they are disclosed directly by the company or another formal first-party source.

### 6.3 Storage resilience is broader than NAND

AI data-center storage also includes nearline HDD.

Public industry research shows that global nearline / mass-capacity HDD supply remains highly concentrated in Seagate, Western Digital, and Toshiba.

Stronger domestic NAND / SSD capability therefore cannot be rewritten as "the entire storage stack is domestically closed."

AI has also not simply eliminated HDD. Mass-capacity / cold-storage demand remains real, while QLC nearline SSD is accelerating substitution for selected workloads.

## 7. Foundry revenue share is not a frontier-AI capability metric

TrendForce's 1Q26 foundry revenue-share estimates are approximately:[^14]

**Table 17.1 — Global foundry revenue share, 1Q26 (TrendForce)**

| Foundry | Revenue share |
|---|---:|
| TSMC | 72% |
| Samsung Foundry (excluding System LSI) | 6.5% |
| SMIC | 5.1% |

**Source:** TrendForce, 2026-06-12.[^14]  
**Note:** These are foundry revenue shares, not advanced-node yield, AI wafer capacity, or frontier-system capability.

These are foundry **revenue shares**, not advanced-node yield, AI wafer capacity, or frontier-system delivery capability.

A frontier AI parallel supply stack requires more than logic dies. It also needs advanced packaging, substrates, HBM integration, yield, high-volume qualification, and power.

Node progress is one necessary condition, not the whole system.

## 8. Algorithmic efficiency can relax hardware constraints without closing the stack

The DeepSeek-V3 Technical Report shows that algorithmic and architectural design can materially lower the hardware required per unit of capability.

The paper reports **2.664M H800 GPU-hours for pre-training** and **2.788M H800 GPU-hours for full formal training**.[^15] Using the paper's US$2/GPU-hour assumption, **US$5.576M corresponds to full formal-training compute conversion**, not full R&D cost.

The supported mechanism is that in a supply-constrained environment, sparse architecture, memory efficiency, routing, quantization, and scheduling can improve effective compute productivity.

It does not support the claim that physical supply no longer matters, nor that any constrained system can automatically reproduce the same economics.

Algorithmic efficiency is a lever—not a substitute for HBM, packaging, interconnect, and power.

## 9. Domestic workloads create the iteration loop

A parallel stack does not need to win the global developer ecosystem before it can create value.

A sufficiently large captive workload can generate production feedback in compiler behavior, deployment, failure recovery, operator coverage, and utilization.

The logic is similar to hyperscaler custom silicon:

> **large captive workloads can first support a narrower but complete system stack before the stack expands toward a broader external ecosystem.**

Long-run progress should therefore be evaluated from production workload scale and persistence—not benchmarks alone.

## 10. Export controls reach manufacturing continuity, not only accelerator access

Export restrictions raise the near-term cost of acquiring advanced hardware and can slow frontier-cluster deployment.

A separate upstream issue is semiconductor-manufacturing equipment, software, and service. ASML's public statements confirm that EUV sales to China are restricted, the most advanced immersion DUV systems require export licences, and later controls expanded to additional advanced manufacturing technologies, including metrology and software.[^16][^17]

This does not mean every domestic memory fab would immediately stop operating under a disruption.

It does mean:

> **domestic memory capacity growth ≠ upstream manufacturing autonomy**

Whether new capacity can ramp, process generations can advance, and installed tools can be sustained depends on critical equipment, software licences, spare parts, field service, and materials qualification continuity.

Over the longer run, the same restrictions increase the incentive for domestic substitution, local hardware/software integration, and parallel-ecosystem development.

Export control is therefore not a one-direction variable.

It can widen short-term performance/scale gaps while strengthening the long-run incentive to build a parallel system.

The final outcome depends on R&D efficiency, capital allocation, ecosystem adoption, and supply scaling—not policy alone.

## 11. Two closure tests

Treating "production substitution" and "full autonomy" as one threshold creates two opposite analytical errors.

The report therefore separates them.

### 11.1 Production-Grade Parallel Stack

A substitute system should be considered production-grade only when the following can operate at verifiable scale:

1. accelerator / compute delivery;
2. HBM-class or equivalent memory subsystem;
3. advanced packaging / integration;
4. high-speed scale-up / scale-out interconnect;
5. compiler/runtime with acceptable migration cost;
6. frontier or high-value production workloads with sustained feedback;
7. system utilization, reliability, power, and economics.

This standard asks whether the system works commercially in real workloads.

It does **not** require every component, tool, or material to be domestic.

### 11.2 Autonomous Frontier Closure

A higher standard applies when the question is whether the stack can continue scaling and advancing when critical external inputs are constrained.

That additionally requires:

8. **advanced-manufacturing continuity:** equipment, software, materials, spares, field service, and process upgrades remain available or substitutable;
9. **infrastructure / capital continuity:** HBM, packaging, LPS/power, and long-duration capital can support multi-generation expansion.

Therefore:

> **production-grade substitution ≠ full technological autonomy**

Import dependence does not prove that no production-grade parallel stack exists. Conversely, successful production deployment does not prove autonomous frontier closure.

## 12. Why partial closure already matters globally

If China's parallel stack scales simultaneously in advanced logic, HBM, packaging, interconnect, and software, it will do more than replace imports.

It will create a second large resource-gravity center, adding new foundry demand, memory demand, packaging demand, power demand, and long-duration capital commitments to regional and global resource allocation.

Current public evidence is not sufficient to show that this second frontier resource center has already reached equivalent full-stack scale.

The cited May 27, 2026 registration prospectus does not disclose an HBM product line. SMIC's 5.1% figure is foundry revenue share rather than frontier capability. Multiple non-silicon layers still require independent validation.

The most accurate current state is therefore:

> **China has moved beyond the stage of "no alternative system," and a real frontier parallel stack is iterating with partial production deployment. But under this chapter's seven Production-Grade Gates, public evidence is still insufficient to confirm that a complete production-grade frontier parallel stack has closed. Autonomous Frontier Closure remains a still higher and unproven threshold.**

## 13. Current read and falsification

As of August 2026:

- smartphone / consumer computing: relatively strong parallel-supply capability;
- PC / workstation: expanding substitution, with software and professional-workload compatibility still determining closure;
- frontier AI: a real and continuing accelerator/software parallel stack exists, while HBM, advanced packaging, interconnect, software migration, power, and scale still require simultaneous validation.

The system-closure judgment should be raised if first-party evidence appears for:

- an official HBM product / revenue line;
- auditable large-cluster utilization and frontier workloads;
- scaled advanced-packaging delivery;
- multi-generation compiler/runtime compatibility;
- stable and sustainable system economics.

If these gates remain uncrossed, "a domestic technology stack exists" cannot be upgraded into "frontier-equivalent substitution."
