# Part III · Chapter 24: Capital Clearing — Lead Indicators, Demand Quality, and the S4 Test

## 1. The S4 question: when does expansion become clearing?

Large AI-infrastructure investment is not evidence of a bubble by itself, and high seller margins are not evidence of healthy buyer economics.

The S4 test begins when **asset formation starts to outrun utilization, realizable cash flow, financing independence, and residual value across multiple parts of the system**. The key is not one weak metric; it is a consistent divergence between capital formation and productive demand.

This chapter therefore does not ask whether AI valuations are high. It asks when the evidence is strong enough to move the scenario framework from S2-style expansion toward **S4 — Capital Hard Landing**.

One CapEx total, one valuation multiple, one stock drawdown, or one impairment is not sufficient. The decision depends on lead indicators, cross-signal confirmation, and causal breadth.

## 2. Clearing signals run on different clocks

These signal families operate on different lead/lag clocks. **Market Clearing, Utilization / Revenue, Forward Commitment, and Demand Quality / Financing can deteriorate before financial statements recognize losses; Accounting is usually a lagging confirmation / severity signal.** The framework therefore does not wait for impairment before increasing S4 weight.

### 2.1 Accounting: lagging confirmation

The slowest but hardest evidence comes from formal recognition:

- impairment;
- inventory write-down;
- accelerated depreciation;
- asset disposal.

These indicate that companies themselves have reduced expectations for future cash flow or residual value.

One-quarter policy or inventory effects cannot automatically be upgraded into structural collapse.

For example, H20-related charges affected NVIDIA FY26 results, but GAAP gross margin was **75.0% in FY26 Q4**, **74.9% in FY27 Q1**, and **75.0% in FY27 Q2**.[^1][^2][^3]

One-time charges and releases have to remain separate from long-run system economics.

### 2.2 Utilization / Revenue: the operating test

The capital cycle ultimately has to be paid for by effective workloads.

The key variables are compute utilization, revenue per deployed system, enterprise AI revenue, and workflow monetization.

High utilization can support very large CapEx.

If a growing asset base remains persistently underutilized, depreciation pressure can arrive before sufficient revenue.

The dangerous condition is not "there are many machines."

It is:

> **the asset base keeps growing while cash-generating effective workload does not grow with it.**

### 2.3 Market Clearing: the earliest price signal

Markets often show easing before financial statements.

Relevant indicators include:

- used accelerator / system prices;
- lease rates;
- cloud-instance discounts;
- delivery lead times;
- HBM prices;
- advanced-packaging availability;
- resale liquidity.

If a new generation rapidly displaces an older one, economic depreciation can move faster than accounting depreciation.

Persistent resale discounts and shorter lead times are therefore more meaningful signs of de-stocking than a stock-market drawdown alone.

### 2.4 Forward Commitment: the next-cycle signal

The next round of expansion depends on hyperscaler CapEx guidance, NVIDIA manufacturing/supply/capacity commitments, long-duration model-company compute contracts, and power commitments.

NVIDIA's public commitment path rose from **US$95.2B** on 2026-01-25 to **US$119B** on 2026-04-26 and **US$279B** on 2026-07-26.[^4]

The FY27 Q2 10-Q states that the latest commitments primarily relate to memory and manufacturing facilities required for data-center infrastructure systems.

The current trajectory looks more like continued forward resource reservation than broad cancellation.

**That statement only rejects the claim that broad cancellation has already occurred; it does not establish healthy independent end demand, healthy buyer ROI, or high financing quality.** Forward commitments must be read together with Section 7 on Demand Quality / Supplier-Assisted Financing.

## 3. The signal is divergence, not a synthetic global total

The four major hyperscalers do not report CapEx / PPE / finance-lease investment on one identical accounting basis. Chapter 19 therefore owns the full capital-base reconstruction and denominator discipline.

This chapter does not repeat the four-cloud CapEx table.

For capital clearing, the higher-value signals are directional:

- whether guidance is cut across multiple periods;
- whether investment mix moves from expansion toward maintenance;
- whether project timing is deferred;
- whether long-duration commitments are renegotiated;
- whether utilization of deployed assets falls at the same time.

Therefore:

> **Bubble detection does not require one global AI-CapEx total. It requires evidence that capital expansion, utilization, and forward commitments are beginning to diverge systematically.**

See [Chapter 19 — The AI Infrastructure CapEx Supercycle](part-III-ch19-capex-supercycle.md).
## 4. Seller economics and buyer ROI clear on different clocks

NVIDIA is a system seller. Hyperscalers are asset buyers.

The seller can recognize revenue and profit at shipment, while buyers carry utilization and depreciation risk on servers, data centers, networking, power, and long-duration contracts for years afterward.

A downturn therefore often unfolds sequentially:

> **buyer ROI deteriorates first → next-generation orders / commitments slow → seller growth weakens later → manufacturing utilization transmits upstream**

Strong seller margin does not prove healthy buyer-side ROI.

Buyer-side ROI pressure also does not imply seller economics collapse in the same quarter.

## 5. Efficiency matters only relative to workload growth

Infrastructure demand can be expressed at a high level as:

```text
Infrastructure Demand
= Unit Resource Intensity
× Workload Volume
```

If unit resource intensity falls faster than workload volume rises, total infrastructure demand can decline.

If workload volume grows faster, total demand can continue increasing despite efficiency gains.

The report therefore does not assign arbitrary demand-elasticity coefficients in the formal analysis.

The variables that matter are observable:

- cost per verified workflow;
- token / workflow volume;
- deployed-compute utilization;
- price / revenue per workload;
- enterprise adoption;
- infrastructure commitments.

Lower model prices or higher algorithmic efficiency do not, by themselves, prove overcapacity.

## 6. Anchor Compute Buyers add a new leading indicator

OpenAI Stargate, Anthropic/AWS, and other long-duration capacity commitments raise model companies from ordinary tenants into infrastructure Anchor Buyers.

They can improve project-financing certainty and pull forward data-center, power, chip, and memory construction.

This also means the leading S4 indicators now include more than "hyperscalers cut CapEx."

They include:

- model-capacity commitments being reduced, delayed, or renegotiated;
- GW-scale project ramps materially missing schedule;
- low utilization on committed long-duration capacity;
- upstream prepayments and seller commitments slowing at the same time.

If those variables weaken alongside buyer utilization and market-clearing prices, the evidence for Capital Hard Landing strengthens materially.

## 7. Demand quality: distinguish independent demand from supplier-assisted financing

When a system seller also participates through customer equity investment, credit enhancement, LPS support, or infrastructure finance, seller-side order visibility cannot be treated as identical to independently financed end demand.

NVIDIA's August 2026 disclosures now provide concrete examples: the company is supporting a **>US$500B third-party-capital mobilization target**, providing LPS credit support around PORTS-Pike, and discussing large frontier-lab investments plus selective credit enhancement.[^5][^6][^7]

This does not prove that the demand is artificial.

The key question is:

> **Can final workload monetization cover infrastructure cash cost and capital cost without requiring continually increasing supplier balance-sheet support?**

S4 monitoring should therefore include:

- customer operating cash flow / financing capacity;
- supplier equity / credit support;
- whether third-party capital bears independent risk;
- asset transferability across customers;
- Anchor Buyer utilization;
- refinancing dependency.

Healthy utilization can make seller-assisted financing an accelerator of real buildout.

Weak utilization combined with rising supplier support should reduce the evidentiary weight of forward commitments.

## 8. Clearing propagates through the asset-duration stack

Short-cycle assets clear first; long-cycle assets clear later.

A representative order is:

> **uninstalled hardware / inventory → system residual value → server utilization → data-center lease → long-duration power/project economics**

S4 can therefore last for years.

Power plants, substations, and data-center shells do not disappear like GPU inventory.

They can be repriced, sold, renegotiated, or become scarce again in the next concentration cycle.

## 9. S4 adjudication rule

This report upgrades S4 from stress test to the current main scenario only when **multiple causally independent signal families** show broad, persistent, mutually reinforcing deterioration across more than one actor or layer. The five groups below are monitoring families, not a five-of-five checklist:

1. **Accounting:** impairment / write-down / disposal rises materially;
2. **Utilization / Revenue:** utilization and effective AI revenue remain persistently weaker than asset expansion;
3. **Market Clearing:** resale prices, lease prices, lead times, and HBM/packaging clearing loosen materially;
4. **Forward Commitment:** CapEx guidance, seller commitments, Anchor Buyer contracts, and power commitments slow, reschedule, or cancel;
5. **Demand Quality / Financing:** customer cash generation weakens, supplier equity / credit support or refinancing dependency rises, and independent risk capital becomes less willing to absorb exposure.

The five groups do not need to deteriorate in the same quarter because accounting, project finance, and market clearing operate on different clocks. **Accounting is confirmation / severity evidence, not a mandatory gate**: financing-led or real-economy clearing can be confirmed before qualifying impairment or write-down evidence appears.

Judgment is cumulative: each signal updates at its own disclosure frequency per the principle in Chapter 29 §12A, without a single fixed threshold; S4 is upgraded only when several groups deteriorate persistently and in a mutually reinforcing way; when signals diverge, the baseline is maintained and the divergence is logged in the monitoring framework.

But one weak signal—a stock-price decline, lower model prices, a single-quarter margin move, or more seller support—is not sufficient if utilization and monetization remain healthy.

## 10. Current read

As of August 2026, public evidence is still closer to:

> **high-intensity capital expansion + rising ROI pressure**

than to a confirmed AI-infrastructure bubble collapse.

NVIDIA FY26 non-GAAP free cash flow remains **US$96.575B**,[^1] FY27 Q2 GAAP operating income **US$63.734B**,[^3] and supply/capacity commitments have risen to **US$279B**[^4] while hyperscalers continue to report very high infrastructure-spending plans.

This does not guarantee a soft landing.

It only means that declaring capital clearing the dominant reality still requires **cross-signal confirmation plus cross-actor causal breadth** across real-economy, market-clearing, commitment, and financing evidence; accounting losses strengthen severity when they appear but are not required for confirmation.

## References

[^1]: NVIDIA, FY26 official results, NVIDIA-defined non-GAAP free cash flow and FY26 Q4 margin, https://nvidianews.nvidia.com/news/nvidia-announces-financial-results-for-fourth-quarter-and-fiscal-2026
[^2]: NVIDIA Corporation, FY27 Q1 Form 10-Q, GAAP gross margin 74.9%, U.S. SEC, https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1045810/000104581026000052/nvda-20260426.htm

# Part III · Chapter 25: AI and Labor Markets — The Task-to-Job Translation Layer

## 1. AI changes tasks before job structures

Jobs are bundles of tasks, and those tasks differ in automation cost, review burden, liability, physical presence, and trust.

The analytical starting point is therefore task and workflow exposure — not direct job-loss extrapolation.

## 2. Exposure is not an employment outcome

ILO and IMF research both require a separation between occupational exposure and actual adoption, job redesign, and employment outcomes.

ILO's 2025 refined index estimates that roughly **one in four jobs worldwide has some GenAI exposure**, while emphasizing that transformation is more likely than simple redundancy.[^1]

The IMF framework estimates around **40% of global employment** and about **60% of advanced-economy employment** as exposed to AI.[^2]

These are occupational / employment exposure measures.

They are not "the same percentage of tasks will be automated," and they are not equivalent to the same percentage of jobs disappearing.

## 2A. 2026 shows localized signals, not a general labor-market verdict

Stanford AI Index 2026 reports that U.S. employment for **software developers aged 22–25 fell by nearly 20% from 2024**, while roughly one-third of surveyed organizations expect AI to reduce workforce over the following year. At the same time, broad employment data have not shown job losses on the same scale across the economy.[^3]

This evidence has to be read narrowly:

- it refers to a specific country, age cohort, and occupation;
- hiring cycles, macro conditions, graduate supply, and organizational restructuring can also affect the observed change;
- it does not establish that AI alone caused a 20% decline.

Anthropic's June 2026 Economic Index provides a different early signal: respondents who use Claude in more automated ways tend to anticipate that AI will be able to perform a larger share of their tasks over the next 12 months.[^4] Anthropic explicitly notes that the survey is not representative of the general population and over-represents computer/mathematical and management occupations.

The defensible 2026 judgment remains:

> **Localized hiring-pipeline and early-career signals are visible, but the translation from task exposure to workflow adoption to employment outcome is still unfolding.**

## 3. Task-to-Job Translation requires multiple gates

This report uses the following transmission chain:

> **Model Capability → Task Suitability → Workflow Redesign → Adoption Economics / Responsibility → Job Composition → Employment / Wage Outcome**

The importance of this chain is that any layer can interrupt a direct extrapolation from better model capability to fewer jobs.

For example:

- a model being able to perform a task does not mean the firm's workflow has been redesigned;
- a workflow being automatable does not mean error, compliance, review, and responsibility costs are low enough;
- compression of one task does not imply disappearance of the whole occupation;
- higher output per employee can show up as demand expansion rather than headcount reduction.

Employment forecasting should therefore begin with tasks and workflows rather than jump directly from benchmark performance to job counts.

## 4. Adoption economics is the first substitution gate

Firms have stronger substitution incentives only when:

> **AI cost + review + error + compliance + integration < human cost / value alternative**

Benchmark improvement therefore does not automatically translate into employment change.

Reliability, latency, security, integration, and legal responsibility all alter adoption economics.

## 5. Exposure is highest where tasks are digital, repeatable, and evaluable

Examples include:

- drafting;
- summarization;
- classification;
- routine coding;
- standard support;
- research assistance.

These tasks have digital inputs/outputs and relatively clear evaluation criteria, making them easier to compress through agent/workflow systems.

Task compression can still appear as higher output per worker rather than immediate elimination of the job.

## 6. Responsibility constraints preserve human review

Medical sign-off, engineering sign-off, field maintenance, nuclear operations, complex negotiation, and care work are constrained by liability, physical presence, licensing, and trust.

AI can change preparation, analysis, and documentation while final responsibility remains with a human.

Human review is therefore both a software cost and a labor-market boundary.

## 7. The entry-level ladder is a key structural variable

Even if total employment does not fall sharply in the short run, traditional entry-level tasks in junior analysis, junior coding, entry-level support, and routine research can be compressed first.

A conventional career ladder often looks like:

> junior work → experience accumulation → senior judgment

If junior tasks are heavily automated, firms and education systems have to redesign how new workers accumulate experience.

This can be more important than one universal unemployment forecast.

## 8. Wage outcomes can diverge

AI can increase leverage for high-skill workers while lowering the value of routine tasks and raising the premium on system, operator, and verification skills.

Possible outcomes include:

- higher productivity for top performers;
- contraction in middle routine roles;
- stronger demand for physical / system technical roles.

Labor outcomes are therefore more likely to be structurally divergent than to produce the same wage decline across all knowledge workers.

## 9. Physical AI can create a slower second wave

Automation in warehouses, manufacturing, logistics, inspection, and maintenance has to cross hardware cost, reliability, safety, maintenance, and regulatory thresholds.

The labor translation of Physical AI is therefore usually slower and more capital-intensive than purely digital workflow automation.

## 10. AI infrastructure creates new skill demand

AI expansion simultaneously increases demand for:

- data-center technicians;
- liquid-cooling engineers;
- power engineers;
- semiconductor process engineers;
- advanced-packaging engineers;
- network engineers;
- safety/compliance roles;
- model-infrastructure roles.

Labor-market change is therefore a reallocation process, not a one-direction reduction in jobs.

## 11. Organization shape depends on review burden and task volume

Agent-native workflows can reduce some coordination work.

But if task volume, exception handling, review, and compliance rise simultaneously, headcount does not necessarily fall in the same proportion.

More useful observables include:

- management layers;
- span of control;
- junior/senior ratio;
- revenue per employee;
- human-review ratio.

## 12. Current read

As of 2026, there is enough evidence to conclude that AI is restructuring tasks.

There is not enough cross-country evidence to support one universal "economy-wide job disappearance rate."

The correct sequence remains:

> **first observe task automation → then workflow redesign → finally employment / wage outcomes**

Any framework that converts an exposure percentage directly into an unemployment forecast skips the most important corporate-adoption and responsibility layers.

## References

[^1]: International Labour Organization, “Generative AI and Jobs: A Refined Global Index of Occupational Exposure,” Working Paper 140, 2025, https://researchrepository.ilo.org/esploro/outputs/encyclopediaEntry/Generative-AI-and-jobs-a-refined/995653520102676
[^2]: International Monetary Fund, “Gen-AI: Artificial Intelligence and the Future of Work,” Staff Discussion Note, 2024, https://www.imf.org/-/media/Files/Publications/SDN/2024/English/SDNEA2024001.ashx
[^3]: Stanford HAI, “2026 AI Index Report — Economy,” early-career software-developer employment and employer workforce expectations, https://hai.stanford.edu/ai-index/2026-ai-index-report/economy
[^4]: Anthropic, “Anthropic Economic Index report: Cadences,” 2026-06-26, reported and anticipated task exposure among Claude users; survey explicitly not representative of the general population, https://www.anthropic.com/research/economic-index-june-2026-report

# Part III · Chapter 26: Education and Talent Systems in the AI Era

## 1. Durable talent advantage comes from system understanding, not tool familiarity

Prompting, model calls, and basic automation can commoditize quickly. Durable advantage comes from understanding how models, software, compute, data, power, economics, and responsibility interact in a production system.

The more durable capability stack includes:

- models;
- data;
- compute;
- memory;
- software;
- networking;
- power;
- economics;
- responsibility.

This mirrors the industrial shift from competition around one chip toward competition around the system stack.

## 2. Adoption is moving faster than curriculum and policy

The Stanford HAI 2026 AI Index Education chapter provides several clear signals:[^1]

- **4 out of 5** U.S. high-school and college students use AI for schoolwork;[^1]
- only about half of middle/high schools have an AI policy;[^1]
- **just 6% of teachers say those policies are clear**;[^1]
- U.S. four-year university CS undergraduate enrollment fell **11% from 2024 to 2025**;[^1]
- AI software-related master's graduates grew **17% from 2023 to 2024**.[^1]

The pattern is:

> **Tool Adoption > Curriculum Update > Institutional Reform**

The same Stanford chapter also reports that **more than 90% of countries** now offer computer science in primary or secondary education, while formal AI education has spread more slowly; China and the United Arab Emirates moved to mandatory AI education beginning with the **2025–26 school year**.[^1]

This moves the question from isolated school experimentation toward national education-system design.

The question is no longer whether students are using AI.

It is whether institutions can verify that students understand, control, and take responsibility for AI-generated outcomes.

### 2A. International frameworks are already moving beyond tool use

UNESCO's AI Competency Framework for Students defines **12 competencies across four dimensions**: human-centred mindset, ethics of AI, AI techniques and applications, and AI system design, organized across Understand → Apply → Create.[^2]

UNESCO's teacher framework defines **15 competencies across five dimensions**, adding AI pedagogy and AI for professional learning to human-centred, ethics, and foundations/application capabilities.[^3]

The direction is consistent with this report's industrial thesis:

> **AI literacy cannot collapse into prompt technique. Durable capability includes judgment, responsibility, system understanding, application design, and verification.**

A curriculum centered only on the current popular model will age quickly. System design, verification, domain reasoning, and responsibility are more portable across models and platforms.

## 3. Layer one: AI literacy

All disciplines need a basic understanding of:

- model limitations;
- hallucination;
- source verification;
- privacy;
- data leakage;
- bias;
- responsible use.

AI literacy is becoming a general digital competency rather than an elective skill for AI specialists.

## 4. Layer two: workflow engineering

Many roles do not require training a foundation model.

They do require:

- model selection;
- context / prompt design;
- retrieval;
- tool orchestration;
- evaluation;
- automation;
- human review.

This forms the application layer of AI engineering.

## 5. Layer three: system engineering

Frontier AI requires accelerators, HBM, networking, distributed computing, compiler/runtime, data centers, and power.

Top-tier AI engineering education therefore has to cross:

> **computer science + computer architecture + systems/networking + infrastructure economics**

Model-only coursework cannot capture real industrial constraints.

## 6. Hardware and software cannot remain fully separated

Inference optimization can simultaneously involve:

- quantization;
- kernels;
- HBM bandwidth;
- interconnect;
- batching;
- power.

Future curricula therefore need more cross-layer labs, real performance/cost measurement, and system debugging.

## 7. Multi-stack capability is a strategic skill

Engineers should not learn only one proprietary stack.

Higher-value capability includes understanding:

- CUDA;
- alternative accelerator stacks;
- cloud custom silicon;
- open compiler/runtime;
- distributed frameworks;

and being able to migrate workloads across systems.

The educational goal is portability, not platform loyalty.

## 8. Physical AI requires a different interdisciplinary stack

Robotics / Physical AI combines control, mechanics, sensing, embedded compute, AI, and safety.

A scarce future skill will be the ability to connect model capability with physical-world reliability.

## 9. Power / cooling / operations belong inside the talent stack

AI data-center buildout makes electrical distribution, power engineering, liquid cooling, networking, and operations part of AI infrastructure.

"AI talent" therefore cannot be measured only by the number of ML engineers.

## 10. Entry-level education has to adapt to task compression

If drafting, routine coding, and basic research are increasingly performed by agents, students need earlier exposure to:

- system debugging;
- verification;
- architecture;
- real-world projects;
- responsibility.

Otherwise, education will continue investing heavily in the tasks most likely to be automated first.

## 11. Assessment has to measure verification and responsibility

A stronger project-based assessment should require students to explain:

- output correctness;
- source / evidence;
- system design;
- cost;
- reliability;
- power / resource use;
- safety / responsibility.

That is closer to real industrial capability than simply banning AI tools.

## 12. Current read

The central educational change is not "create another AI major."

It is:

> **make AI a systems capability across technical and professional education while raising verification, portability, and responsibility into core learning objectives.**

The highest-value talent will not simply be the people who are best at calling models.

It will be the people who can understand systems, verify results, calculate economics, migrate across stacks, and take responsibility for real-world consequences.

## References

[^1]: Stanford Institute for Human-Centered Artificial Intelligence, “2026 AI Index Report — Education,” https://hai.stanford.edu/ai-index/2026-ai-index-report/education
[^2]: UNESCO, “AI competency framework for students,” 2024, updated 2026-01-16, https://www.unesco.org/en/articles/ai-competency-framework-students?hub=66903
[^3]: UNESCO, “AI competency framework for teachers,” 2024, updated 2026-01-16, https://www.unesco.org/en/articles/ai-competency-framework-teachers?hub=779

# Part IV · Chapter 27: Research Method and Evidence Architecture

## 1. Research quality starts with claim identity and denominator discipline

AI-infrastructure research crosses finance, semiconductors, memory, packaging, cloud, power, regulation, and labor. Those fields do not share one denominator, one clock, or one evidence standard.

The first methodological task is therefore to preserve **claim identity, physical unit, time identity, and evidence strength** before building a narrative.

Without one common discipline, recurring errors include:

- mixing company disclosures with institutional forecasts;
- converting revenue share into wafer share;
- rewriting contracted MW as energized / utilized load;
- presenting engineering estimates as vendor disclosures;
- treating guidance as actuals;
- presenting future scenarios as current facts;
- repeating one secondary-source number across chapters until it appears to become multi-source confirmation.

This book therefore uses two complementary frameworks:

> **F → H → M → S → X**

and:

> **A / B / C / D evidence grades**

The first determines the identity of a claim; the second determines evidence strength.

## 2. F — Fact

A Fact must describe something that has already occurred and can be traced to a clear public source.

Preferred sources include:

- 10-K / 20-F / annual reports;
- official earnings materials;
- official product specifications;
- regulator / government documents;
- court filings;
- formal contracts / official project disclosures.

Fact does not mean "many media outlets reported the same thing."

Multiple media reports citing the same origin do not create independent multi-source confirmation.

## 3. H — Hypothesis

A Hypothesis is a future input condition inferred from facts but not fully confirmed by reality.

Example:

> **If a system platform continues to combine stronger cash flow, long-duration orders, prepayment capacity, and roadmap certainty, its bargaining power for next-generation scarce capacity may increase.**

That is not a fact contained in a company filing.

It is a testable hypothesis that can later be checked against allocation, commitment, and delivery data.

## 4. M — Mechanism

A Mechanism must explain the intermediate variables connecting facts and future states.

Example:

> high system margin → higher cash generation → larger forward commitments → stronger capacity reservation → higher delivery certainty → stronger customer retention

A mechanism cannot collapse into "it is strong, therefore it will become stronger."

It has to identify what changes, how the constraint transmits, and which evidence would show that the chain has broken.

## 5. S — Scenario

A Scenario is a conditional state created by multiple hypotheses and mechanisms.

The report uses four standard scenarios:

- **S1 — Competitive Expansion**;
- **S2 — Baseline Concentration**;
- **S3 — Resource Hegemony**;
- **S4 — Capital Hard Landing**.

The objective is not to assign fixed world probabilities.

It is to ask:

> **If a set of observable conditions holds together, which structural state does the industry resemble most closely?**

## 6. X — Falsification

Every strong judgment requires reverse signals.

For example, the thesis that scarce-layer value capture continues strengthening must be weakened if several of the following reverse persistently:

- supply commitments fall;
- advanced packaging stops being tight;
- HBM / DRAM residual supply loosens materially;
- cloud frontier workloads migrate at scale away from the dominant stack;
- open interfaces materially reduce migration costs;
- utilization and forward commitments weaken together.

A long-run narrative without falsification conditions does not enter the report's formal judgment layer.

## 7. A / B / C / D evidence grades

**Table 27.1 — Evidence-grade system used in this report**

| Grade | Definition | Typical source |
|---|---|---|
| A | First-party / formal evidence | filing, official product page, regulator, government, court, formal contract |
| B | Institutional research | TrendForce, ILO, IMF, Stanford HAI, academic / industry research |
| C | Sell-side / supply-chain / media estimate | investment bank, channel, supply-chain source, media estimate |
| D | Report model / stress test | geometry, arithmetic, sensitivity, scenario assumption |

**Source:** Report research method.  
**Note:** Evidence grade measures source strength, not analytical importance. D-grade models can support scenarios and sensitivity analysis but cannot be presented as observed facts.

Examples:

- Micron HBM3E same-node/same-bits ≈ **3× DDR5 wafer supply**: **A**;[^1]
- TrendForce estimate that NVIDIA represented >60% of 2025 CoWoS demand: **B**;[^2]
- a sell-side estimate of one customer's monthly CoWoS wafer count: **C**;
- a report-side sensitivity or scenario input with explicit assumptions: **D**.

Repeating the same number across multiple chapters does not automatically upgrade its evidence grade.

## 8. Dimensional consistency outranks narrative convenience

### Revenue share ≠ wafer share

TSMC customer revenue share cannot be converted directly into N2, 3 nm, HPC, or CoWoS physical share.

### Demand share ≠ allocation share

An institutional estimate of one customer's demand share is not supplier-confirmed physical allocation.

### Contracted / Secured Capacity ≠ Energized Capacity ≠ Utilized AI Load

A long-duration PPA, secured LPS position, or future IT-GW design capacity cannot be rewritten as active AI load.[^3]

Generation MW/GW, PPA capacity, IT-GW, energized facility load, and utilized AI load must remain separate physical identities.

### Product specification ≠ shipment

The GB200 NVL72 specification includes **13.4 TB of HBM3E per rack**.[^4]

That is a system specification. Without a verified deployment count, it cannot be multiplied into an industry-wide actual HBM-consumption figure.

### Guidance / expectation ≠ actual

Company guidance must retain its guidance identity and cannot be combined with another company's TTM actuals into a single "already occurred" total.

### Physical Pool A ≠ Physical Pool B

Front-end logic wafers, advanced-packaging capacity, HBM bits, and energized MW cannot be placed in one denominator merely because they serve the same AI system.

### Production-Grade Parallel Stack ≠ Autonomous Frontier Closure

A substitute system can operate real production workloads while still depending on some external equipment, materials, services, or upstream manufacturing.

The report therefore asks separately:

1. whether production-grade substitution exists;
2. whether the system can continue scaling across generations when critical external inputs are constrained.

### Seller-Assisted Financing ≠ Independent End Demand

Seller equity, credit enhancement, LPS support, and compute-financing platforms can accelerate real infrastructure formation while changing the independence of the demand signal.

Financing-quality analysis must therefore distinguish:

- independently financed customer demand;
- long-duration Anchor Buyer commitments;
- third-party infrastructure capital;
- supplier equity / credit support.

---
## 9. Negative evidence has a boundary

"Not disclosed in this document" proves only that the document does not disclose it.

For example, if CXMT's registration prospectus does not disclose an HBM product line, the supported statement is:

> **The registration prospectus does not disclose an HBM product line.**

It cannot be expanded into "the company has no HBM R&D."

Negative evidence limits what can be confirmed. It does not prove absolute non-existence.

## 10. Forecasts, guidance, and scenarios must retain time identity

Institutional forecasts must preserve:

- source identity;
- publication date;
- forecast window.

For example, TrendForce's "HBM + RDIMM ≈51% of 2026 DRAM bit supply" must remain a **2026E institutional forecast**, not become a 2026 actual through repeated citation.

Likewise, 2030/2036 node prices, monthly packaging capacity, or GW campus estimates can only appear as explicitly conditioned assumptions.

## 11. Concept hierarchy: load-bearing mechanisms vs. supporting language

To avoid treating every named term as a separate theory, the report distinguishes **load-bearing mechanisms** from **supporting descriptive / measurement language**.

### Table 27.2 — Concept hierarchy

| Layer | Concept | Role in the main causal chain |
|---|---|---|
| Load-bearing | **Non-AI Residual Capacity** | Measures what effective supply remains available to traditional end systems after priority allocation |
| Load-bearing | **Layer-specific Incremental Scarce-Capacity Access** | Tracks access to incremental scarce supply separately across logic / HBM / packaging / networking / power |
| Load-bearing | **Rolling Bottleneck Cascade** | Explains how constraints migrate, ease, or reverse across physical layers |
| Load-bearing | **Cloud System-Stack Substitution + Parallel-Stack Closure Boundary** | Separates real second-stack substitution from production-grade versus autonomous frontier closure |
| Load-bearing | **Financing Reflexivity** | Tests feedback between orders, capital formation, seller-assisted financing, and demand independence |
| Load-bearing | **AI Inheritance Effect** | Explains how the existing IT installed base expands workflow penetration and the long-duration demand pool |
| Supporting / measurement | AI Resource Gravity, Scarce-layer Value Capture, Two-Speed Semiconductor Economy, Anchor Compute Buyer Effect, AI Capacity Gap / Growth Gap, AI Software Unit Economics, Task-to-Job Translation | Describes direction, measurement boundaries, or second-order transmission; these do not constitute separate grand theories |

**Source:** Report research method.  
**Note:** Supporting terms can still be analytically important. Their purpose is to make causal structure and denominators clearer, not to imply that naming a mechanism constitutes independent empirical validation.

These models organize causal relationships.

They do not replace source facts.

Their validity must ultimately be tested by new F and X evidence.

## 12. Deduplication and canonical-home rules

A formal report should not repeatedly rewrite the same facts and mechanisms across chapters.

The publication rules are:

1. one high-impact factual table has one primary location;
2. a mechanism is fully defined once and later chapters inherit it through cross-reference;
3. later chapters add only new evidence, boundaries, counterarguments, or falsification conditions;
4. when one number appears across chapters, its original denominator, time window, and evidence grade remain unchanged;
5. sources move with the claims they support;
6. revision history, editorial process, and construction metadata do not enter public-facing text.

The purpose is not to reduce information. It is to let readers distinguish first definition, incremental evidence, and cross-chapter inference.

## 13. Research-quality standard

A high-impact claim should be publication-ready as research only when:

1. its factual identity is explicit;
2. the denominator and unit are stable;
3. observed fact, company guidance, institutional forecast, and report-side calculation are not mixed;
4. the causal mechanism is stated;
5. material counterevidence is represented;
6. the claim has an observable condition that could force it to be revised.

This is a research standard, not a software-release or artifact-packaging process.

## References

[^1]: Micron Technology, FY24 Q2 Prepared Remarks, HBM3E ≈3× DDR5 wafer supply at same node / same bits, https://s25.q4cdn.com/621799436/files/doc_financials/2024/q2/FY24-Q2-Prepared-Remarks.pdf
[^2]: TrendForce, 2025 CoWoS demand structure, NVIDIA estimated >60%, B-grade institutional estimate, https://www.trendforce.com/research/download/RP250314ZJ
[^3]: Talen Energy, 2026 Q2 Form 10-Q, revised AWS PPA up to 1,920 MW, U.S. SEC, https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1622536/000162253626000066/tln-20260630.htm
[^4]: NVIDIA, “GB200 NVL72,” official product page, 13.4 TB HBM3E per rack, https://www.nvidia.com/en-us/data-center/gb200-nvl72/

# Part IV · Chapter 28: 2026–2036 Timeline and Conditional Milestones

## 1. The timeline is a conditional milestone map, not a forecast calendar

A long-range timeline becomes misleading when roadmap dates, capital plans, or project targets are treated as guaranteed future events. This chapter therefore uses time windows as **observation intervals**, not deterministic milestones.

This report uses four observation windows:

- **Phase I: 2026–2027**;
- **Phase II: 2028–2030**;
- **Phase III: 2031–2033**;
- **Phase IV: 2034–2036**.

The years are observation windows, not event guarantees.

## 2. Phase I: 2026–2027 — system race and infrastructure lock-in

Current confirmed anchors include:[^1][^2][^3][^4][^5][^6]

- NVIDIA FY27 Q2 Data Center revenue **US$89.0B**;
- GAAP gross margin **75.0%**;
- Rubin in **full production**;
- manufacturing, supply, and capacity commitments rising from **US$95.2B** on 2026-01-25 → **US$119B** on 2026-04-26 → **US$279B** on 2026-07-26;
- TSMC advanced-packaging tightness still limiting customer growth;[^7]
- public capital-spending metrics for the four major clouds remaining elevated, while retaining their different actual / guidance / expectation identities;[^8]
- power / LPS projects appearing at different maturity states with scales around 835 MW, up to 1,920 MW, up to 500 MWe, and PORTS-Pike initial **4.25 IT-GW design capacity**;[^9]
- TrendForce **forecasting** HBM + RDIMM at roughly **51% of 2026 DRAM bit supply**;
- Sony's FY2026 hardware planning directly linking PS5 unit-sales / promotional flexibility to memory volume procurable at reasonable prices.[^10]

The central question is:

> **Does AI workload demand continue to grow faster than scarce-layer supply expansion?**

Key milestones include:

- Rubin volume deployment / customer adoption;
- HBM4 ramp;
- whether packaging expansion removes the growth constraint;
- next-generation TPU / Trainium / Maia / MTIA production deployment;
- actual delivery against Stargate / Anthropic and other Anchor Buyer commitments;
- conversion of contracted power into energized / utilized MW;
- whether system-seller commitments continue expanding;
- whether memory / storage pressure in directly exposed end systems such as smartphones, PCs, and game consoles continues to transmit into configuration, promotion, or hardware cadence—or begins to ease.

## 3. Phase II: 2028–2030 — does substitution reach production scale?

This window is more likely to determine the divergence between S1 and S2/S3.

### Cloud System Stack

Observe whether TPU, Trainium, Maia, and MTIA:

- continue carrying large production workloads;
- gain share in frontier training / high-value inference;
- win more external customer adoption;
- deliver stable utilization and system-TCO advantages.

### Software Portability

Observe whether open compiler/runtime, model formats, and network interfaces materially reduce CUDA / proprietary-stack migration cost.

### Memory / Packaging

Observe whether HBM supply enters sustained abundance, advanced packaging stops being described as a growth constraint, and new packaging architectures materially increase supply elasticity.

### End-System Residual Pressure

This phase should also test whether upstream expansion actually reaches end systems rather than stopping at supply headlines:

- whether same-price smartphone / PC configuration progress resumes;
- whether game-console memory procurement, promotion, and long-generation cost-down normalize;
- whether automotive / industrial mature-memory, EOL, lead-time, and qualified-alternate conditions improve;
- whether quantity-assurance gaps narrow;
- whether supplier product mix rebalances.

If upstream supply elasticity improves while these end-system proxies do not, growth in gross capacity has not yet translated into **effective non-AI residual supply**.

### Power / LPS

Observe how much headline contracted / secured capacity converts into energized and utilized capacity and whether Time-to-Power continues to shape site selection.

PORTS-Pike is publicly planned to begin phased delivery from **2028**, making it a useful Phase II test of whether secured LPS and future IT-GW design capacity actually become deployable compute rather than a Phase I operating-capacity claim.[^9]

## 4. Phase III: 2031–2033 — geography and industrial structure may harden

If AI infrastructure continues expanding, competition is likely to move further from product roadmaps toward regional system capability.

The key questions are:

- which regions combine **power + data centers + chip access + software / talent** most effectively;
- whether China's frontier parallel stack crosses more Production-Grade Gates and autonomous frontier closure crosses more upstream / infrastructure gates;
- whether sovereign AI creates multiple regional system ecosystems;
- whether supply chains remain cross-region complementary or become durably partitioned;
- whether advanced-manufacturing redundancy creates real qualified second-source capability;
- whether quantity assurance, qualified second-source options, and supplier longevity improve for long-lifecycle end systems—or harden into a structural upgrade tax.

More useful metrics in this phase include:

- deployable compute;
- system utilization;
- energized power;
- cross-stack portability;
- regional supply resilience.

## 5. Phase IV: 2034–2036 — maturity, diffusion, concentration, or clearing

By this observation window, four structural states should be easier to distinguish.

### Competitive Expansion

Multiple system stacks mature, migration cost falls, supply elasticity improves, and the market continues to expand with more distributed value capture. On the end-system side, quantity assurance improves, same-price configuration progress resumes, and long-life supply continuity strengthens.

### Baseline Concentration

A small number of dominant platforms and scarce-layer suppliers persist while credible substitution paths remain. End-system residual pressure remains selective rather than becoming one universal shortage.

### Resource Hegemony

Coordination power across logic, HBM, packaging, software/interconnect, and power becomes more concentrated. If executable second paths remain weak, quantity-assurance gaps and upgrade-tax risk can harden for low-bargaining-power and long-qualification end systems.

### Capital Hard Landing

Built infrastructure exceeds productive workload, shifting the industry from construction competition toward utilization, residual value, and cash-flow clearing. Some resources can flow back toward traditional end systems, while supplier retrenchment, inventory clearing, and specialized-capacity impairment can create a different supply-chain whiplash.

## 6. Technology milestones should not be hard-coded

The timeline does not pre-set:

- exact wafer prices;
- exact future-node ASPs;
- exact CoWoS wafers/month;
- exact GW/rack counts;
- exact company market shares.

These variables depend heavily on yield, architecture, product mix, demand, regulation, and the capital cycle.

The report records falsifiable conditions and milestones rather than treating distant target years as completed facts.

## 7. Algorithmic milestones

The long-run dashboard should track:

- memory compression;
- sparse architectures;
- quantization;
- inference scheduling;
- model routing;
- on-device capability;
- workload orchestration.

If unit resource intensity falls sharply but total workload volume grows faster, aggregate infrastructure demand can still increase.

Algorithmic milestones therefore matter only when interpreted together with demand elasticity.

## 8. Capital milestones

Each phase should track:

- hyperscaler CapEx;
- D&A / useful-life changes;
- seller supply commitments;
- Anchor Buyer contracts;
- utilization;
- second-hand / lease prices;
- enterprise AI monetization;
- infrastructure financing terms;
- supplier equity / credit support;
- refinancing dependency / third-party risk capital.

Technology and capital timelines have to be read together.

## 9. Power milestones

Power / LPS uses one state machine:

> **Announced → Contracted / Secured → Permitted → Construction / Transition → Energized → Utilized**

Crane, Talen/AWS, Kairos/Google, PORTS-Pike, and similar projects must be updated by current state.

Signing a contract, securing LPS, or announcing future design capacity cannot automatically mark AI power as online.

Generation MW/GW, PPA capacity, IT-GW, energized facility load, and utilized AI load must retain their distinct physical identities.

In particular, the April-2026 revised-PPA transition status in Talen's 2026 Q2 10-Q does not prove that every physical transition or full-delivery step was completed.[^5]

The earlier 2025 10-K's spring-2027 full-transition expectation and no-later-than-2032 ramp provide additional physical-clock context.[^5]

## 10. Four thresholds matter more than the calendar year

The next decade is more likely to be decided by four thresholds than by one particular date:

1. **Does system substitution cross the production frontier-workload threshold?**
2. **Does scarce-layer supply cross a durable-abundance threshold?**
3. **Does AI-workload monetization cross the capital-cost-coverage threshold?**
4. **Can infrastructure move from sponsor / seller-supported financing toward a more independent, sustainable long-duration capital structure?**

Until these thresholds are much clearer, 2036 should remain a conditional cross-section rather than a deterministic answer.

# Part IV · Chapter 29: Monitoring Framework — State Variables, Inflection Tests, and Scenario Reweighting

## 1. Monitor variables that can actually change the view

A long-horizon view is useful only if later evidence can force it to change.

The monitoring framework therefore tracks **state variables, transition rates, and evidence quality — not news volume**. Each new observation has to answer three questions:

1. **What changed in the fact layer?**
2. **Which mechanism variable did that change?**
3. **Is the move material and persistent enough to reweight S1–S4?**

This is a governed research-update process, not an automated trading model. The purpose of the dashboard is to prevent one headline, one forecast revision, or one accounting event from being mistaken for a structural regime change.

## 2. Dashboard A — Seller economics / forward reservation

### NVIDIA current anchors

**Table 29.1 — NVIDIA seller economics and forward-reservation baseline**

| Metric | Current public value |
|---|---:|
| FY26 Revenue | US$215.938B |
| FY26 Data Center | US$193.737B |
| FY26 NVIDIA-defined non-GAAP FCF | US$96.575B |
| FY27 Q2 Revenue | US$96.221B |
| FY27 Q2 Data Center | US$89.0B |
| FY27 Q2 GAAP gross margin | 75.0% |
| FY27 Q2 GAAP operating income | US$63.734B |
| Rubin | full production |

**Source:** NVIDIA FY26 results, FY27 Q2 results, and FY27 Q2 Form 10-Q.[^1][^2]  
**Note:** Company-defined non-GAAP FCF and GAAP metrics retain their respective identities; aggregate commitments have no disclosed supplier-level split.

Supply / capacity commitments:[^2]

- 2026-01-25: US$95.2B;
- 2026-04-26: US$119B;
- 2026-07-26: US$279B.

These metrics ask whether the system seller still has enough cash generation and forward-commitment capacity to reserve next-generation scarce resources.

The US$279B figure does not contain a supplier-level split and cannot be converted into TSMC or HBM-vendor revenue.[^2]

## 3. Dashboard B — Foundry / packaging

### TSMC 2025

The following baseline is drawn from TSMC's 2025 Annual Report.[^3]

- 12-inch-equivalent wafer shipments: ~15.0M;
- annual capacity: >17M;
- 3 nm / 5 nm / 7 nm wafer revenue: 24% / 36% / 14%;
- ≤7 nm: 74%;
- HPC platform: 58%, +48% YoY;
- smartphone platform: 29%, +11% YoY;
- largest / second-largest annual customer: 19% / 17% of revenue.

A separate anonymized customer table supports only the 2024→2025 Customer A 12%→19% and Customer B 22%→17% trajectories.[^7]

The two disclosure types cannot be merged into one customer history.

### Packaging

A-grade signal: TSMC continues to describe packaging capacity as tight / growth-limiting.[^8]

B-grade signal: TrendForce estimates NVIDIA at >60% of 2025 CoWoS demand.[^9]

TSMC does not disclose customer-level CoWoS allocation.

## 4. Dashboard C — Memory

### A-grade manufacturing anchor

Micron: HBM3E same-node / same-bits ≈ **3× DDR5 wafer supply**; the FY25 10-K further confirms that HBM requires more wafers / cleanroom space.[^5]

### B-grade forward structure

TrendForce: **2026E HBM + RDIMM ≈51% of DRAM bit supply**.[^4]

### Vendor economics

SK hynix FY25:[^6]

- Revenue KRW97.1467T;
- Operating profit KRW47.2063T;
- Operating margin 49%;
- Net income KRW42.9479T;
- HBM revenue more than doubled YoY.

These indicators help test whether HBM product mix continues to absorb DRAM manufacturing input and whether conventional-DRAM residual capacity is loosening.

## 5. Dashboard D — Capital formation

For Microsoft, Amazon, Alphabet, and Meta, preserve separately:

- accounting line item;
- fiscal / calendar window;
- actual vs. guidance / expectation;
- finance-lease treatment;
- AI/data-center-specific disclosure.

The key question is not whether the total is a new record.

It is whether new capital continues flowing into servers, networking, memory, custom silicon, data centers, and power.

Non-comparable accounting definitions must not be mechanically added into an official AI-hardware CapEx total.

## 6. Dashboard E — Power delivery state

**Table 29.2 — Power project state machine**

| State | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Announced | project / target announced |
| Contracted / Secured | contract signed or land/power/shell capacity secured |
| Permitted | key permits obtained |
| Construction / Transition | physical buildout or contractual transition underway |
| Energized | actual power delivery |
| Utilized | real sustained load |

**Source:** Report power / LPS state framework.  
**Note:** The state machine prevents headline MW/GW, secured IT-GW, contract quantity, or project targets from being rewritten as active AI load. Generation MW/GW, PPA capacity, and IT-GW must retain their distinct physical identities.

Representative current paths include:

- Microsoft / Constellation: ~835 MW restart PPA;[^10]
- Talen / AWS: up to 1,920 MW revised PPA; April-2026 transition status must not be rewritten as full physical delivery complete;[^11]
- Google / Kairos: Hermes 2 up to 50 MWe in 2030; fleet up to 500 MWe by 2035;[^12]
- PORTS-Pike / NVIDIA / SB Energy / OpenAI: initial **4.25 IT-GW design capacity**, representing secured LPS / future phased buildout with planned delivery beginning in 2028.[^13]

The variables that need updating are each project's transition from secured / contracted into construction, energized capacity, actual load, and utilization—not headline capacity alone.

Generation MW/GW, PPA capacity, IT-GW, energized facility load, and utilized AI load must remain separate.

## 7. Dashboard F — Second-stack substitution

For Google / AWS / Microsoft / Meta, track:

- production workload share;
- external availability;
- compiler/runtime maturity;
- large-cluster scaling;
- migration cost;
- utilization;
- system TCO.

A single-chip benchmark is not sufficient evidence of system substitution.

## 8. Dashboard G — China closure tests

Track two different levels rather than one localization rate.

### Production-Grade Parallel Stack

Track:

- accelerator / compute delivery;
- HBM-class or equivalent memory subsystem;
- advanced packaging / integration;
- high-speed interconnect;
- compiler/runtime;
- production workload;
- utilization, reliability, and economics.

### Autonomous Frontier Closure

Then separately track:

- advanced-manufacturing continuity;
- equipment / materials / software / service;
- cross-generation process upgrades;
- HBM / packaging / LPS / power continuity;
- long-duration capital and multi-generation scaling.

Production deployment can strengthen the first level without proving the second.

## 9. Dashboard H — Regulatory state

Use one legal-state machine:

> inquiry / RFI → investigation → Statement of Objections / formal complaint → formal proceeding → final decision → remedy / fine

Do not convert:

- inquiry into "already sued";
- statutory ceiling into "fine is imminent";
- merger review into "monopoly already established."

The variables capable of changing the scenario are interface openness, tying restrictions, access / supply remedies, and structural change.

## 10. Dashboard I — Capital clearing / demand quality

### Accounting

Impairment, inventory write-down, accelerated depreciation, asset disposal.

### Utilization / Revenue

Compute utilization, AI-workflow revenue, revenue per deployed compute.

### Market Clearing

Used-system prices, cloud discounts, lease rates, delivery lead times.

### Forward Commitment

CapEx guidance, seller commitments, Anchor Buyer contracts, power contracts.

### Demand Quality / Financing

Customer cash generation, third-party risk capital, supplier equity / credit support, refinancing dependency, asset transferability, and Anchor Buyer utilization.

S4 should move from stress test toward the main scenario only when **multiple causally independent signal families** show broad, persistent, mutually reinforcing deterioration across more than one actor or layer. The five groups are monitoring families, not a mandatory five-of-five gate; Accounting is confirmation / severity evidence, not a prerequisite.

## 10A. Dashboard J — End-system residual pressure

If the report's downstream question is whether important end systems can still obtain enough, stable, affordable resources on their own product cadence, the monitoring framework cannot stop at seller economics, HBM, packaging, and power.

**Table 29.3 — End-system residual-pressure monitoring panel**

| End system | Main public proxies | Question to answer | Must not be inferred |
|---|---|---|---|
| Smartphone / PC | memory contract price, system ASP, allocation fulfillment, configuration change, replacement cycle | is same-price specification progress slowing; is quantity assurance diverging? | AI caused the full price increase; a named buyer lost a known wafer quantity |
| Game console | memory procurement, hardware unit plan, promotion, hardware margin, memory/storage BOM, generation cadence | is memory/storage pressure changing volume, promotion, SKU, or multi-year cost-down? | direct HBM competition; next generation must be delayed/repriced |
| Automotive / industrial | mature-memory price, EOL, lead time, qualified alternates, product-longevity commitments | do long qualification and long lifecycles amplify supply pressure into redesign / continuity risk? | automotive components and AI GPUs share one physical pool |
| Robotics / Physical AI | industrial-electronics lead time + edge-accelerator / memory availability | are industrial and AI-edge constraints appearing together? | every robot requires frontier HBM |
| High-reliability / aerospace / space | trusted access, EOL, qualification capacity, special-process continuity | is the economics of low-volume, long-life, special-process supply deteriorating? | AI caused a specific program delay / cost increase |

**Source:** Chapter 3 layered-exposure framework, Chapter 21 end-system transmission, and Sony / TrendForce game-console evidence.[^14][^15]  
**Note:** This is not a universal shortage index. It tracks **shared-resource exposure, buyer power, qualification rigidity, and quantity assurance** separately. AI-era attribution is strengthened only when upstream resource evidence and downstream outcome evidence align on the same transmission chain.

### Representative first-party anchor: game consoles

Sony's FY2025 Q4 / FY2026 outlook explicitly links FY26 PS5 hardware sales to memory volume procurable at reasonable prices and states that unit sales / promotional plans can be adjusted to manage hardware profitability.[^14]

That creates one of the cleanest current end-system monitoring chains:

> **memory procurement → hardware volume / promotion → profitability**

TrendForce's 2026 console-shipment / memory-BOM estimates remain B-grade quantitative context and do not override Sony's first-party identity.[^15]

### Dashboard J update direction

**Residual pressure strengthening:**

- memory / storage / component contract prices rise;
- allocation fulfillment deteriorates;
- promotion / configuration / SKU plans are adjusted;
- long-life part EOL / lead-time risk rises;
- end-system vendors explicitly link procurement quantity, price, or supply assurance to hardware cadence.

**Residual pressure weakening:**

- allocation fulfillment improves;
- contract price / lead time declines persist;
- same-price configuration progress resumes;
- long-life supply continuity improves;
- supplier product mix rebalances;
- qualified second-source options expand.

Dashboard J does not independently determine S1–S4. It tests whether:

> **upstream scarcity / concentration is actually transmitting into end-system upgrade tax and supply-assurance pressure.**

## 11. Evidence-weighting rules

**Table 29.4 — Default evidence grade for report updates**

| Data type | Default grade |
|---|---|
| company filing / official product / government / court / formal contract | A |
| institutional research | B |
| sell-side / supply-chain / media estimate | C |
| report geometry / stress test / hypothetical rack count | D |

**Source:** Report evidence method (see Chapter 27).  
**Note:** A default grade can move with source quality, but repeated citation does not upgrade it automatically.

Every update preserves the original evidence grade.

A newer number does not automatically become stronger evidence.

## 12. Scenario-reweighting rules

### Move toward S1

System substitution expands, migration cost falls, HBM/packaging/power supply elasticity improves, and multi-buyer access to scarce resources strengthens.

### Move toward S3

Seller commitments keep expanding, multi-layer scarcity persists, switching costs rise, and powered capacity is locked for long periods by a small set of Anchor Buyers.

### Move toward S4

Multiple independent families among utilization/revenue, market clearing, forward commitments, demand-financing quality, and accounting deterioration cross-confirm one another with sufficient causal breadth. Accounting strengthens severity when present but is not required.

### Remain in S2

Dominant-platform economics stay strong while alternative stacks continue developing; scarce layers remain tight but keep expanding; CapEx remains high while ROI is still serviceable.

## 12A. Materiality and persistence follow each variable's clock

The report does **not** impose one universal rule such as "two quarters" or "three months" across all variables.

That would create false precision because semiconductor allocation, memory pricing, power projects, regulatory proceedings, customer contracts, and accounting losses update on different clocks.

Instead, every monitoring variable should specify:

1. **source cadence** — quarterly filing, monthly pricing series, event-driven contract, annual capacity disclosure, etc.;
2. **physical / economic clock** — hardware, fab, data center, grid, contract, or accounting;
3. **comparable baseline** — same denominator, same unit, same evidence identity;
4. **materiality** — large enough to change the relevant mechanism rather than merely create headline volatility;
5. **persistence / confirmation** — supported by comparable follow-up evidence appropriate to that variable;
6. **cross-signal confirmation** — especially for S4, where one weak metric is insufficient.

**Mechanism-break override:** breadth requirements govern **system-level scenario adjudication**, not whether an underlying mechanism is allowed to survive direct falsification. If a high-quality observation, on the same denominator and time identity, directly breaks a necessary premise of a mechanism, that mechanism must be downgraded, rewritten, or retired immediately even when cross-signal breadth is not yet present. Breadth determines whether the whole S1–S4 state changes; it cannot be used to ignore a broken premise.

### Handling missing data

If a layer does not provide same-unit `AI_Effective_Demand` and `AI_Effective_Capacity`, its Absolute AI Capacity Gap must be labeled **not measurable from public data**.

Growth Gap, lead time, pricing, and allocation fulfillment may be tracked as proxies, but they cannot be renamed as an absolute capacity gap.

### Scenario reweighting

- **Toward S1:** supply elasticity, system substitution, buyer optionality, and portability improve in mutually reinforcing ways;
- **Toward S3:** multi-layer scarcity, switching cost, quantity assurance concentration, and forward reservation strengthen together;
- **Toward S4:** multiple causally independent signal families deteriorate broadly and persistently across actors/layers; at least one real-economy / market / commitment signal must be present, while Accounting is confirmatory rather than mandatory;
- **Otherwise:** keep S2 as the baseline or make only local weighting adjustments rather than changing the main scenario because of one headline.

## 12B. Five headline inflection tests

For readers who want a fast way to judge whether the long-run structure is changing, Dashboards A–J can be compressed into five headline tests. These are not a new model and they do not impose one universal numeric threshold.

1. **Is Non-AI Residual Capacity / Quantity Assurance improving or deteriorating persistently?**  
   Track contract prices, allocation fulfillment, lead times, configuration, promotion, and qualified alternates across smartphones, PCs, game consoles, automotive / industrial systems, rather than relying on gross global wafer or memory capacity.

2. **Has a second System Stack crossed a real production-workload threshold?**  
   Track production-workload share, external availability, migration cost, utilization, and system TCO for Google / AWS / Microsoft / Meta custom systems. A single accelerator benchmark or product launch does not establish system-level substitution.

3. **Is China's Parallel Stack improving both production-grade economics and autonomous closure?**  
   Track real workloads, unit economics, software/runtime, and cluster utilization alongside advanced logic, HBM-class memory, advanced packaging, and equipment / materials / service continuity. A usable production system and a self-sustaining multi-generation frontier stack remain separate tests.

4. **Can utilization, monetization, and independent financing capacity cover the expanding capital base?**  
   Track revenue / utilization, used-system and lease clearing, forward commitments, customer cash generation, third-party risk capital, supplier support, and refinancing dependency. Duration mismatch becomes more dangerous when technical asset life is shorter than financing duration.

5. **Does Power / LPS actually convert from secured to energized and utilized?**  
   The key metric is not headline GW. It is the conversion speed from Contracted / Secured → Permitted → Construction / Transition → Energized → Utilized, and whether Time-to-Power continues to create material delivery differences.

These five tests deliberately avoid arbitrary universal rules such as “eight quarters” or “30%–40%.” A material scenario reweighting requires **comparable continuous observation plus confirmation from at least two independent mechanism dimensions**.

## 13. Governed update protocol

One news item should not rewrite the entire report.

Scenario weighting changes only when new evidence changes a core mechanism variable with materiality and persistence appropriate to that variable's own reporting and asset clock.

This keeps the report updateable without rewriting the industry narrative after every product launch, stock-price move, or rumor.

[^12]: Kairos Power / Google partnership, Hermes 2 up to 50 MWe in 2030 and fleet up to 500 MWe by 2035, https://www.kairospower.com/google
[^13]: NVIDIA, PORTS-Pike Technology Campus / SB Energy / OpenAI, 2026-08-17, initial 4.25 IT-GW design capacity and phased online plan beginning in 2028, https://investor.nvidia.com/news/press-release-details/2026/NVIDIA-Guarantees-SB-Energys-PORTS-Pike-Technology-Campus-in-Ohio-to-Exclusively-Host-NVIDIA-AI-Compute/default.aspx


[^14]: Sony Group, FY2025 Q4 earnings speech / FY2026 outlook, PS5 hardware-sales / promotion management tied to memory procurement at reasonable prices, https://www.sony.com/en/SonyInfo/IR/library/presen/er/pdf/25q4_sonyspeech.pdf
[^15]: TrendForce, 2025-12-02, 2026 game-console shipment forecast and estimated console memory-cost share of BOM, B-grade institutional forecast, https://www.trendforce.com/presscenter/news/20251202-12811.html
