# Part IV · Chapter 30: Scenario Framework — Discriminating S1 to S4

## 1. Scenarios are adjudication states, not stories

The framework does not assign one deterministic 2030 or 2036 outcome, and it does not attach fixed probabilities to S1–S4.

Its purpose is operational: **given the evidence now, which structural state best fits the system, which variables discriminate the alternatives, and what observations would force a reweighting?**

Scenario weighting therefore depends on state variables that can be monitored:

- layer-specific scarce-capacity access;
- HBM / advanced-packaging supply;
- second-stack substitution;
- Anchor Buyer commitments;
- power delivery / Time-to-Power;
- software migration;
- China closure tests;
- utilization / monetization;
- financing quality;
- regulation / geopolitics.

## 2. S1 — Competitive Expansion: supply and substitution outrun concentration

### Structural condition

AI demand keeps growing, but supply expansion and alternative systems mature fast enough to reduce concentration.

Typical conditions include:

- leading-edge capacity expands with more qualified supply;
- HBM supply elasticity improves;
- advanced-packaging tightness eases materially;
- cloud custom systems carry large production workloads;
- open software / interfaces reduce migration cost;
- energized power keeps pace with buildout;
- multiple system stacks coexist in production over long periods.

### Economic result

The dominant platform can remain very large while buyer optionality and bargaining power improve.

Scarcity premiums decline and value capture becomes more distributed.

This is:

> **market expands + concentration declines**

not "AI infrastructure disappears."

### 2A. Supply-response overshoot can accelerate S1

The Rolling Bottleneck Cascade does not imply that the same scarce layer remains short for a decade. Fabs, memory, and packaging all have long construction and qualification cycles. **High prices and high margins can induce several suppliers to expand at the same time; if effective output arrives in a similar window while demand growth decelerates, a layer can move rapidly from shortage to headroom or even oversupply.**

Public forward evidence at the report cutoff already shows this response can be **layer-specific rather than uniform**. TrendForce on July 30, 2026 expected DRAM to remain tight in 2027 while NAND Flash could move toward a looser supply-demand structure in **2H27** as new capacity and bit-output growth arrive; it also expected meaningful output contribution from some new DRAM capacity to emerge only in **2028**.[^10]

The report therefore does not extrapolate 2024–2026 HBM / packaging tightness into permanent ten-year scarcity. The variables to monitor by layer are:

- effective capacity ramp;
- qualification / yield;
- demand-growth deceleration;
- lead time / clearing price;
- utilization;
- product-mix reversal.

If supply growth persistently exceeds demand growth across several scarce layers while lead times and scarcity premiums decline, S1 weight should rise materially. If that easing is accompanied by weakening utilization and clearing prices, S4 weight can rise as well.

## 3. S2 — Baseline Concentration: growth and concentration coexist

### Definition

The current core trends continue:

- AI demand remains high;
- leading system-platform seller economics remain strong;
- HBM / advanced packaging remain periodically tight;
- cloud custom systems continue expanding without fully replacing frontier workloads;
- power becomes an independent deployment constraint;
- China's frontier parallel stack keeps iterating with partial production deployment, while a complete production-grade frontier closure remains unconfirmed by public evidence;
- CapEx / forward commitments remain high.

### Economic result

The market continues expanding rapidly while value capture remains concentrated in a small number of system platforms and scarce-layer suppliers.

For traditional end systems, S2 is not merely background: **high growth and high concentration can coexist with tighter Non-AI Residual Capacity, differentiated quantity assurance, slower configuration upgrades, or an upgrade tax.** This does not mean every end market is shrinking; it means the benefits of growth and the burden of resource pressure can sit at different points in the value chain.

As of August 2026, this is the **baseline state** that best matches the current evidence mix.

It is not a fixed ten-year forecast.

## 4. S3 — Resource Hegemony: multi-layer concentration becomes self-reinforcing

### Definition

Demand growth continues to outrun substitution and supply expansion while multiple scarce layers remain concentrated at the same time.

Typical conditions include:

- advanced packaging stays structurally tight;
- HBM manufacturing intensity / generation constraints persist;
- leading-edge incremental capacity remains highly concentrated;
- proprietary software / interconnect migration costs stay high;
- cloud custom systems divert only part of the workload;
- powered land / firm MW are locked for long periods by a small set of buyers;
- Anchor Buyer and seller forward commitments continue expanding.

### Reinforcement mechanism

> high profit → stronger commitments / prepayment → greater future resource reservation → higher delivery certainty → stronger installed base / workload retention

This describes stronger system-level resource coordination.

It is not a legal monopoly verdict.

## 5. S4 — Capital Hard Landing: capital formation outruns productive demand

### Definition

AI workload demand, utilization, and monetization fail to cover a rapidly expanding capital base.

Typical synchronized signals include:

- utilization and effective AI revenue fall behind the installed capital base;
- instance / lease prices and secondary-market values weaken;
- enterprise AI monetization remains weak;
- hyperscaler CapEx guidance falls;
- seller / Anchor Buyer commitments shrink, reschedule, or cancel;
- impairment / write-down rises;
- customer cash generation weakens while supplier equity / credit support or refinancing dependency rises;
- independent third-party risk capital becomes less willing to absorb exposure.

These are **typical signal families, not a mandatory five-of-five gate**. S4 confirmation depends on cross-signal confirmation plus cross-actor / cross-layer causal breadth. Accounting impairment is confirmatory / severity evidence, not a prerequisite; financing-led or real-economy clearing can be confirmed before qualifying write-down evidence appears.

### Clearing order

The first pressure usually appears in:

- next-generation hardware orders;
- inventory;
- short-lived system residual value;
- speculative / highly concentrated financing.

If weakness persists, the pressure can move into semiconductor CapEx, data-center expansion, and power buildout.

S4 does not mean AI usage disappears.

After capital clearing, cheaper compute can still stimulate new applications.

## 5.1 Comparative scenario matrix

**Table 30.1 — Core drivers, primary outcomes, and reverse signals for S1–S4**

| Scenario | Core drivers | Primary industry outcome | Key reverse signal |
|---|---|---|---|
| S1 — Competitive Expansion | higher supply elasticity, multi-sourcing, and portability | more distributed value capture and greater buyer optionality | scarce-layer tightness strengthens again or migration costs rise |
| S2 — Baseline Concentration | strong demand, strong leading platforms, substitution develops but remains incomplete | high growth and concentration coexist | supply/substitution improve materially or ROI/commitments deteriorate together |
| S3 — Resource Hegemony | multi-layer scarcity + switching costs + forward reservation strengthen | incremental capacity becomes more concentrated and replication costs rise | capacity access broadens and open interfaces materially lower migration costs |
| S4 — Capital Hard Landing | utilization / monetization and demand-financing quality fall short of the capital base | clearing prices, orders, CapEx, asset values, and financing quality weaken | utilization, clearing prices, independent financing, and forward commitments recover |

**Source:** Report S1–S4 scenario framework.  
**Note:** The four scenarios are parallel conditional states. They do not carry fixed probabilities and are not inevitable sequential stages.

## 5A. Capacity Gap is a diagnostic axis, not a fifth scenario

S1–S4 remain parallel conditional states; the report does not introduce a second scenario taxonomy. To separate “is the layer short today?” from “is the pressure widening or converging?”, it uses two complementary metrics.

### Absolute gap

```text
AI_Capacity_Gap_i(t)
= AI_Effective_Demand_i(t)
- AI_Effective_Capacity_i(t)
```

Demand and capacity must be measured within the same physical layer, delivery window, and qualification/quality boundary. Units can include wafers/month, qualified package starts/month, bits/year, or MW.

- **Absolute Gap > 0:** current deliverable supply is insufficient;
- **Absolute Gap ≈ 0:** current supply and demand are near balance;
- **Absolute Gap < 0:** current effective headroom exists.

### Growth gap

```text
AI_Capacity_Growth_Gap_i(t)
= Growth(AI_Effective_Demand_i(t))
- Growth(AI_Effective_Capacity_i(t))
```

The Growth Gap is a same-period growth-rate difference and describes whether the absolute gap is more likely to widen or converge:

- **Growth Gap > 0:** scarcity may widen, or existing headroom may be eroded;
- **Growth Gap < 0:** scarcity may converge, or effective headroom may expand;
- **Growth Gap = 0:** supply and demand are changing at the same rate; it does not mean no shortage exists.

Therefore:

- **Absolute Gap > 0 and Growth Gap > 0 across several critical layers:** scarcity is strengthening and relative weight shifts toward S2 / S3;
- **Absolute Gap > 0 but Growth Gap < 0:** the layer remains short, but supply is catching up and S1 weight can rise;
- **Absolute Gap < 0 with healthy utilization:** supply has headroom, but this is not S4 by itself;
- **Absolute Gap < 0 + falling utilization + weaker clearing prices + shrinking commitments:** S4 weight rises materially.

AI Capacity Gap / Growth Gap must be read together with Non-AI Residual Capacity. The first two ask how much AI currently lacks and how that gap is changing; NARC asks how much effective supply remains for non-AI demand after priority allocation.

## 6. Six variable classes discriminate the scenarios

### Supply

Leading-edge wafers, HBM, advanced packaging, power.

### Substitution

Cloud custom systems, open compiler/runtime, alternative interconnect, China's parallel stack.

### Demand

Token/workflow volume, enterprise adoption, Anchor Buyer commitments, sovereign demand.

### Capital / Monetization

Seller cash generation, hyperscaler CapEx, depreciation, utilization, workload revenue, residual value.

### Financing Quality

Anchor Buyer credit quality, third-party risk capital, supplier equity / credit support, refinancing dependency, asset transferability.

### Regulation / Geopolitics

Export controls, antitrust remedies, interface openness, industrial policy, regional fragmentation.

## 7. Anchor Buyers and financing quality can amplify expansion or clearing

Multi-year compute commitments from large model companies can influence infrastructure financing, custom silicon, power, and data-center buildout.

The 2026 financing layer is now observable in first-party disclosures: NVIDIA has announced compute-financing platforms targeting **more than US$500B of third-party capital mobilization over time**, and PORTS-Pike adds seller credit support around initial **4.25 IT-GW** design capacity.[^7][^8]

If Anchor Buyer demand expands and converts into real utilization, S2/S3 weighting rises.

If long-duration commitments are delayed, renegotiated, or underutilized at scale, S4 weighting rises.

Model-company contracts therefore cannot be treated as ordinary cloud-customer spending.

Seller-assisted financing can strengthen infrastructure formation when it is supported by real utilization, monetization, and independent capital.

It becomes an S4 risk amplifier when customer cash generation weakens while supplier equity / credit support and refinancing dependency keep increasing.

The report therefore distinguishes Anchor Buyer demand from **Financing Reflexivity** rather than treating every financing-supported order as either fully organic or automatically artificial.

## 8. Signals that move S2 toward S1

- alternative system stacks scale in frontier / high-value production workloads;
- HBM / packaging lead times decline sustainably;
- access to advanced capacity becomes more distributed;
- open software / interfaces reduce migration cost materially;
- power expansion shortens Time-to-Power;
- buyer multi-sourcing becomes normal production practice.

## 9. Signals that move S2 toward S3

- seller forward commitments keep expanding rapidly;
- HBM / packaging tightness persists across generations;
- proprietary-system switching cost rises;
- leading-edge incremental supply remains highly concentrated;
- scarce power / powered land is locked by a small set of Anchor Buyers;
- alternative stacks fail to create enough production-scale optionality.

## 10. Signals that move S2 toward S4

- CapEx guidance declines repeatedly;
- utilization falls;
- seller / Anchor Buyer commitments cancel or shrink materially;
- second-hand system clearing prices fall quickly;
- cloud discounts / lease rates deteriorate;
- impairment rises;
- enterprise AI monetization remains persistently below depreciation / operating cost;
- supplier equity / credit support rises while independent customer financing / third-party risk-taking weakens.

## 11. These are states, not stages

They are parallel conditional states.

One region can experience S3-style power scarcity while selected workloads already operate in S1-style multi-stack competition.

Memory can temporarily loosen while packaging remains tight.

Capital markets can clear locally while productive workloads continue to grow.

Scenario weighting therefore describes **relative structure**, not one compulsory industry path.

## 12. Current baseline: S2

As of August 2026, the report's baseline is:

> **S2 — Baseline Concentration**

because:

- NVIDIA / leading-platform seller economics remain strong;[^1]
- HBM and advanced packaging remain important bottlenecks;[^2][^3]
- cloud custom systems are real competitors but have not shown comprehensive frontier-workload substitution;[^4]
- infrastructure CapEx and forward commitments remain high;[^5]
- power constraints are rising;[^6]
- China has real frontier parallel alternatives and partial production deployment, but under Chapter 17's seven Production-Grade Gates a complete production-grade frontier closure remains unconfirmed by public evidence;
- synchronized evidence of an industry-wide Capital Hard Landing remains insufficient.

The baseline remains valid only while this evidence mix remains valid; the discrimination rules are in Section 12A.

## 12A. Discrimination rules

Scenarios are distinguished along the following axes rather than by any single aggregate metric:

- **S1 ↔ S2:** the degree to which alternative systems scale in frontier / high-value production workloads, and whether HBM / packaging lead times keep falling;
- **S2 ↔ S3:** the direction of allocation concentration at scarce layers, the growth rate of seller forward commitments, proprietary switching costs, and the degree of powered-land lockup;
- **S3 ↔ S4:** the ratio of utilization / monetization to the capital base — not publicly disclosed, so judged through public proxies: clearing prices (used-system prices, lease rates, cloud discounts), commitment growth, the share of seller equity / credit support, third-party capital risk premia, and refinancing dependency.

The same capital evidence (for example the **>US$500B** mobilization target, the **US$50B** frontier-lab investment, and **2 GW** of credit support)[^7][^8][^9] supports S2/S3 infrastructure coordination while the proxy signals are healthy, and shifts weight toward S4 when the proxies deteriorate broadly and persistently; the judgment never rests on a single data point.

Boundary case: if demand growth slows materially without producing sufficient cross-signal confirmation and causal breadth across multiple S4 signal families, assignment follows substitution strength — expanding substitution raises S1 weight, while persistent scarce-layer tightness places the state in S2 (reading "high demand" as "demand growth still above effective-supply growth").

## 12B. Calibration status

As of the report cutoff, the S1–S4 discrimination axes are explicit, but there is **no statistically validated universal threshold, fixed observation window, or automatic state-switch rule**.

Therefore:

- one CapEx cut cannot independently trigger S4;
- one packaging / HBM tightness signal cannot independently trigger S3;
- one alternative-chip launch cannot independently trigger S1;
- S2 cannot become a permanent default merely because most observations can still be narrated inside it.

Changes in scenario weight require **several independent public proxies to move in the same direction across repeated observations**, while counterevidence and segment breadth remain explicit. Because disclosure frequencies differ across quarterly, semiannual, and annual data, this report does not force them into a false single observation window.

**Mechanism-break override:** breadth requirements govern **system-level scenario adjudication**, not whether an underlying mechanism is allowed to survive direct falsification. If a high-quality observation, on the same denominator and time identity, directly breaks a necessary premise of a mechanism, that mechanism must be downgraded, rewritten, or retired immediately even when cross-signal breadth is not yet present. Breadth determines whether the whole S1–S4 state changes; it cannot be used to ignore a broken premise.

In other words:

> **The framework already specifies what to observe, but it does not pretend that “how much” and “for how long” have already been statistically calibrated.**

Historical backtesting, lead/lag analysis, and threshold calibration belong to later empirical validation. Until then, S1–S4 should be read as an updateable structural discrimination framework, not a mechanical classifier.

---

## 13. How to use the framework

Future updates should not ask:

> "Which ten-year prediction was proven correct?"

They should ask:

> **Which observable variables changed, and how should S1–S4 relative weighting adjust?**

That keeps long-run judgment responsive to industrial evidence rather than dependent on a one-time bet.

# Part IV · Chapter 31: Strategic Implications Under Alternative AI-Infrastructure States

## 1. Strategy is about preserving executable optionality

This chapter does not optimize for one 2036 forecast. It asks which options remain valuable when concentration, substitution, scarcity, and capital clearing move in different directions.

The competitive unit is already a system stack spanning advanced logic, HBM, advanced packaging, interconnect, compiler/runtime, racks/pods, data centers, power, capital, and sustainable workload demand.

The strategic question is therefore:

> **Which capabilities preserve bargaining power, continuity, and capital flexibility across S1–S4 rather than only under today's S2 baseline?**

This reframes strategy away from predicting one permanent winner and toward maintaining executable options under changing system conditions.

## 2. Five structural variables define the strategic choice set

The long-run industry structure depends primarily on five questions.

**Table 31.1 — Core structural variables used in the long-run strategic judgment**

| Structural variable | Source chapter |
|---|---|
| NVIDIA system stack / switching cost | [Part I · Ch02](part-I-ch02-nvidia-system-stack.md) |
| HBM / memory resource intensity | [Part I · Ch05](part-I-ch05-hbm-memory.md) |
| Advanced Packaging / CoWoS | [Part I · Ch06](part-I-ch06-advanced-packaging.md) |
| Power / Time-to-Power | [Part I · Ch07](part-I-ch07-power-constraint.md) |
| Hyperscaler second system stack | [Part II · Ch12](part-II-ch12-hyperscaler-system-stack.md) |
| China frontier parallel stack / Production-Grade Gates / autonomous frontier closure | [Part II · Ch17](part-II-ch17-china-parallel-stack.md) |
| Value Capture Map | [Part III · Ch20](part-III-ch20-value-capture-map.md) |
| Capital clearing / S4 signals | [Part III · Ch24](part-III-ch24-capital-clearing.md) |

**Source:** Report chapter architecture.  
**Note:** This chapter inherits facts and mechanisms established earlier rather than duplicating high-impact factual tables and physical formulas.

### 2.1 System Portability

Can workloads move between system stacks at acceptable cost?

If compiler/runtime layers, model formats, networking, and orchestration become more open, buyer optionality rises.

If proprietary interfaces and platform-specific engineering deepen, switching cost becomes a larger industrial barrier.

### 2.2 Scarce-Layer Supply Elasticity

Do advanced logic, HBM, and advanced packaging develop more qualified supply?

Higher supply elasticity reduces concentration pressure.

If several scarce layers remain tight at the same time, system platforms and large Anchor Buyers gain a stronger supply-coordination advantage.

### 2.3 Time-to-Power

Do powered land, grid connection, substations, and firm power remain deployment quotas?

If Time-to-Power stays long, AI competition becomes increasingly infrastructural and geographic.

### 2.4 Workload Monetization

Do AI workloads generate enough cash flow to cover:

- compute depreciation;
- data-center cost;
- power;
- operations;
- financing?

This is the fundamental variable that determines whether capital expansion is economically sustainable.

### 2.5 Financing Independence / Demand Quality

Whose balance sheet ultimately supports long-duration compute demand?

By August 2026, compute financing had expanded beyond corporate CapEx into third-party capital, LPS credit support, and seller-assisted financing.[^4][^5]

The report therefore separates:

- independently financed customer demand;
- long-duration Anchor Buyer commitments;
- third-party infrastructure capital;
- supplier equity / credit enhancement.

If utilization and monetization remain healthy, seller-assisted financing can reduce capital-formation friction. If customer cash generation weakens while supplier support and refinancing dependence rise, the system becomes more dependent on a small number of balance sheets.

The long-run question is:

> **Can AI infrastructure evolve from expansion that requires continuing seller / sponsor support into an asset system underwritten by independent workload cash flow and long-duration capital?**

The full S1–S4 definitions belong in [Chapter 30](part-IV-ch30-scenarios.md).

### 2A. The same asset or capability changes value across S1–S4

Scenario analysis matters because it changes which options should be preserved today.

**S1–S4 strategic crosswalk**

| Scenario | Industry structure | Enterprise priority | Capital priority | National / industrial-policy priority |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| S1 — Competitive Expansion | multiple system stacks coexist; supply elasticity and portability improve | multi-stack execution; price/performance optimization | applications/workflows and differentiated suppliers | interoperability, competition, multi-sourcing |
| S2 — Baseline Concentration | high growth and high concentration coexist while substitution paths mature | primary-stack efficiency + executable second path | system platforms / scarce layers, with strict duration discipline | expand key physical layers and reduce single points of dependence |
| S3 — Resource Hegemony | multi-layer scarcity, quantity assurance, and switching cost strengthen together | secure supply / power early while maintaining migration readiness | premium on scarce-capacity access and coordination | frontier supply continuity, power, domestic / alternative stacks |
| S4 — Capital Hard Landing | utilization, clearing, and financing quality deteriorate together | avoid excessive long-duration reservation; prioritize variable cost and cash flow | residual value, debt, refinancing, distressed assets | prevent inefficient projects from trapping power/capital; use cheaper compute to diffuse applications |

**Source:** Report S1–S4 framework and this chapter's strategic synthesis.  
**Note:** This is not an asset-allocation recommendation and does not imply every geography or layer occupies the same scenario simultaneously.

The strategic difference can be compressed further:

- **S1:** optionality comes from portability;
- **S2:** optionality comes from primary-stack efficiency + a real second path;
- **S3:** optionality comes from early access to scarce capacity without complete lock-in to one stack;
- **S4:** optionality comes from cash flow, variable-cost structure, and the ability to reallocate cheaper assets after clearing.

The objective is not to predict one permanent winner. It is:

> **to keep an executable next move when the relative weight of S1–S4 changes.**

## 3. Under S2, efficiency is not enough; optionality matters

As of August 2026, the evidence mix remains closest to **S2 — Baseline Concentration**.

The main reasons are:

- leading system-platform economics remain strong;[^1]
- advanced packaging remains a publicly confirmed growth constraint;[^2]
- HBM / server memory continues to exert strong pressure on manufacturing resources;[^3]
- cloud custom systems are a real second route but have not demonstrated comprehensive replacement of frontier workloads;
- power has become an independent physical constraint;
- China's frontier parallel stack continues to iterate with partial production deployment, but under Chapter 17's seven Production-Grade Gates a complete production-grade frontier closure remains unconfirmed by public evidence;
- synchronized evidence of an industry-wide Capital Hard Landing remains insufficient.

This is not a fixed ten-year forecast.

It is the starting point for current strategic choices.

## 4. Enterprise: prove workflow economics before extending duration

A disciplined sequence is:

1. identify measurable real workflows;
2. establish evaluation / verification;
3. calculate cost per completed task and business value;
4. preserve model / cloud / data portability;
5. use longer compute commitments only after demand becomes sufficiently predictable.

High-certainty workloads can justify longer reservations.

Experimental or rapidly changing workloads should preserve flexible / multi-provider paths.

The dangerous sequence is to reserve large long-duration capacity first and search for enough workloads later.

## 5. Enterprise: price optionality explicitly

Portability does not require equal purchasing across all vendors.

A stronger principle is:

> **Optimize the primary stack for efficiency; price optionality explicitly, and maintain a production-like second path only when continuity / bargaining / recovery value exceeds its carrying cost.**

Even when a continuously maintained second production path is not justified, preserve the cheapest forms of reversibility available:

- model portability;
- data portability;
- cloud portability;
- accelerator / runtime optionality;
- open interfaces;
- workload benchmarks and cost observability.

Where a second path is maintained, it exists to reduce single-point supply, pricing, policy, and migration risk. A low-criticality workload in an abundant / highly portable market may rationally rely on reconstructible optionality instead of carrying a permanently validated duplicate path.

## 5A. End-system companies: manage Quantity Assurance, not price alone

For smartphones, PCs, game consoles, vehicles, industrial equipment, robotics, and other long-lifecycle electronics, the low-regret response is not to predict that one supplier will remain constrained for a decade. It is to improve resilience to resource reprioritization.

Three principles:

1. **Manage price and Quantity Assurance together.** Unit price is not enough; track allocation fulfillment, lead time, LTAs, validated alternates, and recovery time.
2. **Separate supply risk across product tiers.** Flagship / high-performance SKUs and cost-sensitive / long-life SKUs should not automatically depend on the identical scarce-resource path, or one high-end constraint can propagate across the whole portfolio.
3. **Identify long-life part continuity early.** For components with long qualification cycles and high replacement costs, continuously assess whether suppliers still have the economics to maintain long-duration supply, packaging/test, tooling, and engineering support.

This is not a recommendation to maximize inventory.

The objective is:

> **keep the end-product cadence from becoming fully subordinated to the resource clock of the tightest upstream demand pool.**

---

## 6. Capital: classify the asset before valuation

At least four asset classes should remain separate:

**Table 31.2 — Primary value sources and risks across AI-related asset classes**

| Asset | Primary value | Primary risk |
|---|---|---|
| Platform | switching cost / system definition / ecosystem | substitution / regulation |
| Manufacturing | scarcity / yield / technology / capacity | cycle / CapEx / utilization |
| Infrastructure | powered land / grid / data center / duration | utilization / duration mismatch |
| Application | workflow / distribution / data | commoditization / unit economics |

**Source:** Report Value Capture / Duration framework.  
**Note:** The classification is for strategic analysis and is not an investment rating or valuation recommendation.

High CapEx can be an entry barrier and preparation for growth, or a source of future depreciation and stranded assets.

Capital analysis therefore requires CapEx, utilization, revenue, FCF, forward commitments, and clearing prices together.

## 7. Capital: duration mismatch is the structural risk

Short-cycle compute demand can be matched against long-duration debt, PPAs, data-center shells, and transmission assets.

If workload demand disappoints, these assets clear much more slowly than hardware inventory.

The central question is:

> **Does demand duration match asset duration?**

Under high demand, long-duration assets can be a moat.

Under S4, the same assets can become long-lived burdens.

## 8. Industrial policy: the competitive unit is the system, not one fab

A complete frontier-AI industrial capability requires at least:

- equipment / materials;
- advanced logic;
- HBM;
- advanced packaging;
- interconnect;
- compiler/runtime;
- data centers;
- power / grid;
- talent / production operations.

Subsidizing one advanced fab therefore does not create system closure.

Manufacturing, memory, packaging, software, and power have to be managed as one industrial strategy.

## 9. Industrial policy: power policy is now compute policy

High-power racks pull grid planning, generation, interconnection, substations, cooling, and permitting into technology competition.

If chip supply expands while Time-to-Power does not improve, the result can be "hardware available, deployment delayed."

A national AI-infrastructure strategy therefore has to manage:

> **semiconductor clock + power-permitting clock + capital clock**

at the same time.

## 10. Industrial policy: interoperability creates optionality

System resilience does not require rejecting the leading platform.

It requires avoiding a state in which every critical layer depends on one irreplaceable entry point.

Long-run policy should improve:

- open compiler/runtime;
- portable model / data formats;
- interoperable networking;
- multi-architecture engineering education;
- cross-stack production benchmarking.

The objective is not to equalize market share artificially.

It is to make substitution executable when supply, policy, or pricing conditions change.

## 11. China: the long-run issue is two-level closure

Whether China's parallel stack changes the global resource structure does not depend on one localization ratio.

The first level is **Production-Grade Parallel Stack**:

- accelerator / compute;
- HBM-class or equivalent memory subsystem;
- advanced packaging;
- high-speed interconnect;
- compiler/runtime;
- production workload;
- utilization / reliability / system economics.

The second level is **Autonomous Frontier Closure**:

- advanced-manufacturing continuity;
- equipment / materials / software / service;
- cross-generation process upgrades;
- long-duration HBM / packaging / LPS / power / capital continuity.

Consumer / smartphone supply-chain strength does not prove autonomous frontier closure.

But import dependence also does not invalidate production-grade substitution that is already operating in real workloads.

If the production-grade stack keeps expanding, China becomes a stronger second center of AI Resource Gravity.

If upstream continuity also closes across generations, the strategic meaning rises from domestic resilience / selected substitution toward a higher degree of autonomous frontier competition.

## 12. Labor and talent: value moves toward responsibility for outcomes

Models, IDEs, agents, and generation tools can commoditize quickly.

More durable capabilities therefore include:

- domain knowledge;
- problem decomposition;
- system / workflow design;
- verification;
- cross-stack portability;
- risk judgment;
- responsibility for final outcomes.

The industrial logic is the same:

> **As execution cost falls, the value of defining the problem, composing the system, verifying the result, and owning the outcome rises.**

The labor and education implications are developed in Chapters 25 and 26.

---

## 13. What would change strategic priorities

The most important update variables include:

- whether major system-seller commitments keep expanding or begin to contract;
- production workload share and migration cost for cloud custom stacks;
- whether HBM / advanced-packaging tightness materially eases;
- whether leading-edge capacity access becomes more distributed;
- whether energized MW / Time-to-Power improves;
- whether Anchor Compute Buyer commitments convert into real delivery;
- whether AI monetization covers the expanding capital base;
- whether China's frontier parallel stack gains more scaled first-party evidence across the Production-Grade Gates / Autonomous Frontier Closure;
- whether supplier-assisted financing remains supported by independent workload cash flow, third-party capital, and high utilization;
- whether regulation materially lowers interface / tying / switching barriers.

These variables determine the relative weight of S1–S4 rather than a fixed ten-year forecast.

---

## 14. Strategic bottom line

As of August 2026, AI infrastructure remains in a phase where:

> **rapid expansion + high concentration + accelerating formation of substitute systems**

coexist.

The next decade is not primarily about guessing one permanent winner. It is about repeated redistribution of industrial power and capital returns across:

- system substitution;
- scarce-layer supply;
- power;
- Anchor Buyer demand;
- workload monetization;
- capital duration / financing quality;
- regulation;
- geopolitical redundancy.

The common principle for enterprises, capital, and national strategy can be compressed into one sentence:

> **Do not bet on an unchangeable end-state; build options that remain executable when the end-state changes.**

---

## 15. Falsification boundary

Long-run strategic priorities should be re-ranked if:

- multi-stack portability moves from a contingency option into normal production practice;
- scarce-layer supply reaches durable abundance;
- Time-to-Power shortens materially;
- workload monetization independently covers capital cost;
- supplier-assisted financing declines while independent long-duration capital rises;
- China's frontier parallel stack crosses additional Production-Grade Gates / Autonomous Frontier Closure critical layers.

This chapter does not offer a fixed ten-year end-state. It offers a set of strategic priorities that must change when these variables change.

---

# Part IV · Chapter 32: Master View — What Determines AI Infrastructure Power, and What Would Change the View

## 1. Current baseline and load-bearing judgments

**Current baseline (evidence cutoff: August 28, 2026): S2 — Baseline Concentration.**

The industry is not in a state of uncontested resource hegemony, nor is it in broad capital clearing. A small number of system platforms and scarce-layer suppliers retain strong economics; credible alternatives are developing, but they have not yet removed the physical, software, financing, and migration constraints that sustain concentration.

The report's master view rests on eight judgments:

1. **The competitive unit is the system stack, not the accelerator.** Advanced logic, HBM, advanced packaging, interconnect, runtime, rack/pod architecture, power, and production workloads have to close together.
2. **AI/HPC is changing the marginal destination of semiconductor and infrastructure capital.** The question is increasingly which demand pool determines what the next unit of capacity is built for.
3. **Traditional end systems face residual supply, not gross supply.** Their economics depend on qualified availability, quantity assurance, product mix, and lifecycle constraints after priority demand is allocated.
4. **Scarce-capacity access must be evaluated by physical layer.** Historical share cannot substitute for qualified access to incremental logic, HBM, packaging, networking, and energized power.
5. **Substitution can reduce platform rent before it removes upstream scarcity.** Hyperscaler custom stacks and China's parallel stack can carry real workloads while still depending on many of the same scarce inputs.
6. **Capital risk is a utilization, monetization, and financing-quality problem.** CapEx, supply commitments, and seller-assisted financing are leading signals; none proves healthy independent end demand by itself.
7. **Long-run demand is validated at the workflow level.** AI inherits a large digital installed base, but infrastructure investment only earns its way through utilization, verified output, and cash flow.
8. **The 2026–2036 path is conditional.** Supply response, algorithmic efficiency, second-stack adoption, China stack closure, power delivery, financing quality, and capital clearing can move the industry toward S1, S3, or S4.

The central question is therefore:

> **Who can secure qualified scarce inputs, organize them into a production system, and convert that capital base into durable workload economics?**

That question is deliberately broader than "who wins the AI chip war?" and narrower than a claim that one company or country already controls the entire infrastructure stack.

## 2. System coordination is the relevant form of industrial power

NVIDIA's current advantage is not ownership of every physical input. It is the ability to organize GPUs, CPUs, HBM, NVLink, CUDA/runtime, rack-scale architecture, supply-chain commitments, and large workloads into a mature general-purpose commercial system.

NVIDIA's manufacturing, supply, and capacity commitments increased from **US$95.2B** on January 25, 2026 to **US$279B** on July 26, 2026.[^1] The filing does not disclose a supplier-level split, so the aggregate cannot be treated as confirmed revenue for TSMC, CoWoS, or any single HBM vendor.

The correct long-run metric is:

> **Layer-specific Incremental Scarce-Capacity Access: whether a platform can repeatedly obtain enough qualified, stable, deliverable supply across advanced logic, HBM, packaging, networking, and LPS/power.**

In severe shortage conditions, price is not the only allocation mechanism. Long-term agreements, credit quality, prepayments, purchase scale, and allocation fulfillment all affect quantity assurance.

Therefore:

> **willingness to pay ≠ guaranteed quantity**

The competition can consequently move from "who is willing to pay more?" toward "who can secure stable quantities, delivery timing, and system-level priority?"

The latest 2026 developments extend system coordination beyond semiconductor supply. NVIDIA announced compute-financing platforms with six large long-duration capital providers, targeting **more than US$500B of third-party capital mobilization over time**, and separately provided credit support for land, power, and shell at the PORTS-Pike project, whose initial deployment is designed for **4.25 IT-GW** with OpenAI as the customer.[^13][^14]

The first figure is a capital-mobilization target, not deployed capital. The second is future design capacity, not energized load.

Together they show that leading system-platform coordination is beginning to extend from chips into **physical infrastructure and capital formation**.

---

## 3. Marginal capital formation is shifting toward AI/HPC

One of the most important structural changes in 2026 is that marginal advanced-manufacturing and memory demand is becoming increasingly dependent on AI/HPC.

In TSMC's 2Q26 platform revenue mix, HPC represented **66%** and smartphone **22%**. Advanced technologies at 7 nm and below represented **77%** of wafer revenue. TSMC also raised its 2026 capital budget to **US$60B–64B**, with continued investment in advanced processes, packaging, and testing.[^6]

These numbers do not mean all HPC is AI, and they do not prove that Apple, Qualcomm, or any other specific customer has been displaced.

They support the narrower conclusion that:

> **High-growth HPC/AI workloads are materially influencing the direction of incremental advanced-manufacturing capital formation.**

Memory shows the same structural shift. Micron disclosed that, under same-node / same-bit conditions, HBM3E requires approximately **3× the wafer supply of DDR5**.[^2] TrendForce forecasts that HBM + RDIMM will represent approximately **51% of 2026 DRAM bit supply**.[^3]

The report describes this structure as a **Two-Speed Semiconductor Economy**:

> **The AI Infrastructure Economy and the Traditional Device Economy share upstream resources but differ in willingness to pay, order duration, prepayment capacity, BOM constraints, and the ability to induce new capital formation.**

Consumer electronics do not disappear. The structural change is that marginal-demand power is shifting toward hyperscalers, system-platform sellers, and Anchor Compute Buyers.

---

## 4. Bottlenecks rotate across independent physical pools

AI-system delivery depends on several independent physical layers.

### HBM

The important point is not simply HBM market share. It is manufacturing-resource intensity. Micron's approximately **3× wafer-supply trade ratio** indicates that equal-bit HBM3E can consume materially more wafer input than DDR5.[^2]

### Advanced Packaging

TSMC has explicitly stated that advanced-packaging capacity is tight and that packaging tightness has constrained customer growth.[^4]

More leading-edge front-end wafer capacity therefore does not automatically produce proportionally more deliverable AI systems.

### Power and LPS

Approximately **120 kW-class racks**, the approximately **835 MW** Crane restart, the Talen/AWS revised PPA with up to **1,920 MW** of contract quantity, and the Kairos/Google long-duration power-development path show that power has become an independent physical constraint.[^5]

The delivery state must remain explicit:

> **Announced → Contracted / Secured → Permitted → Construction / Transition → Energized → Utilized**

Generation MW/GW, PPA capacity, IT-GW, energized facility load, and utilized AI load are different physical quantities.

When one bottleneck eases, another can become dominant. This is the report's **Rolling Bottleneck Cascade**.

---

## 5. Residual capacity is the downstream denominator

Traditional end markets do not face gross worldwide supply. They face effective supply after priority demand has absorbed its share:

> **Non-AI Residual Capacity = Effective Supply − AI / Anchor Demand − Other Priority Demand**

This explains why both of the following can be true at the same time:

- global wafer, DRAM, and packaging capacity keeps expanding;
- smartphone, PC, automotive, or industrial buyers still face higher prices, weaker allocation, or slower configuration upgrades.

The correct transmission mechanism is therefore not "AI takes all chips."

It is:

> **High-value demand changes product mix, capital priority, and quantity assurance, which changes the effective residual supply available to non-AI markets.**

Lower-margin, lower-bargaining-power, shorter-duration buyers are generally more exposed than buyers with LTAs, scale, stronger balance sheets, and end-market pricing power.

---

## 6. Second stacks reduce platform rent before they eliminate scarcity

Google Ironwood, AWS Trainium, Microsoft Maia, and Meta MTIA show that hyperscaler competition is no longer simply "internal ASIC versus external GPU." It is increasingly **system stack versus system stack**.[^7]

This produces two effects that point in different directions:

1. **Platform-profit diversion:** some workloads no longer pay the full economics of an external platform.
2. **Upstream resource competition:** custom systems still require advanced logic, HBM, packaging, networking, and power.

Custom-silicon success therefore does not automatically mean upstream scarcity disappears.

The long-run variables that matter are:

- production workload share;
- migration cost;
- system utilization;
- external adoption;
- software portability;
- scale-up / scale-out efficiency;
- dependence on the same scarce upstream layers.

If second system stacks carry more production workload and migration cost falls, **S1 — Competitive Expansion** gains weight.

If alternative systems remain constrained by the same scarce inputs and proprietary software friction, S2/S3 gains weight.

---

## 7. Anchor Compute Buyers now influence infrastructure formation

OpenAI Stargate and Anthropic/AWS demonstrate that leading model companies are no longer simply cloud tenants.[^8]

Multi-year compute commitments can produce the following chain:

> **higher revenue visibility → greater financing certainty → earlier cloud / data-center / power / chip buildout → more stable capacity → larger workloads**

This is the **Anchor Compute Buyer Effect**.

The same mechanism can amplify capital-cycle risk. If long-duration demand is rescheduled, utilization disappoints, or commercial revenue fails to cover infrastructure capital cost, the certainty that originally supported construction can become stranded-capacity and duration-mismatch risk.

Model-company long-duration commitments have therefore become a leading indicator on par with hyperscaler CapEx and system-vendor supply commitments; their time-leadership still needs to be verified across more full cycles.

PORTS-Pike extends this model further. Anchor demand can now be combined with **platform credit support + infrastructure developer + long-duration lease** to form land, power, and shell ahead of delivered compute.[^14]

That structure can accelerate infrastructure formation while also increasing customer concentration and duration risk.

---

## 8. China must be evaluated through two closure tests

China's supply position cannot be reduced to one localization rate.

Current public evidence supports several distinct statements:

- consumer computing and smartphone supply chains have meaningful domestic capability;
- DRAM / NAND capability is expanding;
- PC / workstation substitution is growing but remains sensitive to software compatibility and professional workloads;
- a real accelerator/software parallel stack exists in frontier AI, but full autonomous closure is a separate question.

CXMT disclosures support conclusions about DDR/LPDDR and DRAM market position, but do not prove HBM-class frontier closure. Counterpoint's YMTC shipment-share and revenue-share figures must also retain their different denominators.[^9][^10][^11]

Foundry revenue share is similarly not a frontier-AI capability measure. TrendForce's **5.1%** SMIC figure for 1Q26 is foundry revenue share, not proof of frontier-system closure.[^12]

The report therefore uses two levels.

### Production-Grade Parallel Stack

China has moved beyond the stage of having no substitute system. Parts of the stack are already in real production deployment.

### Autonomous Frontier Closure

Public evidence is still insufficient to show that advanced manufacturing, HBM-class memory, advanced packaging, interconnect, compiler/runtime, equipment/material continuity, and cross-generation infrastructure scaling are all autonomous and sustainable at frontier scale.

The long-run question is therefore not the benchmark performance of one domestic accelerator.

It is whether production workloads continue expanding and whether the full stack can preserve performance, supply, power, and economics across generations when critical external inputs become constrained.

---

## 9. Capital-cycle risk lives in utilization and financing quality

Cloud CapEx, system-vendor revenue, foundry revenue, memory revenue, and power investment cannot be mechanically added into one "global AI investment" number because the same economic flow appears on different ledgers.

AI infrastructure combines assets with very different durations:

- accelerator / server: fast technological depreciation;
- network / cooling: medium duration;
- data-center shell: long duration;
- grid / substation / generation: still longer duration;
- future workload revenue: uncertain.

The report therefore uses five groups of capital-clearing signals:

- **Accounting:** impairment, write-downs, accelerated depreciation;
- **Utilization / Revenue:** deployed-compute utilization, AI revenue, verified workflow output;
- **Market Clearing:** lease rates, used-system prices, delivery lead times, inventory;
- **Forward Commitment:** CapEx guidance, supply commitments, compute contracts, power contracts;
- **Demand Quality / Financing:** customer cash generation, third-party capital, supplier equity / credit enhancement, refinancing dependency.

These are **monitoring families, not a five-of-five checklist**. Financing-led or real-economy clearing can be confirmed before qualifying impairment / write-down evidence appears, provided independent signal families and causal breadth are strong enough and alternative explanations do not dominate.

As of August 2026, public evidence remains more consistent with:

> **continued high-intensity capital expansion, with rising ROI and monetization pressure**

than with a confirmed industry-wide Capital Hard Landing.

The latest financing structures add one more variable: **Financing Reflexivity**.

When a system seller provides equity, credit enhancement, LPS support, or compute-financing coordination, seller-side demand visibility becomes more correlated with the financing conditions of the customer and infrastructure developer.

That does not make the demand "fake."

It means demand quality must distinguish:

1. independently financed end demand;
2. long-duration Anchor Buyer commitments;
3. third-party infrastructure capital;
4. supplier equity / credit support.

---

## 10. Demand duration depends on workflow penetration and economic output

A report that stops at GPUs, HBM, CapEx, or data-center capacity is incomplete. Infrastructure is the supply side. The ten-year industrial cycle ultimately depends on whether these physical assets can move through software, enterprise workflows, and the physical world into verifiable economic output.

### AI Inheritance Effect: IT is the installed base

Long-run AI diffusion does not require rebuilding the digital world from zero. Decades of information technology have already created software, databases, cloud systems, APIs, digital documents, source code, identity/access systems, and traceable workflows. In highly digitized organizations, AI can act as an intelligence / automation layer on top of that installed base.

The demand side therefore contains an additional diffusion path:

> **Digital Installed Base → AI Overlay → Workflow Penetration → Automation Depth → Workload Volume**

This expands the long-run infrastructure thesis beyond "models keep getting larger" toward "more existing digital workflows become AI workloads."

The boundary remains essential. Marginal Digital → AI deployment friction can be much lower than Industrial → Digital transformation, but it is not zero. Data quality, integration, security, governance, responsibility, reliability, human review, and unit economics still determine whether adoption moves from feature use into production automation.

### Agent-native software: the unit shifts from seats to verified outcomes

Traditional SaaS scales largely around accounts and seats. Agent-native software continuously consumes inference, retrieval, tools/APIs, retries, observability, and human review.

The more relevant unit is therefore:

> **cost per verified workflow outcome**

Cheaper models and better routing can reduce resource intensity per attempt. But if lower prices unlock more users, longer agent loops, more tool calls, and previously uneconomic workflows, total compute demand can still expand.

The software layer is therefore both a receiver of infrastructure cost and a source of demand elasticity for infrastructure.

### Physical AI: deployment has to clear a capital-payback test

Physical AI is also a systems problem: perception, control, actuation, batteries, sensors, manufacturing, field reliability, and cloud/edge infrastructure have to close together.

The commercial threshold is not whether a robot can complete one task. It is whether:

> **Useful Tasks × Uptime × Deployment Scale** can cover **Energy + Maintenance + Human Supervision + Depreciation + Financing**

Physical AI can therefore become a new pool of compute, memory, storage, and cloud-workload demand, but only scaled utilization and capital recovery can validate its industrial value.

### Labor: Task → Workflow → Job

The labor transmission chain has to pass through:

> **Model Capability → Task Suitability → Workflow Redesign → Adoption Economics / Responsibility → Job Composition → Employment / Wage Outcome**

Occupational exposure therefore cannot be converted directly into job-loss forecasts. More useful long-run indicators include junior/senior ratios, human-review burden, output per employee, exception rates, and organizational layers.

### Talent: system design and verification matter more than model access

As model calling commoditizes, more durable capability moves toward workflow engineering, system engineering, cross-stack portability, verification, performance/cost measurement, and responsibility boundaries.

The long-run talent divide is not merely who "uses AI." It is:

> **who can organize AI into reliable, portable, measurable production systems and remain accountable for the result**

Putting these layers together, the report's demand-side loop is:

> **Physical Infrastructure → System Delivery → Workflow Economics → Verified Output → Adoption / Cash Flow → Utilization → Capital Reinvestment**

If the front half of that chain expands persistently faster than the back half, S4 risk rises. If verified workflow output, utilization, and cash flow continue pulling new demand forward, S2/S3-style capital expansion can persist even as unit compute costs fall.

---

## 11. Compute is productive infrastructure only when output validates the capital

The report uses the idea of compute becoming a productive input in an economic, testable sense—not as a technological slogan.

The relevant standard is:

> **Can an AI system, at a given compute, energy, and capital cost, repeatedly complete workflows that previously required human execution and produce outcomes that can be verified?**

If the answer is no, compute remains primarily an assistive tool.

If the answer is yes at scale, compute, memory, packaging, power, and software begin to function as productive infrastructure.

A more useful metric is therefore:

> **cost per verified workflow outcome + capital required to sustain it**

This is why model capability, infrastructure spend, utilization, and business revenue must be analyzed inside one unit-economics framework.

---

## 12. Four structural states

The report uses four parallel scenarios.

### S1 — Competitive Expansion

Supply elasticity rises, custom systems mature, interoperability improves, switching costs fall, and value capture becomes more distributed.

### S2 — Baseline Concentration

A small number of system platforms and scarce-layer suppliers retain strong economics while substitution paths continue expanding. **As of August 2026, this is the state that best matches the current evidence mix.**

### S3 — Resource Hegemony

Multiple scarce layers, quantity assurance, and switching costs strengthen together, forcing challengers to replicate a more complete infrastructure stack.

### S4 — Capital Hard Landing

Multiple causally independent signal families deteriorate broadly and persistently across actors/layers, with at least one real-economy / market-clearing / commitment signal present. Accounting losses strengthen severity when present but are not required.

These states are not sequential stages and do not carry fixed probabilities.

The variables that will determine the 2030/2036 structure include:

- supply elasticity;
- HBM / packaging / power tightness;
- system substitution;
- software migration cost;
- Anchor Buyer demand;
- utilization / monetization;
- capital discipline;
- regulation;
- China's production-grade parallel stack and autonomous frontier closure;
- demand-financing quality and supplier-assisted financing.

---

## 13. What would change the view

High-value falsification signals include:

- sustained declines or large cancellations in leading system-platform supply commitments;
- advanced packaging no longer being described as a growth constraint by major suppliers;
- HBM / DRAM residual supply becoming durably abundant;
- cloud custom systems creating large-scale, low-migration-cost substitution in frontier workloads;
- open interfaces materially lowering proprietary software / fabric switching costs;
- energized power expanding enough that Time-to-Power stops differentiating projects;
- Anchor Compute Buyer commitments being delayed or underutilized at scale;
- hyperscaler AI monetization persistently failing to cover the expanding capital base;
- China's frontier parallel stack gaining or losing scaled first-party evidence across multiple Production-Grade Gates, or autonomous frontier closure changing materially;
- supplier equity / credit support rising while independent customer financing and utilization weaken;
- formal regulatory remedies materially changing tying, interface, or market-entry conditions.

If these signals contradict the current mechanisms, the report must change.

They cannot be explained away by saying the long-run thesis remains intact.

---

## 14. Research discipline: Fact → Hypothesis → Mechanism → Scenario → Falsification

The report uses five layers:

- **F — Fact:** observed and publicly verifiable;
- **H — Hypothesis:** a future input condition or stress-test assumption;
- **M — Mechanism:** the causal transmission between facts and hypotheses;
- **S — Scenario:** a future structural state created by several conditions;
- **X — Falsification:** reverse signals strong enough to weaken, rewrite, or invalidate the original judgment.

**Mechanism-break override:** breadth requirements govern **system-level scenario adjudication**, not whether an underlying mechanism is allowed to survive direct falsification. If a high-quality observation, on the same denominator and time identity, directly breaks a necessary premise of a mechanism, that mechanism must be downgraded, rewritten, or retired immediately even when cross-signal breadth is not yet present. Breadth determines whether the whole S1–S4 state changes; it cannot be used to ignore a broken premise.

The purpose is to prevent:

- an institutional forecast from becoming a company fact through repetition;
- company guidance from becoming an actual;
- revenue share from becoming wafer share;
- contracted capacity from becoming energized load;
- a D-grade stress test from becoming a deterministic 2030/2036 outcome.

Future judgments can be bold.

The fact layer must remain conservative.

---

## 15. Bottom line

AI is driving an industrial restructuring deeper than a conventional GPU demand cycle.

It is changing:

- the destination of incremental advanced-semiconductor capital;
- memory and advanced-packaging product mix;
- the boundary of system-platform competition;
- the industrial role of hyperscalers and model companies;
- the strategic value of power and data-center infrastructure;
- the residual capacity available to traditional device markets;
- the speed and depth at which the global digital installed base is converted into AI-enabled workflows;
- the shift in Agent-Native Software from seat economics toward verified-workflow economics;
- the transition in Physical AI from technical demo toward utilization, reliability, and capital payback;
- labor-market restructuring from task automation through workflow redesign;
- the migration of talent value from "using models" toward workflow/system engineering and verification;
- the strategic objective of China's parallel stack;
- the dependence of global capital markets on AI workload monetization.

The resulting competition is not simply a chip war.

It is:

> **System Stack + Scarce Capacity + Capital Formation + Workload Monetization**

The state closest to current evidence remains **S2 — Baseline Concentration**: leading platforms and scarce layers retain strong economics, while alternative systems continue to grow without yet eliminating system-level concentration or physical constraints.

If supply elasticity, system portability, and multi-sourcing improve materially, the industry moves toward S1.

If multiple scarce layers and switching costs strengthen simultaneously, S3 gains weight.

If multiple causally independent signal families across utilization/revenue, market clearing, forward commitments, financing quality, and accounting cross-confirm broad and persistent deterioration across actors/layers, S4 becomes the dominant interpretation; Accounting is confirmatory rather than mandatory.

The report's final question is therefore not whether “only AI still matters.” Smartphones, PCs, game consoles, vehicles, industrial equipment, robotics, aerospace, and other long-lifecycle high-reliability systems remain important. The relevant question is:

> **Can those still-important end systems continue to obtain enough, stable, affordable electronics on their own BOM, qualification, lifecycle, and cost constraints from an upstream system whose priorities are being rewritten by AI/HPC?**

The dimensional boundary remains essential. Smartphones, PCs, and selected edge/ADAS systems can directly share advanced logic, memory, or selected packaging with AI. Vehicles, industrial control, and factory systems are more often exposed through adjacent product mix and long qualification cycles. Aerospace, defense, and space electronics often face a different problem: the economics and continuity of trusted, long-life, low-volume, special-process supply, equipment, and engineering talent — not direct competition for HBM.

That is the industrial meaning of the **system-level resource concentration risk**: **other end systems do not become unimportant; rather, importance itself no longer guarantees upstream priority.**

> **Forecasts may be wrong; evidence boundaries cannot be. Scenarios may be bold; the fact layer must remain disciplined. The most valuable long-run industry research is not a one-time guess at the end-state, but knowing which variables are deciding the end-state—and what facts would force the judgment to change.**
